Computational barriers for permutation-based problems, and cumulants of weakly dependent random variables
Bertrand Even and Christophe Giraud and Nicolas Verzelen

Abstract
In many high-dimensional problems, polynomial-time algorithms fall short of achieving the statistical limits attainable without computational constraints. A powerful approach to probe the limits of polynomial-time algorithms is to study the performance of low-degree polynomials. The seminal work of [ SW22 ] connects low-degree lower bounds to multivariate cumulants. Prior works [ LG24 , EGV25 ] leverage independence among latent variables to bound cumulants. However, such approaches break down for problems with latent structure lacking independence, such as those involving random permutations. To address this important restriction, we develop a technique to upper-bound cumulants under weak dependencies – such as those arising from sampling without replacement or random permutations. To show-case the effectiveness of our approach, we uncover evidence of statistical–computational gaps in multiple feature matching and in seriation problems.



1
Introduction

In high-dimensional statistics, the main objective is to design computationally efficient estimation procedures with optimal statistical performance. However, in problems like sparse PCA, planted clique, and clustering, the best known polynomial-time algorithms often fall short of the performance achievable by estimators computationally unconstrained. This has led to conjectures about statistical–computational gap discrepancies between statistically optimal solutions, and those achievable in polynomial time.
To fairly evaluate a computationally efficient algorithm, its performance should be compared not to the unconstrained optimum, but to the best performance achievable in polynomial time. This shift motivates the study of lower bounds for polynomial-time algorithms across various problems. Since high-dimensional problems are inherently probabilistic, classical worst-case complexity, such as P vs NP, is less relevant. Instead, researchers focus on specific computational models such as Sum-of-Squares (SoS)
[
HKKAP+17
,
BHK+19
]
, statistical queries
[
Kea98
,
BBH+21
]
, approximate message passing
[
MM09
,
DMM09
,
MW24
]
, and low-degree polynomials
[
Hop18
,
KWB19
,
SW22
,
Wei25
]
.

Among these, low-degree polynomial (LD) bounds have gained attention for establishing state-of-the-art lower bounds in tasks like community detection
[
HS17
]
, spiked tensor models
[
HS17
,
KWB19
]
, and sparse PCA
[
DKWB24
]
. In this model, only estimators that are multivariate polynomials of degree at most
D
are considered. The premise is that for many problems, degree
D=O(\log n)
polynomials are as powerful as any polynomial-time algorithm
[
KWB19
]
. Thus, failure of such polynomials suggests no efficient algorithm exists for the task. We refer to
[
Wei25
]
for a comprehensive discussion of this conjecture.
The LD framework connects to other models like statistical queries
[
BBH+21
]
, approximate message passing
[
MW24
]
, and free-energy landscapes
[
BEAH+22
]
.
It was originally developed for detection problems, but this perspective fails to characterize the complexity of estimation problems when detection-recovery gaps exist – see e.g.
[
MWZ23
]
.
To overcome this limitation, the framework has been extended to estimation tasks, notably in the work of Schramm and Wein
[
SW22
]
. Their framework has since been applied to submatrix estimation
[
SW22
]
, stochastic block models
[
LG24
,
SW25
]
, Gaussian mixture models
[
EGV24
,
EGV25
]
, dense cycles recovery
[
MWZ23
]
, and graph coloring
[
KVWX23
]
.

Let
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
be the observed matrix, assumed to satisfy the model
Y=X+E
, where
E
is independent of
X
and has entries drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
. We consider a recovery problem consisting in optimally predicting a real-valued random variable
x
, which is
\sigma(X)
-measurable, using degree-
D
polynomials in
Y
. This is formalized via the minimum mean squared error

\mathrm{MMSE}_{\leq D}:=\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}[Y]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-x\right)^{2}\right],

(1)

where
\mathbb{R}_{D}[Y]
denotes the space of real-valued multivariate polynomials in
Y
of degree at most
D
.

A key result of Schramm and Wein
[
SW22
]
is to lower-bound the
\mathrm{MMSE}_{\leq D}
in terms of multivariate cumulants

\mathrm{MMSE}_{\leq D}\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[x^{2}]-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p},\ |\alpha|\leq D}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\kern 5.0pt,

(2)

where
\alpha!=\prod_{(i,j)\in[n]\times[p]}\alpha_{ij}!
, and for each multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
, the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
is defined as

\kappa_{x,\alpha}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,X_{\alpha}\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left\{X_{ij}\right\}_{(i,j)\in\alpha}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(3)

with
X_{\alpha}=\left\{X_{ij}\right\}_{(ij)\in\alpha}
denoting the multiset containing
\alpha_{ij}
copies of each
X_{ij}
for
(i,j)\in[n]\times[p]
. Definitions and properties of cumulants are provided in Appendix
B
.

In certain latent variable models, the signal matrix
X
depends on some latent variables
\pi^{*}\in\Pi\subset[K]^{n}
, and
x=x(\pi^{*})
is
\sigma(\pi^{*})
-measurable. Theorem 2.5 in
[
EGV25
]
shows that bounding
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
can be reduced to bounding cumulants of the form

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(4)

for some function
\delta:[K]^{2}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
.
When the latent variables
\pi^{*}(1),\ldots,\pi^{*}(n)
are independent, such cumulants can be easily upper-bounded
using this independence
[
EGV25
]
.
However, the proof techniques of
[
EGV25
]
and
[
SW22
]
break down under weak dependence, such as when
\pi^{*}
is a uniform random permutation of
[n]
, or is generated by sampling without replacement mechanisms.
Nonetheless, this limitation can potentially be overcome, since multivariate cumulants play a central role in probability theory for deriving central limit theorems (CLTs) in weakly dependent settings – such as Markov chains, random permutations, and models in statistical physics.
A key framework for proving such CLTs is the weighted dependency graph theory developed by Féray
[
Fer18
]
.

Our main contributions

1- We develop a quantitative and non-asymptotic extension of Féray’s theory to bound cumulants of weakly dependent variables. We establish a new upper-bound on the cumulants of certain classes of weakly dependent random variables.
Then, we explain how to derive from this result some bounds on the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
in scenarios where the latent variable
\pi^{*}
exhibits weak dependences.

2- We instantiate our methodology to derive LD bounds for three problems: multiple feature matching, seriation and clustering with prescribed group sizes. Our LD bounds are tight up to poly-logarithmic factors, and match the performance of state-of-the-art polynomial-time algorithms for these problems, except in a few narrow parameter regimes.

3- We derive the corresponding information-theoretic limits, revealing a statistical-computational gap for these three problems.

Before delving into the technical details, we summarize our key results for these problems.

Multiple feature matching.

Feature matching problems arise in many fields, ranging from image analysis
[
Low04
]
, to computational biology
[
SXB08
]
.
We observe
M
datasets, each of them gathering (noisy) observations of
K
feature vectors randomly permuted.
In the specific case where
M=2
, a probabilistic formulation of this model was introduced by Collier and Dalalyan
[
CD16
]
. We extend this model as follows. The dataset indexed by
m\in[M]
stores
K
feature vectors
Y^{(m)}_{1},\ldots,Y^{(m)}_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
For some unknown random permutations

\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{m}
on
[K]
, and unknown means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the
p
-dimensional feature vectors
Y^{(m)}_{k}
are assumed to be generated as follow

Y_{1}^{(m)}=\mu_{\pi^{*}_{m}(1)}+\varepsilon_{1}^{(m)},\ \ldots\ ,Y_{K}^{(m)}=\mu_{\pi^{*}_{m}(K)}+\varepsilon_{K}^{(m)}\,,\quad\text{for $m\in[M]$,}

(5)

with
\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}I_{p}\right)
.
The goal is to recover the permutations
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{m}
, i.e. to match the features across the datasets. This problem is closely related to the problems of database alignement
[
DCK19
,
DCK23
,
Tam22
]
, geometric planted matching
[
KNW22
]
, particle tracking
[
CKK+10
]
and fair clustering
[
KSAM19
,
GSV21
]
.
In particular, our LD bounds have implications in these frameworks.
All those works are restricted to
M=2
, to the notable exceptions of
[
CKK+10
]
, which considers the specific case where the
M
datasets are snapshots of a time series.
We provide a complete characterization of the problem in terms of the separation

\Delta^{2}=\min_{k\neq l}\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}
,
identifying both the information-theoretic threshold and a computational barrier within the LD framework. We also identify when it is beneficial to match the
M
datasets simultaneously, rather than matching them pairwisely.
Exact recovery is information-theoretic possible, with high probability, as soon as
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(KM)+\sqrt{\frac{p\log(KM)}{M}}
, which shows benefit of the multiplicity of datasets as soon as
p\gtrsim\log(KM)
. We prove a LD lower bound suggesting that the separation needed for recovering
\pi^{*}
in polynomial time is
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}1+\min\left(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{K}}\right)
, and so, there is a benefit of having access to multiple datasets as soon as
M\geq_{\log}K
. This computational barrier is matched, up to logarithmic factors, in a wide range of regimes by applying a clustering procedure on the set
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
. All those results provide evidences of a statistical-computational gap for large
K,M
and for
p\geq_{\log}M/K
.

Seriation.
The seriation problem consists in ordering
n
items from noisy pairwise observations. Originally introduced for chronological dating of graves in archeology
[
Rob51
,
Pet99
]
, it has since found a wide range of applications, including genome alignement
[
GJM11
,
RBd17
]
, time synchronization in distributed networks
[
EKPS04
,
GK06
]
, and envelope reduction for sparse matrices
[
BPS95
]
. This problem has attracted a lot of attention both in the noiseless case
[
ABH98
,
FJBd13
]

and in the noisy case. The optimal statistical rates have been derived for various loss functions in
[
FMR19
,
CM23
,
BCV24
]
. However, the best known polynomial-time algorithmes either achieve much slower rates
[
CM23
,
BCV24
]
, or require strong additional assumptions on the model
[
JS22
,
IGV24
]
. For this reason,
statistical-computational gaps have been conjectured e.g. in
[
CM23
,
BCV24
]
. In this work, we provide strong evidence of this gap
by establishing a LD lower bound for the most emblematic seriation problem.

For some random permutation
\pi^{*}=(\pi^{*}(1),\ldots,\pi^{*}(n))
, the signal matrix
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is defined by
X_{ij}=\lambda\mathbf{1}\{|\pi^{*}(i)-\pi^{*}(j)|\leq\rho\}
where the positive integer
\rho
is a scale parameter, and
\lambda>0
is the signal strength.
Given
Y=X+E
, where
E
is made of iid standard Gaussian variables, we focus on the problem of reconstructing the matrix
X
. We establish in Section
4
the low-degree hardness of weak reconstruction in the regime where
\lambda\preccurlyeq_{\log}1\wedge\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\rho}
, thereby matching the performances of polynomial-time procedures available in the literature. As a comparison, weak reconstruction of
X
is statistically possible in the much wider regime
\lambda\succcurlyeq\log(n)/\sqrt{\rho}
. As a straightforward consequence, we also confirm the existence of statistical-computational gap for related seriation problems–see Appendix
G
.
Although seriation shares similarities with one-dimensional random geometric graphs
[
Pen03
]
, and in particular to planted dense cycles problems
[
MWZ23
,
MWZ24
]
, an important difference is that, in our case,
\pi^{*}
is a permutation of
[n]
, whereas in related works
[
MWZ23
,
BCV24
]
,
\pi^{*}
is a random element of
[n]^{n}
, so that the
\pi^{*}(i)
are independent. The latter property considerably simplifies the arguments as explained in Section
3
.

Clustering with prescribed group sizes.
Gaussian mixture clustering is a central problem in unsupervised learning
[
Das99
,
VW04
,
LZ16
,
RV17
,
GV19
,
LL22
,
DKPZ23
]
. For some unknown vectors
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, noise level
\sigma>0
, and partition
G^{*}=\left\{G^{*}_{1},\ldots,G^{*}_{K}\right\}
of
[n]
, the observations
Y_{ij}
are sampled independently with distribution

Y_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{kj},\sigma^{2}),\quad\text{for}\ i\in G^{*}_{k}\quad\text{and}\ j\in[p]
.
When the clusters are made of independent assignments, i.e.
G_{k}^{*}=\left\{i:k^{*}_{i}=k\right\}
with
k^{*}_{1},\ldots,k^{*}_{n}
i.i.d. uniform on
[K]
,
Even
et al.

[
EGV25
]
provide the computational threshold for poly-time clustering
in terms of the separation

\Delta^{2}=\min_{k\neq l}\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}
,
except in a narrow parameter regime.
As a simple example for explaining our theory, we focus here on the case of perfectly balanced clusters, where
G^{*}
is sampled uniformly among all partitions fulfilling
|G^{*}_{k}|=n/K
for all
k\in[K]
. While being close to the independent assignment case, the perfectly balanced case cannot be handled with the technique of
[
EGV25
]
, due to weak dependencies in the assignment process. Our results prove that the perfect knowledge of the cluster sizes does not help (significantly) for cluster recovery, in the sense that we recover the same computational threshold as in
[
EGV25
]
.

Limitations and perspectives.

As in the original work of
[
SW22
]
, our techniques only allow to characterize hardness regimes up to poly-logarithmic factors. This limitation has been recently tackled by Sohn and Wein
[
SW25
]
, who provide an approach to derive sharp thresholds. However, the latter requires to solve large overcomplete linear systems, which limits its applicability in complex models. Let us also mention that, for some permutation-based models with no detection-estimation gap – such as matching correlated
Erdős-Rényi graphs – one can establish LD bounds by reduction to a detection problem
[
DDL23
]
.

Still, we believe that our techniques can be instantiated to provide LD lower bounds in other permutation-based models, for which both statistical-computational, and detection-estimation gaps are conjectured, such as rectangular seriation problems
[
FMR19
]
, tensor estimation with unknown permutations
[
LW24
]
, or constrained clustering
[
KSAM19
,
GSV21
,
HLLW
]
.

Notation and organisation of the manuscript.

Given a vector
v
, we write
\|v\|
for its Euclidean norm. For a matrix
A
, we denote
\|A\|_{F}
for its Frobenius norm, and
\|A\|_{op}
for its operator norm. For an integer
n
, we write
\mathcal{S}_{n}
as the set of permutation on
[n]
.
For two functions
u
and
v
, we write
u\lesssim v
if there a exists a numerical constant such that
u\leq cv
. For two functions that may depend on some parameters
n,p,K
or
M
, we write
u\leq_{\log}v
, if there exist numerical constants
c
and
c^{\prime}
such that
u\leq c\log^{c^{\prime}}(npKM)v
. For a finite subset
S
, we write
|S|
for its cardinality.

We identify a matrix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
(resp. a tensor
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}
) with the multiset of
[n]\times[p]
(resp.
[K]\times[M]\times[p]
) containing
\alpha_{ij}
(resp.
\alpha_{kmj}
) copies of
(i,j)
(resp.
kmj
). For
i\in[n]
, we write
\alpha_{i:}
the
i
-th row of
\alpha
. Similarly, for
j\in[p]
, we write
\alpha_{:j}
the
j
-th column of
\alpha
. We shall write
|\alpha|
the
l_{1}
-norm of
\alpha
, which is the cardinality of
\alpha
viewed as a multiset. Finally,
\alpha!
stands for
\prod_{ij}\alpha_{ij}!
(resp.
\prod_{kmj}\alpha_{kmj}
).
For
W_{1},\ldots,W_{l}
random variables on the same space, we write
\mathop{cumul}\left(W_{1},\ldots,W_{l}\right)
for their joint cumulant.

First, we show-case in Section
2
the benefit of our general approach, by characterizing the hardness regimes in multiple feature matching. In Section
3
, we explain our general strategy for bounding cumulants of the form (
4
), and underline the key steps of the approach. We then describe another application of this method in Section
4
by proving a nearly tight LD lower bounds for seriation problems. In Section
5
, we state a tight LD bound for Gaussian mixture models with group sizes known to be eaxactly
n/K
. Although less significant than the other applications, this result is interesting for pedagogical purpose as this is one of the simplest models that requires our new proof techniques, and its proof is therefore more transparent. All the proofs and related results are postponed to the appendices.

2
Multiple Feature Matching

A first problem that we investigate is the problem of Multiple Feature Matching, where one seeks to estimate multiple permutations from the observation of multiple datasets. Using our general techniques for dealing with weak dependencies – see Section
3
– we characterize a computational barrier for this problem in the LD framework. This barrier is matched in almost all regimes with a clustering procedure. In Appendix
A
, we also characterize the information-theoretic thresholds for this problem, giving evidence of a statistical-computational gap for Multiple Feature Matching.

Setup:

We observe
M\geq 2
datasets, and for
m\in[M]
, the dataset indexed by
m
is made of
Y^{(m)}_{1},\ldots,Y^{(m)}_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
generated according to (
5
).
Our aim is to determine the minimum separation
\Delta^{2}=\min_{k\neq l\in[K]}\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}
required for recovering
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
, up to some global permutation of
[K]
. To quantify the error, we consider the proportion of mismatched points

err(\pi,\pi^{*})=\min_{\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{K}}\frac{1}{KM}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{1}_{\psi(\pi_{m}(k))\neq\pi^{*}_{m}(k)}\kern 5.0pt,

(6)

where

\mathcal{S}_{K}
stands for the set of all permutations on
[K]
. The infimum over
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{K}
accounts for the fact that
\pi^{*}
is identifiable only up to a global permutation
\psi
.
This model is a generalization of the feature matching model considered in
[
CD16
]
to the case where the number
M
of datasets can be larger than
2
. We wonder to what extent the presence of multiple datasets can help to recover the permutations : Is it easier to match
M>2
datasets than to match two datasets? In principle, the presence of several datasets could be helpful to estimate the unknown
\mu_{k}
’s, and thereby to improve the accuracy of the matchings.
We emphasize that our model differs from that of
[
KNW22
]
, where the second dataset is a noisy and permuted version of the first dataset. In the latter setup, having access to multiple datasets would indeed not be helpful for matching two specific datasets.

In this section, our main contribution is a LD lower-bound for multiple feature matching. Alongside, we also exhibit polynomial-time procedures that nearly achieve this bound in most regimes. Finally, the information-theoretical threshold of the problem is also characterized in Appendix
A
, so that we provide a full characterization of the problem. To give a glimpse of our results, let us recall that, for feature matching with
M=2
, Collier and Dalalyan
[
CD16
]
have established exact recovery of the matching, i.e.
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})=0
, as soon as
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(K)+\sqrt{p\log(K)}
.
This recovery can be efficiently achieved by an
Hungarian algorithm. For multiple feature matching, we prove that exact recovery is statistically possible as soon as
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(KM)+\sqrt{p\log(KM)/M}
– see Proposition
A.1
and Theorem
A.2
. This result shows the benefit of permuting simultaneously multiple samples, when
p
is large compared to
\log(KM)
. If we restrict our attention to polynomial-time algorithms, we prove in Theorem
2.2
that
\Delta^{2}\succcurlyeq_{\log}1+\max(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}})
is required for low-degree polynomials.
Corollary
2.3
shows that successful recovery is possible in polynomial-time above this separation, in most regime.
Hence, for computationally efficient procedures, the benefit of multiple permuted samples only arises for
M\geq_{\log}K
, but this benefit is quite strong for large
M
.

2.1
LD lower-bound for Multiple feature matching

Applying the general techniques of Section
3
to the particular case of Multiple Feature Matching, we are able to characterize the computational barrier in the LD framework. Low-degree polynomials are not well suited for directly outputting permutations
\hat{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{M}
. Instead, we focus on the problem of estimating the matrix
\Gamma^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K\times M)\times(K\times M)}
defined by
\Gamma^{*}_{(k,m),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})}=\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}_{m}(k)=\pi^{*}_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\right\}
.
Next proposition shows that proving computational hardness for estimating
\Gamma^{*}
implies computational hardness for estimating
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
. We refer to Appendix
H.3
for a proof.

Proposition 2.1
.

Suppose we are in a regime where
K,M
(and possibly
p
) go to infinity. For any prior on the choice of
\pi^{*}
and
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}
, if

MMSE_{poly}:=\inf_{\hat{\Gamma}\ poly-time}{1\over M(M-1)K^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=K^{-1}(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt,

then, all polynomial-time estimators
\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
satisfy
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})\right]=1+o(1)
.

In other words, it is impossible to reconstruct in polynomial time the permutations
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
better than random guessing in the regime where
MMSE_{poly}=K^{-1}(1+o(1))
. Thus, we focus in the following on the problem of estimating
\Gamma^{*}
. For proving a LD lower-bound, we
consider a Bayesian setting with the following prior distribution.

Definition 1
.

We draw
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{m}
uniformly and independently over
\mathcal{S}_{K}
. For a given
\bar{\Delta}>0
, independently from
(\pi_{m})_{m\in[M]}
, we draw
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
iid with law
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda^{2}I_{p}\right)
, with
\lambda^{2}=\frac{1}{p}\bar{\Delta}^{2}\sigma^{2}
. Conditionally on
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
and
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}
, we draw independently, for
k\in[K]
and
m\in[M]
,
Y_{k}^{(m)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{\pi^{*}_{m}(k)},\sigma^{2}I_{p}\right)
. The observed tensor is
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}
defined by
Y_{k,m,d}=\left(Y_{k}^{(m)}\right)_{d}
.

We emphasize that, with high probability under this prior, the separation satisfies
\Delta^{2}=\bar{\Delta}^{2}(1+o_{p}(1))
, when
p\gtrsim\log(K)
.
As a proxy for
MMSE_{poly}
, we lower bound in Theorem
2.2
the low-degree mean square error
MMSE_{\leq D}:=\inf_{\hat{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}(Y)}{1\over M(M-1)K^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]
.
The proof of Theorem
2.2
, postponed to Appendix
D
, builds on the general technique of Section
3
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that
K\geq 2\left(D+2\right)^{2}
, and suppose
\zeta:=64D^{40}\lambda^{4}p\max\left(1,\frac{M}{K}\right)<1
. Then,

MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{K^{2}}\left(1+64(D+1)D^{36}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Taking
D=\left(\log(n)\right)^{1+\eta}
, whenever
K\geq 2\left(\log(n)^{1+\eta}+2\right)^{2}
, we deduce from Theorem
2.2
,
\left(\log(n)\right)^{1+\eta}
-degree hardness when
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq_{\log}\min(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}})
.
Combining this result with the information-theoretic threshold (Theorem
A.2
) for partial recovery, we get hardness of Multiple Feature Matching when

\Delta^{2}\leq_{\log}1+\min\left(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(7)

2.2
Matching the LD lower-bound with clustering procedures

Let
n=KM
, and let us identify
(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]
to the integer
q=K(m-1)+k
. Define
G^{*}
the partition of
[n]
induced by
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
with
G_{k}^{*}=\{\left(\pi^{*}_{m}\right)^{-1}(k),\kern 5.0ptm\in[M]\}
. There is a one-to-one correspondance between

G^{*}
and the partitions
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
, up to a global permutation of
[K]
.
This correspondance suggests the following procedure to estimate
\pi^{*}
: first apply a clustering procedure
\hat{G}
on the
n
data points
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
, and then construct
\hat{\pi}
from
\hat{G}
. If the clustering procedure
\hat{G}
outputs a partition that does not represent a
M
-tuple of permutations, we can round it greedily to output a
M
-tuple of permutations.

When the partition
\hat{G}
recovers exactly
G^{*}
, the estimator
\hat{\pi}
recovers exactly the
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi^{*}=\pi^{*}_{M},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
, up to a global permutation.
Combining Proposition 3.2 of
[
EGV25
]
, Theorem 1 of
[
GV19
]
, and Proposition 4 of
[
EGV24
]
, which provide guarantees for exact recovery in clustering, we obtain the following result, proved in Appendix
H.4
.

Corollary 2.3
.

There exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that the following holds whenever
M\notin[K,K^{c}]
or
p\notin[\log(KM),\frac{p}{KM}]
. Suppose that
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}1+\min\left(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}\right)
, then, there exists an estimator
\hat{\pi}=\hat{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{M}
such that, with high probability,
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})=0
.

Corollary
2.3
shows that feature matching is possible in polynomial time above the low-degree threshold (
7
) in almost all parameter regimes. In the remaining regime where both
M\in[K,K^{c}]
and
p\in[\log(KM),\frac{p}{KM}]
, we have the next result, that we suspect to be suboptimal.

Remark 1
.

When both
M\in[K,K^{c}]
and
p\in[\log(KM),\frac{p}{KM}]
,
we can derive from Proposition G.7 of
[
EGV25
]
perfect recovery of
\pi^{*}
, with high probability, whenever
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}\sqrt{K}+\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}
.

3
Bounding cumulants of weakly dependent random variables

In the class of models we are considering, to establish LD lower bounds –
either directly, or after applying Theorem 2.5 of
[
EGV25
]
–
the problem reduces to upper-bounding cumulants of the form
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)
, where
\delta:[K]^{2}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
, and
\pi^{*}(1),\pi^{*}(2),\ldots
are weakly dependent random variables. The techniques of
[
SW22
,
LG24
,
EGV25
]
break down in such a setting.
We describe in this section a methodology for upper-bounding
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)
in such cases.

Ex: Multiple feature matching.
The labels
\pi^{*}(k,m)
, for
(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]
are defined by
\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi^{*}_{m}(k)
with
\pi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}_{M}
being permutations drawn independently and uniformly over
\mathcal{S}_{K}
. After applying Theorem 2.5 of
[
EGV25
]
, we are reduced to upper-bounding cumulants of the form
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)
, see Proposition
B.3
, p.
B.3
. We use the techniques of this section for dealing with the fact that, for
k,k^{\prime},m
,
\pi^{*}(k,m)
and
\pi^{*}(k^{\prime},m)
are not independent.

3.1
Core bound on cumulants

The core result on which relies our analysis is an upper-bound on the cumulants of a class of weakly dependent variables.
Let us consider three families of non-random disjoint (within a family) subsets of
[n]
, denoted
I_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell}
,
A_{1},\ldots,A_{q}
, and
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
. For
\Delta\subset[\ell]
, we define
I_{\Delta}=\cup_{t\in\Delta}I_{t}
. The next lemma provides an upper-bound on the joint cumulant of random variables
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
with mixed moments given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{t}\right]=

\displaystyle\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x_{0}}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x_{0}}

(8)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y_{0})\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y_{0}\right)\ ,\kern 5.0pt\text{ for any $\Delta\subset[\ell]$.}

Such random variables appear when sampling without replacement and/or random permutations occurs, see Equations (
31
), (
39
) and (
43
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
have mixed moments given by (
8
) and let
L=|I_{[\ell]}|
.

1.

When
r\geq 1
,
2L^{2}y_{0}\leq 1
, and
2x_{0}\leq y_{0}
, we have

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}\right)\right|\leq 4\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{y_{0}}\right)^{cc(\mathcal{B})-1}\kern 5.0pt,

(9)

where
cc(\mathcal{B})
is the number of connected components of the graph
\mathcal{B}
with node set
[\ell]
and with edges between
t
and
t^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
i\in[r]
such that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersect
B_{i}
.

2.

When
r=0
and
2L^{2}x_{0}\leq 1
, we have

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}\right)\right|\leq 2\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}x_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}.

(10)

Lemma
3.1
is proved in Appendix
F
.
The proof builts on ideas from Féray
[
Fer18
]
, which are revisited in order to provide quantitative and non-asymptotic controls of the cumulants.

Random variables
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
having mixed moments (
8
) are weakly correlated when
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
are small. When
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
go to zero, they become uncorrelated, and their joint cumulant
\mathop{cumul}\left(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}\right)
is zero, see Lemma
B.2
. Lemma
3.1
shows that this cumulant remains small when
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
are positive but small enough.
While the random variables
\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}
in (
4
)
do not fulfill the mixed moment condition (
8
) in the models that we are considering, for some fixed
\pi
and some
I_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell}\subset[n]
, the random variables
Z_{t}=\prod_{i\in I_{t}}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}
do fulfill (
8
), as highlighted by Equations (
31
), (
39
) and (
43
). For example, if
\pi^{*}
is drawn uniformly over all permutations on
[n]
, then
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{t}\right]=\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{\Delta}|}\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{-1}\times\ldots\times\left(1-{|I_{\Delta}|-1\over n}\right)^{-1}
, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
.
We explain below how we can reduce the problem of controlling the cumulant (
4
) to the problem of controlling cumulant of random variables like
Z_{t}
. This reduction is made in 4 steps.

3.2
From cumulant (
4
) to cumulants of variables fulfilling (
8
)

Step 1: Reduction.

We observe that the random variables
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots
in (
8
) are weakly correlated, while the variables

\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}
can have strong dependencies (for example if
i_{s}=i_{s^{\prime}}
for some
s\neq s^{\prime}
). The first step of our analysis is to reduce the control of cumulants like (
4
), to the control of cumulants of weakly dependent variables, by grouping together variables presenting (possibly) strong dependencies. This reduction is based on Proposition 5.2 of
[
Fer18
]
, recalled in Proposition
B.4
page
B.4
.

To perform the reduction, we introduce
a weighted graph
\mathcal{W}
with vertex set
[0,l]
, and with edges
w_{s,s^{\prime}}
defined by

-
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
, if
\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}\cap\left\{i_{s^{\prime}},i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\right\}\neq 0
,

-
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=w\in(0,1)
, otherwise, with
w
to be chosen later on.

In this graph, we have assigned edge weight
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
, when there exists some strong dependences within the variables
\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i_{s^{\prime}}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\right)
.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
. Write
\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]
for the weighted graph reduced to
S^{(0)}
, and write
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
for the graph obtained by only keeping the edges of
\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]
of weight
1
. We also denote by
\boldsymbol{S}
the set of connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
.
To upper-bound the cumulant (
4
), Proposition
B.4
ensures
that it is enough to upper bound

C_{\mathbf{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\delta\left(\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right)\right)_{S\in\mathbf{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(11)

in terms of some super-multiplicative function
\psi(S^{(0)})
times the weight

\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right):=\max_{\mathcal{T}\in\text{span-tree}(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}])}\quad\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}}w_{e}\ ,

where the maximum runs over all spanning trees
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]
, and where the product is over all edges
e
of
\mathcal{T}
.
The benefit of this first step is to reduce the control of the cumulant (
4
) to the control of the cumulant (
11
), where strongly dependent variables (linked by weight one) are grouped together.
The next lemma expressed the weight
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}])
in terms of

w
and of the number of connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For all
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
, we have
\displaystyle{\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)=w^{cc(\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}])-1}=w^{|\mathbf{S}|-1}\kern 5.0pt.}

The three next steps provide a path to upper bound
|C_{\mathbf{S}}|
in terms of a super-multiplicative function
\psi(S^{(0)})
times
w^{|\mathbf{S}|-1}
.

Step 2: Expansion.

While the variables
\left(\prod_{s\in S}\delta\left(\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right)\right)_{S\in\mathbf{S}}
appearing in (
11
) are weakly dependent, their mixed moments are not of the form (
8
). Yet, for a fixed
\pi\in\Pi
, writing
supp(S)=\cup_{s\in S}\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
, the random variables
\left(\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}\right)_{S\in\mathbf{S}}
typically have a distribution of the form (
8
), in the models we are considering. In this second step, we leverage the multilinearity of cumulants in order to expand
C_{\mathbf{S}}
as a sum of cumulants of such random variables.

For
S\in\mathbf{S}
, we have

\prod_{s\in S}\delta\left(\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right)=\sum_{\pi\in[K]^{supp(S)}}\ \prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}\prod_{s\in S}\delta\left(\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(12)

For any
\pi=\left(\pi(i)\right)_{i\in supp(S^{(0)})}\in[K]^{supp(S^{(0)})}
, we say that
\pi
is active if and only if, for all
s\in S^{(0)}
,
\delta(\pi(i_{s}),\pi(i^{\prime}_{s}))=1
and if, for all
S\in\mathbf{S}
, the event
\forall i\in supp(S),\kern 5.0pt\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)
is of positive probability.
By definition of
\mathbf{S}
, the subsets
supp(S)
, for
S\in\mathbf{S}
, form a partition of
supp(S^{(0)})
so
[K]^{supp(S^{(0)})}=\prod_{S\in\mathbf{S}}[K]^{supp(S)}
. Hence from (
12
) and the multilinearity of cumulants, the cumulant (
11
) can be expanded as

C_{\mathbf{S}}=\sum_{\pi\kern 4.09723ptactive}\underbrace{\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S}\right)_{S\in\mathbf{S}}\right)}_{=:C_{\mathbf{S}}(\pi)},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{\pi,S}=\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

(13)

The cumulants
C_{\mathbf{S}}(\pi)
involve some variables
Z_{\pi,S}
having mixed moments like (
8
) in the models we consider, unless some
Z_{\pi,S}
are incompatible, in the sense that
\pi
is not realizable and they cannot be jointly non-zero a.s.
In the latter case an additional pruning step is required before applying Lemma
3.1
. In the following, we write
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
.

Step 3: Pruning incompatible configurations.

This step is required when there exists disjoint subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{v}
of
[n]
such that for
i,i^{\prime}\in U_{v^{\prime}}
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\pi^{*}(i)=\pi^{*}(i^{\prime})\right]=0
. This situation typically arises when the latent variable
\pi^{*}
is related to some permutation.
For example, this step will be implemented for feature matching and seriation, but not for clustering.

Incompatible
Z_{\pi,S}
’s have some strong dependencies since they cannot be jointly non-zero. To cope with these dependencies we rely again on Proposition
B.4
.
For some active
\pi\in[K]^{supp(S^{(0)})}
, we define
\mathcal{V}
the weighted graph with vertex set
[|\mathbf{S}|]
and with

•

an edge of weight
1
between
t,t^{\prime}\in[|\mathbf{S}|]
if and only if there exists
i\in supp(S_{t})
and
i^{\prime}\in supp(S_{t^{\prime}})
such that
\pi(i)=\pi(i^{\prime})
and
i,i^{\prime}\in U_{v^{\prime}}
for some
v^{\prime}\in[v]
,

•

edges of weight
m\in(0,1)
to be chosen later on, otherwise.

We underline that the weighted graph
\mathcal{V}
depends on the choice of
\pi
, and a weight
1
between
t
and
t^{\prime}
encodes incompatibility of
Z_{\pi,S_{t}}
and
Z_{\pi,S_{t^{\prime}}}
.
Let us denote by
\boldsymbol{R}
the connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
. To apply Proposition
B.4
, we must upper bound the cumulant
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{t\in R^{\prime}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{R^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{R}}\right)
in terms of
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}[R]\right)
.

A key feature of the construction of
\mathcal{V}
is that, when
R\subseteq[|\mathbf{S}|]
is such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
has at least an edge of weight
1
, then we have
\prod_{t\in R^{\prime}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}=0
a.s. for all
R^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{R}
of size at least
2
. As a consequence

\mathop{cumul}\left(\big(\prod_{t\in R^{\prime}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\big)_{R^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{R}}\right)=0
,
and Proposition
B.4
implicitely expurgates such incompatible
Z_{\pi,S}
.

When
\mathcal{V}[R]
has no edge of weight
1
, then
\boldsymbol{R}
reduces to singletons, and
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{t\in R^{\prime}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{R^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{R}}\right)
is equal to
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)
. Furthermore we have a simple formula for the weights of
\mathcal{V}[R]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
R\subseteq[|\mathbf{S}|]
, we have

\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}[R]\right)=m^{cc\left(\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]\right)-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where
cc\left(\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]\right)
stands for the number of connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
.

Let us define
\boldsymbol{S}[R]:=\left(S_{t}\right)_{t\in R}
.
The above results show that, for applying Proposition
B.4
, it is sufficient to upper-bound the cumulants

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)=:C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)\kern 5.0pt,

(14)

in terms of
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}[R]\right)=m^{|R|-1}
, for all
R\subseteq[|\mathbf{S}|]
for which
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have edges of weight
1
. Once this is done, Proposition
B.4
provides a bound on the the cumulant
C_{\mathbf{S}}(\pi)
in terms of
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)
.

Step 4: leveraging Lemma
3.1

We have now reduced the problem of controlling the cumulants
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
to the problem of controlling the cumulants (
14
)
for
R\subseteq[|\mathbf{S}|]
for which
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have edges of weight
1
. We define
I_{t}=supp(S_{t})
and
I_{\Delta}=\cup_{t\in\Delta}supp(S_{t})
for
\Delta\subseteq R
. In the models that we consider, we observe that the variables
\overline{Z}_{t}:=Z_{\pi,S_{t}}
have mixed moments of the form (
8
). Hence, Lemma
3.1

ensures that the cumulants
C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)
defined in (
14
) fulfill

•

When
r\geq 1
,
|I_{R}|^{2}y_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}y_{0}
,

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)|\leq 4|R|^{2|R|}\eta^{|I_{R}|}\left(|I_{R}|^{2}y_{0}\right)^{|R|-1}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{y_{0}}\right)^{cc(\mathcal{B})-1}.

•

When
r=0
, and
|I_{R}|^{2}x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
,

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\eta^{|I_{R}|}\left(|I_{R}|^{2}x_{0}\right)^{|R|-1}.

For example, assume that we are in the case
r=0
(the case
r\geq 1
can be treated similarly). Setting
m=|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}x_{0}
, upper-bounding
|R|^{2|R|}\leq|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}
, and noticing that
\psi(R)=\eta^{|I_{R}|}
is super-multiplicative for
\eta\in(0,1]
, Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
ensure that

C_{\mathbf{S}}(\pi)\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\eta^{supp(S^{(0)})}\left(|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}x_{0}\right)^{cc\left(\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle\right)-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where we recall that
cc\left(\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle\right)
stands for the number of connected components of the graph induced by the edges of weight
1
of
\mathcal{V}
.
Plugging this bound in (
13
), we end-up with an upper bound of the form

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\leq D_{\infty}\,\tilde{\psi}(S^{(0)})\,w^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1},

for some super-multiplicative function
\tilde{\psi}
and some
w\in(0,1)
, both of them dependending on the set of active
\pi
. We can then come back to Step 1, and proceed to the pending application of Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
, to get a bound on the cumulant of interest

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\delta\left(\pi^{*}(i_{s}),\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})\right)\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\leq D_{\infty}(l+1)^{6(l+1)}\,\tilde{\psi}([0,l])\,w^{cc(\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle)-1}.

In Appendix
C
, we present a simple instantiation of the proof technique outlined in this section, applied to the problem of clustering with prescribed group sizes. The more involved proofs for multiple feature matching and seriation are provided in Appendix
D
and
E
respectively.

4
Seriation

The seriation problem amounts to order
n
objects from pairwise noisy measurements
(Y_{ij})
of interaction between items
i
and
j
. The purpose of this section is to highlight a statistical-computational gap for seriation through the prism of low-degree polynomials.

For this reason, we focus here on the arguably simplest model, which is defined as follows. Henceforth,
\rho
is a non-negative integer corresponding to a scale parameter, and
\lambda
is positive and corresponds to a signal strength. For some unknown permutation
\pi^{*}
of
[n]
, the signal matrix
X
is defined by

X_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda&\text{ whenever }|\pi^{*}(i)-\pi^{*}(j)|\leq\rho\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(15)

Given an observation
1
1

1

Actually, the diagonal of
Y
is irrelevant, but we define it for the purpose of keeping unified notation through this manuscript.
of the matrix
Y=X+E
where the entries of
E
are i.i.d. and follow a centered Gaussian distribution with variance
1
, our objective is recover the underlying permutation
\pi^{*}
. In this section, we focus on the reconstruction problem, which amounts to estimate the matrix
X
in Frobenius norm. In Appendix
G
, we discuss other loss functions, that are pertaining to the estimation of
\pi^{*}
.

When
\pi^{*}
is sampled uniformly at random, for some given
i\neq j
, the probability to have
|\pi^{*}(i)-\pi^{*}(j)|\leq\rho
is
\varphi:=\frac{2\rho}{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\rho+1}{2n}\right)
. Hence, the best constant estimator in terms of the
L^{2}
-norm is
X_{0}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\right]
, which is the matrix whose diagonal is equal to
\lambda
, and whose non-diagonal terms are equal to
\lambda\varphi
.
An estimator
\hat{X}
is said to achieve weak reconstruction when
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}]
is small compared to the risk
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|X-X_{0}\|_{F}^{2}=\frac{n}{n-1}\varphi(1-\varphi)
of the best constant estimator.

We focus on finding conditions
(\lambda,\rho)
such that weak-construction is possible in polynomial-time. Our main result is the following LD lower bound.
Considering a Bayesian setting where
\pi^{*}
is sampled uniformly at random among all permutations. For a degree
D>0
, we define the low-degree
MMSE_{\leq D}
by

MMSE_{\leq D}:=\inf_{\hat{X}\in\mathbb{R}_{D}[Y]}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(16)

With the proof technique of Section
3
, we prove
the following LD lower bound, which provides evidence of hardness for recovering
X
(and thereby the permutation
\pi^{*}
).

Theorem 4.1
.

Let us suppose that
n\geq 2(2D+2)^{2}
and
\zeta:=2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\max\left(1,\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n}\right)<1
. Then,

MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\lambda^{2}\frac{n}{n-1}\left[\varphi(1-\varphi)-2^{18}\frac{\rho^{2}}{(n-1)^{2}}\left(D+1\right)^{44}\frac{\max(\zeta,1/n)}{1-\max(\zeta,1/n)}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

We focus on the case where
D\asymp\log(n)
, which, according to the low-degree conjecture, is a strong indication to rule out the performances of polynomial-time algorithms
[
KWB19
]
. If
\lambda\preccurlyeq_{\log(n)}[1\wedge\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\rho}]
, then
MMSE_{\leq D}
is close to the risk
MMSE_{\leq 0}
of
X_{0}
, and it is therefore impossible to achieve weak reconstruction with low-degree polynomials.

Whereas statistical-computational gaps in seriation have been conjectured e.g. in
[
CM23
]
, we are only aware of the computational lower bounds of
[
BCV24
]
, where they consider different models, with
\pi^{*}
a uniform element of
[n]^{n}
instead of a random permutation. The difficulty in establishing lower bounds for seriation stems from the fact that, when
\pi^{*}
is an uniform permutation,
\pi^{*}(i)
and
\pi^{*}(j)
are not independent.

Conversely, whenever
\lambda\succcurlyeq_{\log(n)}[1\wedge\sqrt{n}/\rho]
, there exists a polynomial-time estimator of
X
achieving weak reconstruction.

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider the seriation model (
15
) with an unknown partition
\pi^{*}
.

•

Let
\hat{\pi}
be the polynomial-time estimator
PINES
from
[
BCV24
]
. Then,

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{5\lambda^{2}}{n}+\frac{\lambda}{n^{1/2}}\sqrt{\log(n)}\ ,

(17)

•

Define the thresholding estimator
\hat{X}_{th}
defined by
(\hat{X}_{th})_{ij}=\lambda\mathbf{1}\{Y_{ij}>\lambda/2\}
. If
\lambda\geq 4\sqrt{\log(n)}
, then

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}}\ .

In particular, when
\lambda\geq c\sqrt{\log(n)}\big[1\wedge\sqrt{n}/\rho]
, then the risk of those estimator is significantly smaller than
2\lambda^{2}\rho/n\asymp\varphi(1-\varphi)
, and those estimators therefore achieve weak reconstruction.

In contrast, weak reconstruction of the matrix
X
is information-theoretically possible in the much wider regime where

\lambda\geq\sqrt{\log(n)/\rho}
, as shown by the next proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

Consider
\hat{\pi}\in\arg\min_{\pi}\|Y-X_{\pi}\|_{F}^{2}
be the least-square estimator. Then,

\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{8\log(n)}{n}\ .

As a consequence,
X_{\hat{\pi}}
achieves weak reconstruction as long as
\lambda
is large compared to
\log(n)/\sqrt{\rho}
.

In Appendix
G
, we shall deduce from Theorem
4.1
some hardness results for ordering Toeplitz-Robinson matrices, thereby answering an open question in
[
BCV24
]
.

5
Clustering with constant size groups

The problem of clustering a mixture of isotropic Gaussians can be summarized as follows. For some unknown vectors
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, noise level
\sigma>0
, and partition
G^{*}=\left\{G^{*}_{1},\ldots,G^{*}_{K}\right\}
of
[n]
, we observe
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
with entries sampled independently with distribution

Y_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{kj},\sigma^{2}),\quad\text{for}\ i\in G^{*}_{k}\quad\text{and}\ j\in[p].

The goal is to recover
G^{*}
.
This problem has received a lot of attention
[
Das99
,
VW04
,
LZ16
,
RV17
,
GV19
,
LL22
,
DKPZ23
,
EGV24
]
.
When the partition originates from independent assignments, i.e.
G_{k}^{*}=\left\{i:k^{*}_{i}=k\right\}
with
k^{*}_{1},\ldots,k^{*}_{n}
i.i.d. uniform on
[K]
, the
Information-Theoretic and Low-Degree thresholds are now well established
[
EGV24
,
EGV25
]
, except in a narrow parameter regime.

We consider in this section the problem of clustering a Gaussian mixture with groups
G^{*}_{k}
of known size
n/K
. While close to the independent assignment setting, handling such a size constraint raises some challenges due to the dependences inherited from the constraint.
Our main motivation for considering this problem is to provide, in Appendix
C
, a simple instantiation of the proof technique of Section
3
, before delving into the more involved proofs of Theorems
2.2
and
4.1
in Appendices
D
and
E
.

To prove the LD bound, we consider a Bayesian setting with the following prior.

Definition 2
.

Assume that
K
divides
n
. We draw
\pi^{*}(1),\ldots,\pi^{*}(n)
uniformly among all
\pi\in[K]^{n}
satisfying
|\left\{i\in[1,n],\kern 5.0pt\pi^{*}(i)=k\right\}|=\frac{n}{K}
, for all
k\in[1,K]
. For a given
\bar{\Delta}>0
, independently from
(\pi^{*}(i))_{i\in[1,n]}
, we draw
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
i.i.d. with
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\lambda^{2}I_{p}\right)
distribution, with
\lambda^{2}=\frac{1}{p}\bar{\Delta}^{2}\sigma^{2}
.

Then, conditionally on
(\pi^{*}(i))_{i=1,\ldots,n}
and
(\mu_{k})_{k=1,\ldots,K}
, the
Y_{i}
’s are sampled independently with
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{\pi^{*}(i)},\sigma^{2}I_{p})
distribution. The partition
G^{*}
is obtained from the
\pi^{*}(i)
’s with the canonical partitioning
G^{*}_{k}=\{i\in[1,n],\kern 5.0pt\pi^{*}(i)=k\}
.

With high probability under this prior, the separation
\Delta^{2}=\min_{k\neq l}\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}
satisfies
\Delta^{2}=\bar{\Delta}^{2}(1+o_{p}(1))
, when
p\gtrsim\log(K)
.

The partition
G^{*}
and its estimator
\hat{G}
are combinatorial objects and are not, as such, well suited to low-degree polynomials. As done previously (see e.g.
[
BMV+18
]
), we rather focus on the problem of estimating the partnership matrix
\Gamma^{*}
defined by
\Gamma^{*}_{ij}=\mathbf{1}\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi^{*}(j)\}
, for
i,j\in[n]
. Given any partition
G=G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
, we define the partnership matrix
\Gamma^{G}
by
\Gamma^{G}_{ij}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathbf{1}\{i,j\in G_{k}\}
. We know from
[
EGV24
]
p.5 that

\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\|\Gamma^{G}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\min_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{K}}{1\over n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G^{*}_{k}\triangle\hat{G}_{\pi(k)}|=:2\,err(\hat{G},G^{*})\ ,

(18)

where
\triangle
represents the symmetric difference,
\mathcal{S}_{K}
the permutation group on
[K]
, and where
err(\hat{G},G^{*})
is the average proportion of misclassified points in
\hat{G}
. Hence, estimating
\Gamma^{*}
in polynomial-time with small square-Frobenius distance is no harder than building a polynomial-time estimator
\hat{G}
with a small error
err(\hat{G},G^{*})
. Next proposition, proved in Appendix
H.2
, shows that non trivial estimation of
\Gamma^{*}
is as hard as non trivial recovery of
G^{*}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose we are in a regime where
K,n
(and possibly
p
) go to infinity, with
n/K=o(1)
. If

\inf_{\hat{\Gamma}\ poly-time}{1\over n(n-1)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=K^{-1}(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt,

then, all polynomial-time estimators
\hat{G}
with prescribed group size
n/K
satisfy
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[err(\hat{G},G^{*})\right]=1+o(1)
.

In other words, it is impossible to reconstruct in polynomial time the partition
G^{*}
better than random guessing in the regime where
MMSE_{poly}=\frac{1}{K}(1+o(1))
. As a proxy for
MMSE_{poly}
, we analyse the Low-Degree
MMSE
defined by

MMSE_{\leq D}:=\inf_{\hat{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}_{D}[Y]}\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(19)

Theorem 5.2
.

We suppose
p\geq\frac{n}{K^{2}}
,
n\geq\max\left(2(D+2)^{2}K,(D+2)^{4}\right)
,
K\geq D+2
and
\zeta:=\frac{\overline{\Delta}^{4}}{p}D^{22}(D+2)^{2}\max\left(1,\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)<1
. Then, under the prior of Definition
2
, we have

MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{K^{2}}\left(1+18D^{21}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem
5.2
entails that, when the size of the groups is known to be exactly
n/K
, the clustering problem is computationally hard when,

\Delta^{2}\leq_{\log}\min\left(\sqrt{\frac{pK^{2}}{n}},\sqrt{p}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(20)

This threshold matches, up to possible logarithmic terms, the LD lower bounds established in
[
EGV25
]
for random-size partitions.
Hence, the perfect knowledge of the cluster sizes does not help (significantly) for cluster recovery.

Conversely, most state-of-the-art procedures based on spectral projection
[
VW04
]
, hierarchical-clustering
[
EGV24
]
, SDP
[
GV19
]
or high-order tensor projection
[
LL22
]
are valid for partitions with fixed constant size –see e.g. Proposition 3.2 in
[
EGV25
]
, so that (
20
) turns out to be matched by polynomial-time procedures in most regimes.

Appendix A
Information-theoretic thresholds for multiple feature matching

As discussed in Section
2
, the Multiple Feature Matching problem can be seen as the problem of clustering the
n=KM
data points
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
into
K
groups, with the constraint that, for each
m\in[M]
, each cluster
G^{*}_{k}
contains one, and only one, observation from
\left\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:k\in[K]\right\}
. In this section, we build on this observation for deriving a minimax optimal matching procedure, and we discuss thoroughly the link between clustering and multiple feature matching.

IT thresholds for multiple feature matching.

A minimax near optimal algorithm for clustering a Gaussian Mixture Model is the Exact
K
-means estimator
[
EGV24
]
. We adapt below this estimator to the Multiple Feature Matching problem.
Given a
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi=(\pi_{m})_{m\in[M]}\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
, we define

Crit(\pi)=\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{m\in[M]}\Big\|Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k)}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k)}\Big\|^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

(21)

This criterion corresponds to the exact
K
-means criterion applied to the partition of
[M]\times[K]
induced by
\pi
. Our estimator
\hat{\pi}
will be any minimizer of this criterion over all
M
-tuples of permutations

\hat{\pi}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}Crit(\pi)\kern 5.0pt.

(22)

The partition of
[K]\times[M]
induced by
\hat{\pi}
corresponds to the minimizer of the
K
-means criterion over the partitions that verify
|G_{k}\cap([K]\times\{m\})|=1
for every
m\in[M]
and
k\in[K]
. Adapting the analysis of the exact
K
-means Criterion made in
[
EGV24
]
, we readily derive the following property, the proof of which is sketched in Appendix
H.5
.

Proposition A.1
.

There exist positive numerical constants
c,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
such that the following holds. If
\Delta^{2}\geq c_{1}\left(\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p\log(K)}{M}}\right)
, then, with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{2}}{n^{2}}
, the estimator (
22
) fulfills

err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})\leq\exp\left(-c_{3}\left(\Delta^{2}\wedge\frac{\Delta^{4}M}{p}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, if
\Delta^{2}\geq c\left(\log(KM)+\sqrt{\frac{p\log(KM)}{M}}\right)
, then, with the same probability we have
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})=0
.

Proposition
A.1
implies that partial recovery (i.e. a small
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})
) is possible when

\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(K)+\sqrt{p\log(K)\over M}\ ,

(23)

and perfect recovery (i.e.
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})=0
) is possible when

\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(Km)+\sqrt{p\log(Km)\over M}\ .

(24)

We now prove that these separation conditions are, up to some multiplicative constant, minimax optimal for multiple feature matching.

Given
\bar{\Delta}>0
, we denote
\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}
the set of
K
-tuple
(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{K})\in(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{K}
such that

\frac{min_{k\neq l}\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\geq\bar{\Delta}^{2}.

Given a
K
-tuple
\mu=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{K})\in(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{K}
, and a set of permutations
\pi=\left\{\pi_{m}:m\in[M]\right\}
, we denote
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}
the distribution of
Y=\left(Y_{k}^{(m)}\right)_{k,m\in[K]\times[M]}
, for which all the
Y_{k}^{(m)}
’s are drawn independently and
Y_{k}^{(m)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{\pi^{*}_{m}(k)},\sigma^{2}I_{p}\right)
. Next theorem, proved in Appendices
H.6
and
H.7
, provides minimax lower-bounds, matching the rates (
23
) and (
24
) derived for the constrained
K
-means estimator (
22
).

Theorem A.2
.

We have the following minimax lower bounds for the multiple feature matching problem.

1.

Perfect recovery.
There exist positive numerical constants
c
,
C
, and
K_{0}
such that the following holds. If
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c\left(\log(KM)+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}\right)
and
K\geq K_{0}
,

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\mu\in\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}}\sup_{\pi^{*}\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{*}}\left(err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})\neq 0\right)\geq C\kern 5.0pt.

2.

Partial recovery.
There exist positive numerical constants
c
,
c^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime\prime}
,
C
, and
K_{0}
such that the following holds. Suppose that
p\geq c\log(K)
,
K\geq K_{0}
and
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c^{\prime}\left(\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(K)}\right)
, then,

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times K}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\mu\in\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}}\sup_{\pi^{*}\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu,\pi^{*}}\left(err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})\right)\geq C\kern 5.0pt.

Combining Proposition
A.1
and Theorem
A.2
, we conclude that the minimax rate for perfect recovery in multiple feature matching is (
23
) and (
24
), up to some multiplicative factor. Comparing these rates to the LD rate (
7
) provides evidence for the existence of a statistical-computational gap for the multiple feature matching problem, when
p\geq_{\log}M/K
.

Multiple feature matching versus clustering.

As mentioned before, multiple feature matching can be seen as the problem of clustering the
n=KM
data points
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
into
K
groups, with the constraint that, for each
m\in[M]
, each cluster
G^{*}_{k}
contains one and only one observation from
\left\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:k\in[K]\right\}
. Given this last constraint, we can expect that a lower separation
\Delta^{2}
is required for successful multiple feature matching, than for clustering
n=KM
points in dimension
p
.
Yet, we observe that both the IT rates (
23
)–(
24
), and the LD rate (
7
) for multiple feature matching are the same than those for clustering
n=KM
points in dimension
p

[
EGV24
,
EGV25
]
. This means, that, in terms of rates (blind to constant factors), multiple feature matching is not easier than clustering. Let us give some intuitions for explaining this observation.

For the IT threshold, the optimal algorithm for multiple feature matching and for clustering is, in both cases, a minimizer of the same
K
-means
criterion, the only difference is that the minimization is performed over
(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
for multiple feature matching, and over the set
\mathcal{P}_{K}(KM)
of all partitions of
[KM]
into
K
groups for clustering. Since the performance of both minimizers of the
K
-means criterion is mainly driven by the concentration of the same quadratic functionals of
Y
, as a first approximation, only the size of the log-cardinalities

\log\left(|(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}|\right)
and
\log\left(|\mathcal{P}_{K}(KM)|\right)
can differentiate the rates.
We observe that
\log\left(|\mathcal{P}_{K}(KM)|\right)\leq KM\log(K)
, while
\log\left(|(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}|\right)\sim KM\log(K)
for
K
large, so both cardinalities are similar. While the constraint
|G_{k}\cap([K]\times\{m\})|=1
for every
m\in[M]
and
k\in[K]
reduces the number of admissible partitions, this reduction is not strong enough in order to significantly differentiate the minimax rates.

As for the LD threshold, we observe that the constraint
|G_{k}\cap([K]\times\{m\})|=1
for every
m\in[M]
and
k\in[K]
is complex and combinatorial in nature.
[
EGV25
]
provides evidence that poly-time algorithms – at least low-degree polynomials – can struggle to fully exploit complex combinatorial structures, as for example in sparse clustering or biclustering. In light of this observation, it is not surprising that poly-time algorithms fail to exploit the constraint
|G_{k}\cap([K]\times\{m\})|=1
for every
m\in[M]
and
k\in[K]
, in order to perform significantly better than unconstrained clustering algorithms.

Finally, we mention that multiple feature matching can be viewed as a specific case of socially fair-clustering
[
KSAM19
,
GSV21
]
. In fair-clustering, we observe
n
data points
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
, each of them having an observed sensitive attribute (gender, social group, etc), say
m\in[M]
. One of the goal in fair-clustering is to provide a partition of
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
minimizing a criterion like
K
-means or
K
-median under the constraint that every demographic group is proportionally represented in each cluster
[
KSAM19
]
.
Considering
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
as data points to be clustered, and seeing the label
m
as the sensitive attribute of
Y_{k}^{(m)}
, the multiple feature matching problem can be seen as a fair-clustering problem. Indeed, if we seek for a partition of
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
into
K
clusters, each of them having an equal number of each label
m
, then, for each cluster, we are forced to peak one, and only one, observation from
\left\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:k\in[K]\right\}
. Hence, a fair clustering of
\big\{Y_{k}^{(m)}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]\big\}
into
K
clusters provide a multiple feature matching.

Appendix B
Background on cumulants

In this section, we summarize results on mixed cumulants of random variables that we shall use in our proof.

B.1
Classical results on cumulants

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}
be random variables on the same space
\mathcal{Y}
. Their cumulant generating function is

K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}):=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l}t_{l^{\prime}}Y_{l^{\prime}}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

and their joint cumulant is the value of the partial derivative of
K(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})
at 0

\mathop{cumul}\left(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}\right):=\left(\frac{\partial^{l}\,K}{\partial t_{1}\cdots\partial t_{l}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l})\right)_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}=0}\kern 5.0pt.

In the proof, we use standard results on cumulants that we state in Lemma
B.1
and
B.2
. We refer e.g. to
[
Nov14
]
for proofs and more details. The first lemma is the Möbius formula that expresses joint cumulant of random variables as a linear combination of their mixed moments.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}
be random variables on the same space
\mathcal{Y}
. For
G
a partition of
[l]
, we write
m(G)=(-1)^{|G|-1}(|G|-1)!
the Möbius function. Then

\mathop{cumul}\left(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}\right)=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{P}([l])}m(G)\prod_{R\in G}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i\in R}Y_{i}\right]\ ,

where
\mathcal{P}([l])
is the collection of all partitions of
[l]
.
In particular, cumulants are multilinear.

The next lemma gives a sufficient condition for the nullity of cumulants.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}
be random variables on the same space
\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose that there exists disjoint sets
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, non-empty and covering
[l]
, such that
(Y_{i})_{i\in I_{1}}
and
(Y_{i})_{i\in I_{2}}
are independent. Then, we have
\mathop{cumul}\left(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}\right)=0
.

B.2
Expansion of cumulants in latent variable models

In some latent models, as considered in Sections
5
and
2
,
the cumulants
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
arising in the control (
2
) of the
MMSE_{\leq D}
have intricate forms. In this subsection, we recall some formulas derived in
[
EGV25
]
. Assume that the signal matrix
X
is of the form

X_{ij}=\mu_{\pi^{*}(i)j},\quad\textrm{for}\quad(i,j)\in[n]\times[p],

(25)

where
\pi^{*}:[n]\mapsto[K]
is possibly randomly generated and the
\mu_{kj}
’s are generated independently according to
\mathcal{N}(0,\lambda^{2})
with
\lambda>0
.
In particular, this encompasses the prior distributions in Definitions
2
and
1
.

The following proposition is a corollary of Theorem 2.5 in
[
EGV25
]
. It shows that for bounding
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
, it suffices to control mixed cumulants in terms of the latent variables
\pi^{*}(i)
.

Proposition B.3
.

[
[
EGV25
]
]
Consider the model (
25
), and fix any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
. If
|\alpha|
is odd, then
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
. For
|\alpha|=2l
, suppose that, for all decomposition
\alpha=\beta_{1}+\ldots+\beta_{l}
, with
\beta_{s}
of the form
\beta_{s}=\left\{(i_{s},j_{s}),(i^{\prime}_{s},j_{s})\right\}
, we have

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left\{x,\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i_{1})=\pi^{*}(i_{2})\right\},\ldots,\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i_{l})=\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{l})\right\}\right\}\right|\leq C_{\alpha}\kern 5.0pt,

then,

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq\lambda^{|\alpha|}|\alpha|^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}-1}C_{\alpha}\kern 5.0pt.

(26)

Proof of Proposition
B.3
.

Let us remark that, in the model (
25
),
(x,X)
has the same law as
(x,-X)
. Thus, if
|\alpha|
is odd, we deduce from the multilinearity of cumulants

\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(-X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{|\alpha|}\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}\right)=-\kappa_{x,\alpha}\kern 5.0pt,

and we directly have
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
.
Suppose that
|\alpha|=2l
. We recall that we consider
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
as a multiset containing
\alpha_{ij}
copies of
(i,j)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)
denote the set of all partitions of
\alpha
into groups of size
2
. Given
G=\left\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{l}\right\}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\left(\alpha\right)
and
s\in[l]
, we define
\beta_{s}(G)\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
with entries
\left(\beta_{s}(G)\right)_{ij}
recording the number of copies of
(i,j)
in
G_{s}
. Then, Theorem 2.5 in
[
EGV25
]
states that

\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\lambda^{\alpha}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)}\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{1}(G)}},\ldots,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{l}(G)}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where, for
\beta=\left\{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\right\}
, we write
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta}}=\mathbf{1}\left\{j=j^{\prime}\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi^{*}(i^{\prime})\right\}
. So, for
G\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)
, writing
\beta_{s}(G)=\left\{(i_{s},j_{s}),(i^{\prime}_{s},j^{\prime}_{s})\right\}
, if there exists
s
such that
j_{s}\neq j^{\prime}_{s}
, then

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{1}(G)}},\ldots,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{l}(G)}}\right)=0\kern 5.0pt,

and, if not, the hypothesis of the proposition leads to

|\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{1}(G)}},\ldots,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\beta_{l}(G)}}\right)|\leq C_{\alpha}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence,
|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq|\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)|\lambda^{|\alpha|}C_{\alpha}\leq\lambda^{|\alpha|}|\alpha|^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}-1}C_{\alpha}
, where we use
|\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)|\leq|\alpha|^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}-1}
.
∎

B.3
Weighted dependency graph and cumulants

Our method for bounding cumulants builds upon the theory of weighted dependency graphs from
[
Fer18
]
, on which we provide an account here. This section will be useful when we want to reduce random variables into product of random variables, as for example in Steps 1 and 3 of Section
3
. For a set
S
, let
(Y_{i})_{i\in S}
be random variables on the same space
\mathcal{Y}
, that are indexed by
S
.

Definition 3

(Weighted Graph)
.

A weighted graph
\mathcal{L}=(S,E)
is an undirected graph with vertex set
S
such that any edge
e
has a weight
w_{e}\in(0,1]
.

Definition 4

(Weight of a graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(S,E)
a weighted graph with vertex set
S
, and weights
w_{e}
for
e\in E
. Given a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{L}
, its weight is the product of the weights of its edges, that is
weight(\mathcal{T})=\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}}w_{e}
. We define
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{L})
as the maximum weight over all spanning tree of
\mathcal{L}
. Here, we use the convention that
\mathbb{M}[\mathcal{L}]=0
if
\mathcal{L}
is not connected.

For
\mathcal{L}
a weighted graph, we define
\mathcal{L}\langle 1\rangle
the graph induced by the edges of weight
1
. Given
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
, we define
\mathcal{L}[S]
the restriction of
\mathcal{L}
to the vertex set
S^{\prime}
.

In the following, we seek to bound cumulants of subfamilies of
(Y_{i})_{i\in S}
in terms of the maximal weight of a weighted graph, that keeps track of the low-dependencies between the random variables
Y_{i}
. To do so, we need to consider super-multiplicative functions on subsets of
S
.

Definition 5
.

Consider a non-negative function
\psi
mapping any subset of
S
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then,
\psi
is said to be super-multiplicative if for any
S_{1}
,
S_{2}\subseteq S
, we have
\psi\left(S_{1}\cup S_{2}\right)\geq\psi(S_{1})\psi(S_{2})
.

We are now in position to state Proposition 5.2 from
[
Fer18
]
, that is very useful to reduce the problem of computing cumulants of random variables, to the problem of computing cumulants of products of random variables.

Proposition B.4
.

[Proposition 5.2 of
[
Fer18
]
]
Let
\mathcal{L}
a weighted graph with vertex set
S
and
\psi
a super-multiplicative function on multisets of elements of
S
. Let
(D_{r})_{r\geq 1}
a sequence of positive numbers. Assume that, for any subset
B\subseteq S
, one has

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\prod_{i\in B_{1}}Y_{i},\ldots,\prod_{i\in B_{l}}Y_{i}\right)\right|\leq D_{|B|}\,\psi(B)\,\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{L}[B]\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
B_{1},\ldots,B_{l}
are the vertex sets of the connected components of
\mathcal{L}\langle 1\rangle[B]
. Then, for all
B\subseteq S
, one has

|\mathop{cumul}\left((Y_{i})_{i\in B}\right)|\leq C_{|B|}\psi(B)\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{L}[B]\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with
C_{r}
a sequence defined recursively by

C_{r}=D_{r}+\underset{G\neq\{[r]\}}{\sum_{G\in\mathcal{P}([r])}}\prod_{R\in G}C_{|R|}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathcal{P}([r])
stands for the collection of all partitions of
[r]
.

Remark 2
.

The proof of Proposition
B.4
in
[
Fer18
]
is a recursion on
B
. Thus, if one wants to upper-bound, for a specific subset
B\subseteq S
, the quantity
|\mathop{cumul}\left((Y_{i})_{i\in B}\right)|
, it is sufficient to upper-bound, for all subsets
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
, the cumulants
|\mathop{cumul}\left(\prod_{i\in B^{\prime}_{1}}Y_{i},\ldots,\prod_{i\in B^{\prime}_{l}}Y_{i}\right)|
, where
B^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,B^{\prime}_{l}
are the vertex sets of the connected components of
L\langle 1\rangle[B^{\prime}]
.

To establish non-asymptotic bounds of cumulants, we provide the following upper bound on the sequence
|C_{r}|
defined in Proposition
B.4
.

Lemma B.5
.

In Proposition
B.4
, if we suppose that the sequence
(D_{r})_{r\geq 1}
is upper-bounded by some constant
D_{\infty}>0
, then, for all
r\geq 1
,
C_{r}\leq D_{\infty}r^{6r}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
D_{\infty}=1
. We shall prove by induction over
r\geq 0
that
C_{r}\leq r^{6r}
. By definition,
C_{0}=1=0^{0}
.

We now assume that, for all
r^{\prime}<r
, we have
C_{r^{\prime}}\leq r^{\prime 6r^{\prime}}
, and we shall prove that
C_{r}\leq r^{6r}
. We begin by isolating one subset in the recursion formula

\displaystyle C_{r}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\pi\neq\{[r]\}}\prod_{R\in\pi}C_{|R|}=1+\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq T\subsetneq[r]}C_{|T|}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}([r]\setminus T)}\prod_{R\in\pi}C_{|R|}\right)=1+\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq T\subsetneq[r]}C_{|T|}\left(2C_{r-|T|}-1\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we apply the recursion hypothesis,

\displaystyle C_{r}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq T\subsetneq[r]}|T|^{6|T|}\left(2(r-|T|)^{6(r-|T|)}-1\right)\leq 2\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq T\subsetneq[r]}|T|^{6|T|}(r-|T|)^{6(r-|T|)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{l=1}^{r-1}\binom{r}{l}l^{6l}(r-l)^{6(r-l)}\kern 5.0pt.

For
l\in[1,r/2]
, we have
\binom{r}{l}l^{6l}(r-l)^{6(r-l)}\leq\binom{r}{l}l^{6l}r^{6(r-l)}\leq r^{6r}\left(\frac{l}{r}\right)^{6l}\binom{r}{l}
. Then,
\log\left(\left(\frac{l}{r}\right)^{6l}\binom{r}{l}\right)\leq 6l\log(l/r)+l\log(er/l)\leq-5l\log(r/l)+l\leq-5l\log(2)+l\leq-3l\log(2)
, where the last inequality comes from the fact that
\log(2)\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, we arrive at

\displaystyle C_{r}

\displaystyle\leq 4r^{6r}\sum_{l=1}^{r/2}2^{-3l}\leq\frac{1}{2}r^{6r}\frac{1-2^{-3r/2}}{1-2^{-3}}\leq r^{6r}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the induction and the proof of Lemma
B.5
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
5.2
– pedagogical instantiation of Section
3

To prove Theorem
5.2
, we proceed in three steps:

1.

We apply
[
SW22
]
to reduce the derivation of LD bound to proving bounds on cumulants
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,X_{\alpha}\right)
;

2.

We instantiate the methodology of Section
3
to upper bound
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
;

3.

We conclude following similar reasoning as in
[
SW22
,
EGV25
]
.

1. From LD bound to cumulants with
[
SW22
]
.

With no loss of generality, we assume through this proof that
\sigma^{2}=1
.
Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n\geq\max\left(2(D+2)^{2}K,\left(D+2\right)^{4}\right)
and
K\geq D+2
. We suppose
p\geq\frac{n}{K^{2}}
and

\zeta:=\frac{\overline{\Delta}^{4}}{p}D^{22}(D+2)^{2}\max\left(1,\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)<1\kern 5.0pt.

Since the minimization problem defining
MMSE_{\leq D}
in Equation (
19
) is separable, and since the random variables
M^{*}_{ij}
are exchangeable, the
MMSE_{\leq D}
can be reduced to

\displaystyle MMSE_{\leq D}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{n}\inf_{f_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f_{ij}(Y)-\Gamma^{*}_{ij}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-\Gamma^{*}_{12}\right)^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

In the remaining of the proof, we write
x=\Gamma^{*}_{12}=\mathbf{1}_{\pi^{*}(1)=\pi^{*}(2)}
. Then, our goal is to upper-bound

MMSE_{\leq D}=\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-x\right)^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

The model of Definition
2
is a particular instance of the Additive Gaussian Noise Model considered in
[
SW22
]
.
Therefore, we can use Theorem 2.2 from
[
SW22
]
, stated in Equation (
2
). For a matrix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
, we define
|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{ij}
and
\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{ij}!
. We write
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
for the signal matrix, whose
i
-th row is the vector
\mu_{\pi^{*}(i)}
. Using Equation (
2
), we have the lower-bound

MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{K^{2}}-\underset{\alpha\neq 0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\kern 5.0pt,

(27)

where, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
,
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}(x,X^{\alpha}):=\mathop{cumul}(x,X_{a_{1}},\ldots,X_{a_{m}})
, with
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
the multiset that contains
\alpha_{ij}
copies of
(i,j)
, for
i,j\in[1,n]\times[1,p]
.

Hence, in order to prove the theorem, it is enough to prove the bound

\underset{\alpha\neq 0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq\frac{18D^{21}}{K^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\kern 5.0pt.

(28)

In light of Equation (
28
), we shall identify the zero cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
and
control
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
. For this purpose, it is convenient to represent
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
as a bipartite multi-graph.
More precisely, we define
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
as the bipartite multi-graph on two disjoint sets of nodes
U=\{u_{i},i\in[1,n]\}
and
V=\{v_{j},j\in[1,p]\}
, with
\alpha_{ij}
edges between
u_{i}
and
v_{j}
, for
i,j\in[1,n]\times[1,p]
. We denote
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
the restriction of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
to its non-isolated nodes and to
\left\{u_{1},u_{2}\right\}
.
Henceforth,
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
stands for the multigraph
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
to which we have added an edge between
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
We write
cc(\alpha)
the number of connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
. We denote
supp(\alpha):=\left\{i\in[n],\alpha_{i:}\neq 0\right\}
,
col(\alpha)=\left\{j\in[p],\alpha_{:j}\neq 0\right\}
.

2. Bounding
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
with the methodology outlined Section
3
.

The crux for deriving the lower bound of Theorem
5.2
lies in obtaining an upper-bound on the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
.
Following our methodology outlined Section
3
, we obtain the following upper-bound.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
\alpha\neq 0\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
. If
n\geq 2\left(|\alpha|+2\right)^{2}K
and
K\geq|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
, then,

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq 4\lambda^{|\alpha|}|\alpha|^{5|\alpha|+9}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\left(\frac{K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc(\alpha)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

For clarity and pedagogical purposes, the proof of this result is deferred to Appendix
C.1
.

Remark 3
.

In the case where
cc(\alpha)=1
and where
[1,2]\subseteq supp(\alpha)
, we recover, up to some
|\alpha|^{|\alpha|}
terms, the bound obtained in
[
EGV25
]
for independent labels.

3. Concluding similarly as in
[
SW22
,
EGV25
]
.

It remains to characterize the set of
\alpha
’s such that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
and to upper-bound the sum of the remaining cumulants in (
28
).

3.a. Identifying non zero
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
.
In the case where the labels are sampled independently, as in
[
EGV25
]
, it is possible to build upon the independence of the random variables involved in the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
and thereby to easily deduce that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
when
cc(\alpha)>1
– see
[
EGV25
]
. As no such independence holds in our case, the condition for having
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
is more involved.
Suppose that there exists
j_{0}\in col(\alpha)
such that
|\alpha_{:j_{0}}|=1
. Then, since
\left(x,(X_{ij})_{ij\in[n]\times[p]}\right)
has the same distribution as
\left(x,((-1)^{j=j_{0}}X_{ij})_{ij\in[n]\times[p]}\right)
, we deduce from the multilinearity of cumulants that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=-\mathop{cumul}\left(x,X_{\alpha}\right)=-\kappa_{x,\alpha}
, and so
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
. Hence, for having
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
, we need
|\alpha|\geq 2|col(\alpha)|\geq 2
. Moreover, since
|\alpha|+1
corresponds to the number of edges of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
and since
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|+|col(\alpha)|
corresponds to the number of nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
, we deduce that
|\alpha|\geq|col(\alpha)|+|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-cc(\alpha)-1
. Putting these two conditions together leads to the following condition on the
\alpha
’s such that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0

|\alpha|\geq\max\left(2|col(\alpha)|,|col(\alpha)|+|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-cc(\alpha)-1\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Let us identify two other constraints satisfied by
supp(\alpha)
,
cc(\alpha)
and
|\alpha|
.
Each connected component of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
contains at least one point of
\left(u_{i}\right)_{i\in supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}}
, with one of them containing both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. Hence,
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|\geq cc(\alpha)+1
. It is also clear that
|\alpha|+2\geq|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
.

3.b. Bounding the sum (
28
).

Let
d,r,m,cc\geq 1
, with
d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)
and
d+2\geq m\geq cc+1
. There are at most
(rm)^{d}p^{r}n^{m-2}\leq\left(d(d+2)\right)^{d}p^{r}n^{m-2}
matrices
\alpha
satisfying
