|\alpha|=d
,
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|=m
,
|col(\alpha)|=r
and
cc(\alpha)=cc
.
Since
|\alpha|\leq D
,
K\geq D+2
, and
n\geq 2(D+2)^{2}K

by assumption of Theorem
5.2
,
we can apply Lemma
C.1
, and get

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\neq 0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{d=2}^{D}\sum_{\alpha:\ |\alpha|=d}\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\underset{d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)}{\underset{d+2\geq m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{r,cc\geq 1}}}\lambda^{2d}(d+2)^{d}d^{11d+18}p^{r}\left(\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-2}\left(\frac{K^{2}m^{4}}{n^{2}}\right)^{cc-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16D^{18}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\underset{d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)}{\underset{d+2\geq m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{r,cc\geq 1}}}\lambda^{2d}\left(D^{11}(D+2)\right)^{d}p^{r}\left(\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1}\left(\frac{(D+2)^{4}}{n}\right)^{cc-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16D^{18}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\underset{d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)}{\underset{d+2\geq m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{r,cc\geq 1}}}\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\right)^{d}p^{r}\left(\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1}\kern 5.0pt\ ,

where we used in the last line that
n\geq(D+2)^{4}
. Let us fix
d,r,m,cc\geq 1
, with
d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)
and
d+2\geq m\geq cc+1
, and let us upper-bound the quantity
\Upsilon:=\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\right)^{d}p^{r}\left(\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1}
.

First, let us consider the case
r\geq m-cc-1
. Leveraging the constraint
d\geq 2r
, we derive

\displaystyle\Upsilon\leq

\displaystyle\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\right)^{d-2r}\left(\lambda^{4}D^{22}(D+2)^{2}p\right)^{r-(m-cc-1)}\left(\lambda^{4}D^{22}(D+2)^{2}p\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1}\leq\zeta^{d/2}\kern 5.0pt,

by definition of
\zeta
.

Second, when
r<m-cc-1
, we use the constraint
d\geq r+m-cc-1
to obtain

\displaystyle\Upsilon\leq

\displaystyle\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\right)^{d-r-m+cc+1}\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}p\right)^{r}\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\right)^{d-r-m+cc+1}\left(D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\frac{n}{K^{2}}\right)^{m-cc-1-r}\left(D^{22}(D+2)^{2}\lambda^{4}\frac{pn}{K^{2}}\right)^{r}\kern 5.0pt.

It is clear that
D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\leq\sqrt{\zeta}
and
D^{22}(D+2)^{2}\lambda^{4}\frac{pn}{K^{2}}\leq\zeta
. For the remaining term, using
p\geq\frac{n}{K^{2}}
, we have
D^{11}(D+2)\lambda^{2}\frac{n}{K^{2}}=\frac{n}{K^{2}p}D^{11}(D+2)\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq\sqrt{D^{22}(D+2)^{2}\frac{n}{K^{2}p}\bar{\Delta}^{4}}\leq\sqrt{\zeta}
. We end up with
\Upsilon\leq\zeta^{d/2}
.
Hence, we can conclude

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\neq 0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16D^{18}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\underset{d\geq\max\left(2r,r+m-cc-1\right)}{\underset{d+2\geq m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{r,cc\geq 1}}}\zeta^{d/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16D^{18}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\frac{d(d+1)(d+4)}{4}\zeta^{d/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{18D^{21}}{K^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used,
d(d+1)(d+4)\leq 4.5d^{3}
for
d\geq 2
, and the identity
\sum_{d\geq 2}z^{d}=\frac{z^{2}}{1-z}
, for any
-1<z<1
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

C.1
Proof of Lemma
C.1
– simple instantiation of Section
3

For proving Lemma
C.1
, we instantiate the methodology outlined in Section
3
.

Step 0: From
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
to cumulants like (
4
).

A preliminary step is to apply Proposition
B.3
in order to reduce the problem of bounding
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
to the problem of bounding cumulants like (
4
).

Let
\alpha\neq 0\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times p}
. We recall the hypotheses
n\geq 2(|\alpha|+2)^{2}K
and
K\geq|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
.
According to Proposition
B.3
, all we need to upper-bound
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
is to upper-bound,
for any decomposition
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l})
of
\alpha
with
|\beta_{s}|=2
of the form
\beta_{s}=\left\{(i_{s},j_{s}),(i^{\prime}_{s},j_{s})\right\}
, the cumulant

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i_{s})=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)\right)
.
Let us take the convention
i_{0}=1
,
i_{0}^{\prime}=2
, so that we can write

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(29)

which is of the form (
4
) of the cumulants that are dealt with in Section
3
. We can now follow the general methodology described in Section
3
.

Step 1: Reduction.

As explained in Section
3
, the first step is to reduce the control of the cumulant (
29
), to the control of cumulants of products of strongly dependent
\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}
.
We introduce a weighted graph
\mathcal{W}
to which we will apply Proposition
B.4
. Let
\mathcal{W}
be the weighted graph on
[0,l]
with weights
w_{s,s^{\prime}}
defined by;

•

If
\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}\cap\left\{i_{s^{\prime}},i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\right\}\neq\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
;

•

If
\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}\cap\left\{i_{s^{\prime}},i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\right\}=\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}/n
.

We observe that when
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
, there exists some strong dependencies within the variables
\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i_{s^{\prime}}\right),\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\right)
. As for the value
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}/n
for the second case, it is chosen based on forthcoming Lemma
C.2
.
Let us fix
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
. We recall that
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
stands for the weighted graph induced by
S^{(0)}
, where we only keep the edges of weight one. Let us write
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
for the partition of
S^{(0)}
induced by the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. In order to apply Proposition
B.4
, we shall upper-bound, for such an
S^{(0)}
, the cumulant

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with respect to the maximal weight
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)=\left(\frac{K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}
. The next lemma (steps 2-4 of Section
3
), proved in Section
C.2
, provides such an upper-bound.

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose
n\geq 2(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}K
and
K\geq|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
. Let
S^{(0)}\subset[0,l]
and let
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
denote the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. Writing
supp(S^{(0)})=\cup_{s\in S^{(0)}}\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
, we have

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{3|\boldsymbol{S}|+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\left(\frac{K|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

From Lemma
C.2
, we deduce that for any
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
, with
\boldsymbol{S}
the set of connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{3|\boldsymbol{S}|+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The function
S^{(0)}\to\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}
is super-multiplicative. Moreover,
4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{3|\boldsymbol{S}|+1}\leq 4\left(l+1\right)^{3l+4}
. Hence, applying Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
, we get that

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\right|\leq 4\left(l+1\right)^{9l+10}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In other words, writing
cc(\mathcal{W})
the number of connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle
, we have

\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\right|\leq$\mbox{}\hfil\\
4\left(l+1\right)^{9l+10}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\left(\frac{K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc(\mathcal{W})-1}\kern 5.0pt.

We recall that
cc(\alpha)
stands for the number of connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
. For all
s\in[0,l]
,
u_{i_{s}}
and
u_{i^{\prime}_{s}}
are in the same connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
, since they are connected via
v_{j_{s}}
. Moreover, for
s\neq s^{\prime}
,
\{u_{i_{s}},u_{i^{\prime}_{s}}\}
and
\{u_{i_{s^{\prime}}},u_{i^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}\}
are in the same connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}
if and only if
s
and
s^{\prime}
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle
. We deduce from these two claims that
cc(\alpha)=cc(\mathcal{W})
, and so

\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\right|\leq$\mbox{}\hfil\\
4\left(l+1\right)^{9l+10}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\left(\frac{K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc(\alpha)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

This being true for all decomposition
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}
of
\alpha
, we get from Proposition
B.3
that

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq 4\lambda^{|\alpha|}|\alpha|^{5|\alpha|+9}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\left(\frac{K(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|)^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc(\alpha)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
C.1
.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
C.2

Step 2: Expansion.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
and let
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
be the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
, which is the subgraph induced by
S^{(0)}
obtained only keeping the edges of
\mathcal{W}
of weight
1
.
The second step amounts to expand the cumulant

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|=\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\right|\kern 5.0pt,

over all possible values of
\pi^{*}
.
For any
S\subseteq[0,l]
, We write
supp(S)=\cup_{s\in S}\left\{i_{s},i^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
. By definition of the graph
\mathcal{W}
and of
\boldsymbol{S}
, the subsets
supp(S)
, for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, are disjoint, and form a partition of
supp(S^{(0)})
. Moreover, for all
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, and for all
i,i^{\prime}\in supp(S)
, there exists a path
s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}\subseteq S
, such that
i\in\left\{i_{s_{1}},i^{\prime}_{s_{1}}\right\}
,
i^{\prime}\in\left\{i_{s_{h}},i^{\prime}_{s_{h}}\right\}
, and such that
(s_{h^{\prime}-1},s_{h^{\prime}})
, for
h^{\prime}\in[2,h]
, is an edge of weight
1
of
\mathcal{W}
. Thus, for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
,

\pi^{*}(i_{s})=\pi^{*}(i^{\prime}_{s})
if and only if
\pi^{*}
is constant on
supp(S)
, and we have

\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}=\sum_{a\in[K]}\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=a\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, we are interested in configurations
\pi^{*}
that are constant on each
S_{l}
for
l=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|
. For that purpose, we focus on

\pi=(\pi(S_{l}))_{l=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|}\in[K]^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
. Then, using the multilinearity of cumulants, we get

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(i^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)=\sum_{(\pi(S_{l}))_{l=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|}\in[K]^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}}C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\kern 5.0pt,

(30)

where

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi):=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(S)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Step 3: Pruning incompatible configurations.

No pruning required in this case.

Step 4: Leveraging Lemma
3.1
.

The last step is to recognize some variables having mixed moment of the form (
8
).
In the following, for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, we write
Z_{\pi,S}=\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi\left(S\right)\right\}
. We denote
B_{1},\ldots,B_{K}
the partition
2
2

2

Actually, this is not a partition as some of the
B_{k}
’s are empty; we only use that the
B_{k}
’s do not intersect and cover
supp(S^{(0)})
.
of
supp(S^{(0)})
induced by
\pi
, i.e
B_{k}=\cup_{S\in\boldsymbol{S},\pi\left(S\right)=k}supp(S)
. We recall that we write
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
. Let
\Delta\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
and let us denote
I_{\Delta}=\cup_{t\in\Delta}supp(S_{t})
. Since
K\geq|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
by assumption, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right]=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\forall t\in\Delta,\forall i\in supp(S_{t}),\pi^{*}(i)=\pi\left(S_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)\times\ldots\times(n-|I_{\Delta}|+1)}\prod_{k\in[K]}\frac{n}{K}\times\ldots\times\left(\frac{n}{K}-|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{k}|+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|I_{\Delta}|}\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{n}}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-\frac{|I_{\Delta}|-1}{n}}\prod_{k\in[K]}\left(1-\frac{K}{n}\right)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{k}|-1\right)\frac{K}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(31)

We recognize mixed moments of the form (
8
).
Applying Lemma
3.1
with
\eta=\frac{1}{K}
,
x_{0}=\frac{1}{n}
and
y_{0}=\frac{K}{n}
, we get that, whenever
|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}K/n\leq 0.5
and
K\geq 2
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\right|\leq 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}\frac{K}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{\#\pi-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where we denote
\#\pi=|\left\{\pi(S),S\in\boldsymbol{S}\right\}|
.

Conclusion.

Back to (
30
), to upper-bound
|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|
, it remains to sum
over all possible
\pi=(\pi(S_{l}))_{l=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|}\in[K]^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}

\pi\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}

\displaystyle\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi=\left(\pi(S)\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}}4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{K|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{\#\pi-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{K|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\sum_{k=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\underset{|\pi|=k}{\sum_{\pi=\left(\pi(S)\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}}}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{K|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\sum_{k=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{k-1}K^{k}k^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4|\boldsymbol{S}|^{3|\boldsymbol{S}|+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\left(\frac{K|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of Lemma
C.2
.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Remark 4
.

The observation
Y
can either be seen as a matrix
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{KM\times p}
or as a tensor
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}
. For clarity, we consider throughout the proof the tensor notation. But, we can still apply the framework for proving LD lower-bounds with latent structure from
[
EGV25
]
which is written for the case where we observe a matrix
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume in this proof that
\sigma^{2}=1
. Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that
K\geq 2(D+2)^{2}
. We suppose

\zeta:=64D^{40}\lambda^{4}p\max\left(1,\frac{M}{K}\right)<1\kern 5.0pt.

(32)

Since the minimization problem defining
MMSE_{\leq D}
in Equation (
19
) is separable, and since the random variables
\Gamma^{*}_{(k,m),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})}
are jointly exchangeable both with respect to
(k,k^{\prime})
and
(m,m^{\prime})
, the
MMSE_{\leq D}
can be reduced to

\displaystyle MMSE_{\leq D}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{KMK(M-1)}\sum_{u=(k,m),u^{\prime}=(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})\text{ with }m\neq m^{\prime}}\inf_{f_{uu^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f_{uu^{\prime}}(Y)-\Gamma^{*}_{uu^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-\Gamma^{*}_{(1,1),(1,2)}\right)^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

For all
(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]
, in order to keep in line with the notation of Section
3
, we write
\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi^{*}_{m}(k)
. In the remaining of the proof, we write
x=\Gamma^{*}_{(1,1),(1,2)}=\mathbf{1}\{\pi^{*}(1,1)=\pi^{*}(1,2)\}
. Then, our goal is to upper-bound

MMSE_{\leq D}=\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}(Y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-x\right)^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

The model of Definition
1
is a particular case of the Gaussian Additive Model considered by
[
SW22
]
. Therefore, we can use Theorem 2.2 from
[
SW22
]
, stated in Equation (
2
). For a tensor
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}
, we define
|\alpha|=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{k,m,j}
and
\alpha!=\prod_{k,m,j}\alpha_{k,m,j}
. We write
X\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}
the signal matrix defined by
X_{k,m,j}=\left(\mu_{\pi^{*}(k,m)}\right)_{j}
. Using Equation (
2
), we have the following lower-bound on the low-degree MMSE

MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{K^{2}}-\underset{0<|\alpha|\leq D}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\kern 5.0pt,

(33)

where
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}
is the cumulant
\mathop{cumul}(x,\left\{X_{k,m,j}\right\}_{(k,m,j)\in\alpha})
, where
\left\{X_{k,m,j}\right\}_{(k,m,j)\in\alpha}
is the multiset that contains
\alpha_{k,m,j}
copies of
X_{k,m,j}
, for
(k,m,j)\in[K]\times[M]\times[p]
.
Hence, in order to prove Theorem
2.2
, it is sufficient to prove that

\underset{0<|\alpha|\leq D}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq\frac{64(D+1)D^{36}}{K^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\kern 5.0pt.

(34)

The first step is to find necessary conditions for having
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
. In the following, we define

\displaystyle supp(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\left\{(k,m)\in[K]\times[M],\kern 5.0pt\sum_{j\in[p]}\alpha_{k,m,j}\neq 0\right\}\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle sample(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left\{m\in[M],\kern 5.0pt\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{j\in[p]}\alpha_{k,m,j}\neq 0\right\}\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle col(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left\{j\in[p],\kern 5.0pt\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{l\in[M]}\alpha_{k,m,j}\neq 0\right\}\kern 5.0pt\ ,

where
col(\alpha)
is the set of features involved in
\alpha
,
sample(\alpha)
is the collection of data-sets involved in
\alpha
.
The following lemma establishes some sufficient conditions for the nullity of the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
. It is based on some independence properties together with Lemma
B.2
.

Lemma D.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}\neq 0
such that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
. Then,
|\alpha|\geq\max\left(2|col(\alpha)|,2|sample(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-2\right)
. In particular, we have
|\alpha|\geq 2
.

The second step, which contains in fact the core of the arguments amounts to controlling the remaining
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
’s.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
\alpha\neq 0\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}
with
|\alpha|\geq 2
. Suppose that
K\geq 2\left|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}\right|^{2}
, then

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq\lambda^{|\alpha|}\left(8|\alpha|^{18}\right)^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{\left|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}\right|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

For
d\in[2,D]
,
v\in[KM]
and
l\in[M]
satisfying
d\geq 2l
and
v\geq l
, there exist at most
\left(vd/2\right)^{d}K^{v-2}m^{l-2}p^{d/2}\leq\left(d\right)^{2d}K^{v-2}M^{l-2}p^{d/2}
matrices
\alpha
with
d=|\alpha|
,
d\geq 2|col(\alpha)|
,
l=|sample(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
and
v=|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}|
. Combining Lemma
D.1
and Lemma
D.2
leads us to

\displaystyle\underset{0<|\alpha|\leq D}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times m\times p}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq

\displaystyle 4\sum_{d=2}^{D}\sum_{v=2}^{d+2}\ \sum_{l=2}^{v\wedge(d/2+1)}D^{2d}K^{v-2}M^{l-2}p^{d/2}\left(8D^{18}\right)^{d+2}\lambda^{2d}\left(\frac{1}{K^{2}}\right)^{v-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{64D^{36}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\sum_{v=2}^{d+2}\sum_{l=2}^{v\wedge(d/2+1)}\left(\lambda^{2}\sqrt{p}8D^{20}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{v-2}M^{l-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{64D^{36}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\sum_{v=2}^{d+2}\sum_{l=2}^{v\wedge(d/2+1)}\left(\lambda^{2}\sqrt{p}8D^{20}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{M}{K}\right)^{l-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{64(D+1)D^{36}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{D}\sum_{l=0}^{d/2-1}\left(\lambda^{2}\sqrt{p}8D^{20}\right)^{d-2l}\left(64D^{40}\lambda^{4}p\frac{M}{K}\right)^{l}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{64(D+1)D^{36}}{K^{2}}\sum_{d=2}^{+\infty}\sqrt{\zeta}^{d}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{64(D+1)D^{36}}{K^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{1-\sqrt{\zeta}}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used the definition (
32
) of
\zeta
in the penultimate line. We have shown (
34
), which concludes the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
D.1

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}\neq 0
such that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
. Let us first prove that
|\alpha|\geq 2|col(\alpha)|
. For that, it is sufficient to prove that, for all
j\in col(\alpha)
,
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\alpha_{k,m,j}\geq 2
.

Let us consider
\alpha
such that there exists
j_{0}\in col(\alpha)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\alpha_{k,m,j_{0}}=1
. The joint distribution of
(x,\left(X_{k,m,j}\right)_{k,m,j\in[K]\times[M]\times[p]})
and
(x,\left(\left(-1\right)^{\mathbf{1}\left\{j=j_{0}\right\}}X_{k,m,j}\right)_{k,m,j\in[K]\times[M]\times[p]})
are the same, so we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{x,\alpha}=

\displaystyle\mathop{cumul}\left(x,(X_{k,m,j})_{k,m,j\in\alpha}\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,(\left(-1\right)^{\mathbf{1}\left\{j=j_{0}\right\}}X_{k,m,j})_{k,m,j\in\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathop{cumul}\left(x,(X_{k,m,j})_{k,m,j\in\alpha}\right)=-\kappa_{x,\alpha}\kern 5.0pt,

which, in turn, implies
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=0
for such an
\alpha
. As a consequence, if
\kappa_{x,\alpha}\neq 0
then
|\alpha|\geq 2|col(\alpha)|
.

Let us now prove that
|\alpha|\geq 2|sample(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-2
. Using Proposition
B.3
, we know that
|\alpha|
is even and that there exists at least a decomposition
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l})
of
\alpha
, with
|\beta_{s}|=2
of the form
\beta_{s}=\left\{(k_{s},m_{s},j_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s},j_{s})\right\}
, such that

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[1,l]}\right)\neq 0\kern 5.0pt.

Let us take the convention
k_{0}=1
,
k^{\prime}_{0}=1
,
m_{0}=1
and
m^{\prime}_{0}=2
. Hence, we get

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\neq 0\kern 5.0pt.

(35)

Let us prove the following claim: for all
m\in\cup_{s\in[0,l]}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
, we have
\sum_{s\in[0,l]}\mathbf{1}\left\{m_{s}=m\right\}+\mathbf{1}\left\{m^{\prime}_{s}=m\right\}\geq 2
. To do so, we suppose that it is not the case and we shall find a contradiction. Let
m\in\cup_{s\in[0,l]}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
with
\sum_{s\in[0,l]}\mathbf{1}\left\{m_{s}=m\right\}+\mathbf{1}\left\{m^{\prime}_{s}=m\right\}=1
. By symmetry, suppose that
m=m_{s_{0}}
. Then,
\pi^{*}\left(k_{s_{0}},m_{s_{0}}\right)
is independent of
\left(\left(\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})\right)_{s\in[0,l]\setminus s_{0}},\pi^{*}\left(k^{\prime}_{s_{0}},m^{\prime}_{s_{0}}\right)\right)
and so
\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s_{0}},m_{s_{0}})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s_{0}},m^{\prime}_{s_{0}})\right\}
is a Bernoulli with parameter
1/K
independent from the random variables
\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]\setminus\left\{s_{0}\right\}}
. Using Lemma
B.2
, we deduce that
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)=0
, which contradicts (
35
). We conclude that, for all
m\in\cup_{s\in[0,l]}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
,
\sum_{s\in[0,l]}\mathbf{1}\left\{m_{s}=m\right\}+\mathbf{1}\left\{m^{\prime}_{s}=m\right\}\geq 2
.

From this claim, we deduce that
2l+2\geq 2|sample(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
which implies
|\alpha|\geq 2|sample(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-2
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
D.1
.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
D.2

Let
\alpha\neq 0\in\mathbb{N}^{K\times M\times p}
. We recall the hypothesis
K\geq 2\left|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}\right|^{2}
. Using Proposition
B.3
, we are reduced to upper-bounding, for any decomposition
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l})
of
\alpha
, with
|\beta_{s}|=2
of the form
\beta_{s}=\left\{(k_{s},m_{s},j_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s},j_{s})\right\}
, the cumulant

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[1,l]}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Let us take the convention
k_{0}=1
,
k^{\prime}_{0}=1
,
m_{0}=1
and
m^{\prime}_{0}=2
. With this notation, we can write

\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[1,l]}\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(36)

Step 1: Reduction.

As explained in Section
3
, we first reduce the control of the cumulant (
36
) to the control of cumulants of products of strongly dependent
\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(k_{s},m_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right)\right\}
. As previously, we introduce a weighted graph to which we will apply Proposition
B.4
. Let
\mathcal{W}
be the weighted graph on
[0,l]
with weights
w_{s,s^{\prime}}
defined by;

•

If
\left\{(k_{s},m_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\cap\left\{(k_{s^{\prime}},m_{s^{\prime}}),(k^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}},m^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})\right\}\neq\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
;

•

If
\left\{(k_{s},m_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\cap\left\{(k_{s^{\prime}},m_{s^{\prime}}),(k^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}},m^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}})\right\}=\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=w\in(0,1)
,

where
w
is to be fixed later.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
the subgraph induced by
S^{(0)}
while only keeping the edges of weight 1, and by

\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
the partition of
S^{(0)}
into the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. In order to apply Proposition
B.4
, we shall upper-bound, for such a
S^{(0)}
, the cumulant

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with respect to the maximum weight
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)=\left(w\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}
. The next lemma (steps 2-4 of Section
3
) provides such an upper-bound.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
and let
\boldsymbol{S}
be its partition into the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. Writing
supp(S^{(0)})=\cup_{s\in S^{(0)}}\left\{(k_{s},m_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}
, we have, whenever
K\geq 2|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{9}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

From Lemma
D.3
, we know that for any
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
with
\boldsymbol{S}
the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq\left(2(l+1)^{9}\right)^{l+1}\left(2l+2\right)^{2(l+1)}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}w^{-l-1}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The function
S\to\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S)|-1}
is super-multiplicative. Hence, applying Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
together with the fact that
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\leq 1
, we get that

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\right|\leq w^{-l-1}\left(8\left(l+1\right)^{17}\right)^{l+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}|-1}.

We also have
l=\frac{|\alpha|}{2}
and so
l+1\leq|\alpha|
. Since the above bound is valid for any
w<1
, by taking
w
arbitrarily close to one, we conclude that

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\right|\leq\left(8|\alpha|^{17}\right)^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{\left|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}\right|-1}.

This bound of the cumulant (
36
) is valid for for any decomposition
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}
of
\alpha
with
\beta_{s}=\left\{(k_{s},m_{s},j_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s},j_{s})\right\}
. Since the number of such decomposition is at most
|\alpha|^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}-1}
, we deduce from Proposition
B.3
that

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq\lambda^{|\alpha|}\left(8|\alpha|^{18}\right)^{\frac{|\alpha|}{2}+1}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{\left|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{(1,1),(1,2)\right\}\right|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
D.2
.

D.3
Proof of Lemma
D.3

Step 2: Expansion.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
and let
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
the connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. We seek to control

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

For any
S\subseteq[0,l]
, we write
supp(S)=\cup_{s\in S}\left\{(k_{s},m_{s}),(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}
. For
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, we write
I_{R}=\cup_{t\in R}supp(S_{t})
. By definition of the graph
\mathcal{W}
, the sets
supp(S_{t})
are disjoint and form a partition of
supp(S^{(0)})
. Moreover, for all
(k,m^{\prime}),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\in supp(S_{t})
, there exists a path
s_{1},\ldots,s_{h}\subseteq S_{t}
such that
(k,m^{\prime})\in\left\{(k_{s_{1}},m_{s_{1}}),(k^{\prime}_{s_{1}},m^{\prime}_{s_{1}})\right\}
and
(k^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\in\left\{(k_{s_{h}},m_{s_{h}}),(k^{\prime}_{s_{h}},m^{\prime}_{s_{h}})\right\}
and such that
\mathcal{W}
has an edge of weight
1
between all
s_{h^{\prime}},s_{h^{\prime}+1}
. Thus, for
t\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
,
\prod_{s\in S_{t}}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}
can be written

\prod_{s\in S_{t}}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k_{s},m_{s})=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime}_{s},m^{\prime}_{s})\right\}=\sum_{a\in[K]}\prod_{(k,m)\in supp(S_{t})}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k,m)=a\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, we are interested in configurations
\pi^{*}
that are constant on each
S_{t}
for
t=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|
. For that purpose, we consider
\pi=(\pi(S_{t}))_{t=1,\ldots,|\boldsymbol{S}|}\in[K]^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
. Then, using the multilinearity of cumulants, we get

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}=\sum_{\pi=\left(\pi(S)\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}}\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{(k,m)\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi(S)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(37)

Let us fix such a
\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}
, and for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, let us define

Z_{\pi,S}=\prod_{(k,m)\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi(S)\right\}.

In the following, we shall control

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi):=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{(k,m)\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi(S)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right).

Step 3: Pruning incompatible configurations.

Note that
k\to\pi(k,m)
defined by
\pi(k,m)=\pi(S)
for
(k,m)\in supp(S)
may not be injective, in which case it is impossible to have all the
Z_{\pi,S}
non zero for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
. To cope with such cases,
we shall apply Proposition
B.4
to a weighted dependency graph
\mathcal{V}
on
|\boldsymbol{S}|
defined by

•

an edge of weight
1
between
t
and
t^{\prime}
if and only if
\pi(S_{t})=\pi(S_{t^{\prime}})
and
\cup_{s\in S_{t}}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}\cap\cup_{s\in S_{t^{\prime}}}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}\neq\emptyset
,

•

an edge of weight
\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{K}
otherwise.

We highlight the fact that, although this is not explicit,
\mathcal{V}
actually depends on the choice of
\pi=\left(\pi(S)\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}
.
Weights 1 are attributed to connected components having the same value
\pi(S)
and sharing some common datasets.

Consider the case where
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
is such that
\mathcal{V}
has an edge of weight
1
between two nodes of
R
. Then, writing
t
and
t^{\prime}
two such nodes that are connected in
\mathcal{V}
be an edge of weight
1
, we observe that this implies that
\pi(S_{t})=\pi(S_{t^{\prime}})
and there exists
m\in[M]
such that
m
belongs to both
\cup_{s\in S_{t}}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
and
\cup_{s\in S_{t^{\prime}}}\left\{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
. Hence, since
S_{t}
and
S_{t^{\prime}}
are disjoint, having
Z_{\pi,S_{t}}=1
and
Z_{\pi,S_{t^{\prime}}}=1
implies that there exists
k\neq k^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi^{*}(k^{\prime},m)
, the latter being impossible since
\pi^{*}(.,m)
is a permutation of
[K]
. As a consequence,
Z_{\pi,S_{t}}Z_{\pi,S_{t^{\prime}}}=0
a.s. Denoting
\boldsymbol{R}=R_{1},\ldots,R_{|\boldsymbol{R}|}
the connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
, we deduce from Lemma
B.2
that

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{t\in R_{h}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{h\in[|\boldsymbol{R}|]}\right)=0\kern 5.0pt.

(38)

On the other hand, if
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
is such that
\mathcal{V}
has no edges of weight
1
between nodes in
R
, the partition
\boldsymbol{R}
of
R
induced by the connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
is made of singletons, and can be identified to
R
itself. Thus, in order to apply Proposition
B.4
, we only have to control the cumulants

C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi):=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

for all subsets
R
of
[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have any edge of weight
1
. In other words, we only have to control the cumulants for configurations
(\pi(S))_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}
that are possibly compatible with
\pi^{*}
.

Step 4: Leveraging Lemma
3.1
.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not contain any edge of weight
1
. If
K\geq 2|I_{R}|^{2}
, with
I_{R}=\cup_{t\in R}supp(S_{t})
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)\right|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|I_{R}|}\left(\frac{|I_{R}|^{2}}{K}\right)^{|R|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

Let
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have any edge of weight
1
. For
m\in[M]
, we denote
A_{m}
the set
\left\{(k,m),k\in[K]\right\}
. Let
\Delta\subseteq R
,
I_{\Delta}=\cup_{t\in\Delta}supp(S_{t})
, and let us compute

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right]=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\forall t\in\Delta,\forall(k,m)\in supp(S_{t}),\pi^{*}(k,m)=\pi(S_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{K(K-1)\times\ldots\times\left(K-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{m}|-1\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\frac{1}{\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{m}|-1\right)\frac{1}{K}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

(39)

We are therefore in position to apply Lemma
3.1
with
\eta=\frac{1}{K}
and
x_{0}=\frac{1}{K}
. Hence, we deduce from (
10
) that, whenever
K\geq 2|I_{R}|^{2}
, we have

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}[R]}(\pi)\right|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|I_{R}|}\left(\frac{|I_{R}|^{2}}{K}\right)^{|R|-1}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The function
R\mapsto\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|I_{R}|}
is super-multiplicative. Combining Lemma
D.4
with Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
, we end up with

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\right|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Conclusion.

For
\pi=\left(\pi(S)\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}
, we write
cc(\pi)
the number of connected components of the induced graph
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
(we recall that the graph
\mathcal{V}
depends on the choice of
\pi
). In light of the definition of
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)
, we arrive at

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\right|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{K}\right)^{cc(\pi)-1}\kern 5.0pt,

for any
\pi
.
Plugging this inequality in (
37
) leads us to

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\underset{cc(\pi)=cc}{\sum_{\pi\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}}}\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{K}\right)^{cc-1}\kern 5.0pt.

The following lemma bounds the number of configurations
\pi
with a given number of
cc(\pi)
.

Lemma D.5
.

For any
cc\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, we have

\left|\left\{\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}},\kern 5.0ptcc(\pi)=cc\right\}\right|\leq(2|\boldsymbol{S}|)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}K^{cc}\kern 5.0pt.

Putting everything together, we conclude that

\displaystyle|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|\leq

\displaystyle 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\underset{cc(\pi)=cc}{\sum_{\pi\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}}}\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{K}\right)^{cc-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{9}\right)^{\boldsymbol{S}}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2cc-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{9}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{K}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of Lemma
D.3
is complete

D.4
Proof of Lemma
D.5

Let us fix
cc\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, and let us count the number of
\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}
with
cc(\pi)=cc
. To do so, we fix a permutation
\psi\in\mathcal{S}([|\boldsymbol{S}|])
of
[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
and we fix
\mathcal{G}
a subgraph of the set of edges
\left\{(\psi(1),\psi(2)),(\psi(2),\psi(3)),\ldots,(\psi(|\boldsymbol{S}|-1),\psi(|\boldsymbol{S}|))\right\}
having exactly
cc
connected components. We first seek to upper-bound the number of
\pi
’s such that the induced graph
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
has the same connected components as
\mathcal{G}
. In order to highlight the dependency of
\mathcal{V}
with respect to
\pi
, we now write
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
for
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
. If
\mathcal{G}
has an edge between
t
,
t^{\prime}\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, then we must choose
\pi
satisfying
\pi_{S_{t}}=\pi_{S_{t}^{\prime}}
. So, for
R
a connected component of
\mathcal{G}
, we must have
\pi_{S_{t}}=\pi_{S_{t^{\prime}}}
for all
t,t^{\prime}\in R
. Hence, there are at most
K^{cc}
possibilities for choosing
\pi
such that
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
has the same connected components as
\mathcal{G}
.

In total, the number of
\pi\in[K]^{\boldsymbol{S}}
such that
cc(\pi)=cc
is at most
(2|\boldsymbol{S}|)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}K^{cc}
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
D.5
.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Without loss of generality, we assume through the proof that
\sigma^{2}=1
. Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n\geq 2(2D+2)^{2}
. We suppose

\zeta:=2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\max\left(1,\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n}\right)<1\kern 5.0pt.

Denote
x=\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(1)-\pi^{*}(2)|\leq\rho\right\}
. Since the minimization problem defining
MMSE_{\leq D}
in Equation (
16
) is separable, and since the random variables
X_{ij}
are jointly exchangeable, the
MMSE_{\leq D}
can be reduced to

MMSE_{\leq D}=\lambda^{2}\frac{n-1}{n}\inf_{f\in\mathbb{R}_{D}[Y]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(f(Y)-x\right)^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

We can compute
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right]=\frac{2\rho}{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\rho+1}{2n}\right)
.
The model considered is a particular case of the Gaussian Additive model analyzed by
[
SW22
]
and hence, we can use Equation (
2
)

\frac{n}{n-1}MMSE_{\leq D}\geq\lambda^{2}\frac{2\rho}{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\rho+1}{2n}\right)-\frac{4\lambda^{2}\rho^{2}}{(n-1)^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\rho+1}{2n}\right)^{2}-\lambda^{2}\underset{D\geq|\alpha|>0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}
is the cumulant
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}\right)
, where
\left(X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}

is the multiset that contains
\alpha_{ij}
copies of
X_{ij}
, for
i,j\in[1,n]
. In light of Equation (
E
), it is sufficient, for proving Theorem
4.1
, to establish that

\underset{D\geq|\alpha|>0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq 2^{18}\frac{\left(D+1\right)^{44}\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\kern 5.0pt.

In the proof of this theorem, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}
, we denote
supp(\alpha)=\left\{i\in[n],\kern 5.0pt\alpha_{i:}\neq 0\right\}\bigcup\left\{i\in[n],\kern 5.0pt\alpha_{:i}\neq 0\right\}
. Let us define a multigraph
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
on
[n]
with
\alpha_{ij}
edges between the nodes
i
and
j
, for
i,j\in[n]
. We define
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
the graph obtained after removing the isolated notes (except
1
and
2
) and we denote
cc(\alpha)
the number of connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(1,2)\right\}
, which is the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
where we have added an edge between
1
and
2
.

The following lemma states the desired control of the cumulant.

Lemma E.1
.

For any
\alpha\neq 0
with
n\geq 2\left(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|\right)^{2}
, we have

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq\lambda^{|\alpha|}2^{5|\alpha|+7}\left(|\alpha|+1\right)^{20|\alpha|+21}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{cc(\alpha)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Before showing this lemma, we first conclude the proof of the theorem.

For
\alpha\neq 0
, the number of edges of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(1,2)\right\}
is
|\alpha|+1
, and the number of nodes is
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|
. Since in
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}
, no node except possibly
1
and
2
are isolated, one deduces from these definitions that

|\alpha|\geq\max\left(|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-cc(\alpha)-1,\frac{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-2}{2}\right)\ .

Moreover, the nodes
1
and
2
belong to the same connected component of
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{-}\cup\left\{(1,2)\right\}
, so we also have

|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|\geq cc(\alpha)+1\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, given
d,cc\geq 1
and
m\geq cc+1
, with
d\geq\max\left(m-cc-1,\frac{m-2}{2}\right)
, there are at most
n^{m-2}m^{2d}\leq n^{m-2}(2d+2)^{2d}
matrices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}
satisfying
|\alpha|=d
,
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|=m
and
cc(\alpha)=cc
. Recall that we assume that
n\geq 2\left(2D+2\right)^{2}
. Combining the last bound with Lemma
E.1
leads us to

\displaystyle\underset{D\geq|\alpha|>0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\hskip-28.45274pt\underset{D\geq d\geq\max\left(m-cc-1,\frac{m-2}{2}\right)}{\underset{m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{cc\geq 1}}}\hskip-28.45274pt\left(2D+2\right)^{2d}\lambda^{2d}\left(2^{10}\left(D+1\right)^{40}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{m-1}\left(\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}\right)^{cc-1}n^{m-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{16}\frac{\left(D+1\right)^{42}\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\underset{D\geq d\geq\max\left(m-cc-1,\frac{m-2}{2}\right)}{\underset{m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{cc\geq 1}}}\left(2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n}\right)^{m-2}\left(\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}\right)^{cc-1}.

Let us fix
d,cc\geq 1
and
m\geq cc+1
, with
d\geq\max\left(m-cc-1,\frac{m-2}{2}\right)
, and let us upper-bound the term
\Upsilon:=\left(2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n}\right)^{m-2}\left(\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}\right)^{cc-1}
. Using the hypothesis
d\geq m-cc-1
, we get

\displaystyle\Upsilon\leq

\displaystyle\left(2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\right)^{d-m+cc+1}\left(\frac{4\rho^{2}}{n}2^{12}\lambda^{2}\left(D+1\right)^{42}\right)^{m-cc-1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{cc-1}\leq\max(\zeta,1/n)^{d}\kern 5.0pt

by definition of
\zeta
.
We end up with

\displaystyle\underset{D\geq|\alpha|>0}{\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}}}\frac{\kappa_{x,\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha!}\leq

\displaystyle 2^{16}\frac{\left(D+1\right)^{42}\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\underset{D\geq d\geq\max\left(m-cc-1,\frac{m-2}{2}\right)}{\underset{m\geq cc+1}{\sum_{cc\geq 1}}}\max(\zeta,1/n)^{d}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{16}\frac{\left(D+1\right)^{42}\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\left(2D+1\right)^{2}\sum_{d=1}^{D}\max(\zeta,1/n)^{d}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{18}\frac{\left(D+1\right)^{44}\rho^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\max(\zeta,1/n)}{1-\max(\zeta,1/n)}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

E.1
Proof of Lemma
E.1

Let
\alpha\neq 0\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times n}
and let us upper-bound the absolute value of
\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}\left(x,\left(X_{ij}\right)_{ij\in\alpha}\right)
.
We recall that
X_{ij}=\lambda\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(i)-\pi^{*}(j)|\leq\rho\right\}
.
From the multilinearity of cumulants, we know that
\kappa_{x,\alpha}
is proportional to
\lambda^{|\alpha|}
. Without loss of generality, we suppose in the following that
\lambda=1
. Let
l=|\alpha|
and let
(i_{s},j_{s})_{s\in[l]}
such that
\alpha=\left\{(i_{s},j_{s}),s\in[l]\right\}
. We use the convention
i_{0}=1
and
j_{0}=2
. With those notation,
supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}=\cup_{s\in[0,l]}\left\{i_{s},j_{s}\right\}
, and we have

\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(i_{s})-\pi^{*}(j_{s})|\leq\rho\right\}\right)_{s\in[0,l]}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(40)

This cumulant can be controlled using the general approach described in Section
3
.

Step 1: Reduction.

As explained in Section
3
, we first reduce the control of the cumulant (
40
) to the control of cumulants of products of strongly dependent
\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}\left(i_{s}\right)=\pi^{*}\left(j_{s}\right)\right\}
. We introduce a weighted graph to which we shall apply Proposition
B.4
. Let
\mathcal{W}
be the weighted graph on
[0,l]
with weights
w_{s,s^{\prime}}
defined by:

•

if
\left\{i_{s},j_{s}\right\}\cap\left\{i_{s^{\prime}},j_{s^{\prime}}\right\}\neq\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=1
,

•

if
\left\{i_{s},j_{s}\right\}\cap\left\{i_{s^{\prime}},j_{s^{\prime}}\right\}=\emptyset
, then
w_{s,s^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2\rho}
.

For any subset
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
, we denote by
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
the subgraph of
\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]
, where we only keep the edges with weight one, and we
denote by
\boldsymbol{S}
the partition of
S^{(0)}
into connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. In order to apply Proposition
B.4
, we have to bound the cumulant

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}:=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(i_{s})-\pi^{*}(j_{s})|\leq\rho\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Such a control is provided in the next lemma (steps 2-4 of Section
3
), which is proved in Section
E.2
. For any
S\subseteq[0,l]
, we write
supp(S)=\cup_{s\in S}\left\{i_{s},j_{s}\right\}
. In particular, we have
supp(\alpha)=supp([1,l])
and
supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}=supp([0,l])
.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
and suppose
n\geq 2\left(|supp(S^{(0)})|\right)^{2}
. Let
\boldsymbol{S}
be the set of connected components of
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. Then,

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|\leq 4|\boldsymbol{S}|\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{10}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{4}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right)=\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}
,
Lemma
E.2
implies that, for all
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
,

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}\right|\leq 4\left(l+1\right)\left(2(l+1)^{10}|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|^{4}\right)^{l+1}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}[S^{(0)}]\right).

The function
S^{(0)}\mapsto\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}
being super-multiplicative,
applying Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
, with the weighted dependency graph
\mathcal{W}
, we get that, since
l=|\alpha|
and
|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|\leq 2(|\alpha|+1)
,

|\kappa_{x,\alpha}|\leq 4\left(|\alpha|+1\right)\left(2^{5}(|\alpha|+1)^{20}\right)^{|\alpha|+1}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(\alpha)\cup\left\{1,2\right\}|-1}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of Lemma
E.1
is complete since
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{cc(\alpha)-1}
.

E.2
Proof of Lemma
E.2

Step 2: Expansion.

Let
S^{(0)}\subseteq[0,l]
and let
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
the connected components
\mathcal{W}\langle 1\rangle[S^{(0)}]
. Recall that we aim at bounding in absolute value the following cumulant

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(i_{s})-\pi^{*}(j_{s})|\leq\rho\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

For
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, we denote
\mathcal{A}_{S}
the set of all injections
\pi_{S}:supp(S)\to[n]
such that, for all
s\in S
,
|\pi_{S}(i_{s})-\pi_{S}(j_{s})|\leq\rho
. We have, for
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
,

\prod_{s\in S}\mathbf{1}\left\{|\pi^{*}(i_{s})-\pi^{*}(j_{s})|\leq\rho\right\}=\sum_{\pi_{S}\in\mathcal{A}_{S}}\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi_{S}(i)\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

By the multilinearity of cumulants, we deduce

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}=\sum_{\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}}\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi_{S}(i)\right\}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(41)

Let us fix
\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}
. For
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
and
i\in supp(S)
, we write
\pi(i)=\pi_{S}(i)
. For
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
, let us write
Z_{\pi,S}=\prod_{i\in supp(S)}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}
. In light of (
41
), we need to control the cumulants

C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi):=\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Note that the corresponding function
\pi
is possibly non-injective, in which case it is impossible that all
Z_{\pi,S}
with
S\in\boldsymbol{S}
are simultaneously non-zero. Indeed, if there exists
i\in S
and
i^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
such that
\pi(i)=\pi(i^{\prime})
, it is impossible to have
\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)
and
\pi^{*}(i^{\prime})=\pi(i^{\prime})
since
\pi^{*}
is permutation of
[n]
.

Step 3: Pruning incompatible configurations.

We recall that we write
\boldsymbol{S}=S_{1},\ldots,S_{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
. We shall apply Proposition
B.4
to some weighted graph
\mathcal{V}
with vertex set
[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
with:

•

an edge of weight
1
between
t,t^{\prime}\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
if and only if
\left\{\pi(i),i\in supp(S_{t})\right\}\cap\left\{\pi(i),i\in supp(S_{t^{\prime}})\right\}\neq\emptyset
,

•

an edge of weight
\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}
between
t
and
t^{\prime}
otherwise.

As argued above, if the graph
\mathcal{V}
contains at least an edge with weight
1
, then
\pi^{*}
cannot be compatible with
\pi
. The graph
\mathcal{V}
depends on
\pi
but we do not write explicitly this dependency for simplicity of notation. If
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
is such that
\mathcal{V}
has an edge of weight
1
between two nodes of
R
, denote
\boldsymbol{R}=R_{1},\ldots,R_{|\boldsymbol{R}|}
the partition of
R
into connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
. If such a connected component
R_{h}
contains at least two nodes, this implies that
\prod_{t\in R_{h}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}=0
a.s. since
\prod_{t\in R_{h}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\neq 0
would imply that, for some
i\neq i^{\prime}
,
\pi^{*}(i)=\pi^{*}(i^{\prime})
. It then follows from Lemma
B.2
that

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{t\in R_{h}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{h\in[|\boldsymbol{R}|]}\right)=0\kern 5.0pt.

(42)

On the other hand, if
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
is such that
\mathcal{V}
has no edges of weight
1
between nodes in
R
, the partition
\boldsymbol{R}
of
R
induced by the connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
is made of singletons, and can be identified to
R
itself. Thus, in order to apply Proposition
B.4
, we only have to control the cumulant
\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)\right|
for all subsets
R
of
[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have any edge of weight
1
.

Step 4: Leveraging Lemma
3.1
.

For
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, let us write
I_{R}=\cup_{t\in R}supp(S_{t})
.

Lemma E.3
.

Let
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have any edge of weight
1
. If
n\geq 2|I_{R}|^{2}
,

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)\right|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{R}|}\left(\frac{|I_{R}|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|R|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of Lemma
E.3
.

Let
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
such that
\mathcal{V}[R]
does not have any edge of weight
1
. We recall that this corresponds to the case where the event such that
\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)
for all
i\in S_{t}
with
t\in R
holds with positive probability.
Equivalently, this implies that
\pi
is an injection on
I_{R}
. We seek to upper-bound
\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)\right|
, where, for
t\in R
,
Z_{\pi,S_{t}}=\prod_{i\in supp(S_{t})}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right\}
. Let
\Delta\subseteq R
, and let us compute, writing
I_{\Delta}=\cup_{t\in\Delta}supp(S_{t})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right]=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left[\forall t\in\Delta,\kern 5.0pt\forall i\in supp(S_{t}),\kern 5.0pt\pi^{*}(i)=\pi(i)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n(n-1)\times\ldots\times(n-|I_{\Delta}|+1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{\Delta}|}\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{n}}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-\frac{|I_{\Delta}|-1}{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

(43)

We are in position to apply the general bound for cumulants with weakly dependant random variables from Lemma
3.1
. Indeed, applying Equation (
10
) with
x_{0}=\eta=\frac{1}{n}
, leads us to

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\right)\right|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{R}|}\left(\frac{|I_{R}|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|R|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

whenever
n\geq 2|I_{R}|^{2}
.

∎

For all
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
, denoting
\boldsymbol{R}=R_{1},\ldots,R_{|\boldsymbol{R}|}
the connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle[R]
, we have

\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}[R]\right)=\left(\frac{|I_{R}|^{2}}{n}\right)^{|R|-1}
.
Hence, when
n\geq 2\left(supp(S^{(0)})\right)^{2}
, the identity (
42
) and Lemma
E.3
imply that,

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\prod_{t\in R_{h}}Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{h\in[|\boldsymbol{R}|]}\right)\right|\leq 2|R|^{2|R|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{R}|}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}[R]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Since the function
R\to\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|I_{R}|}
is super-multiplicative, we have all the ingredients to apply Proposition
B.4
and Lemma
B.5
, leading to

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\right|=\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{\pi,S_{t}}\right)_{t\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]}\right)\right|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Conclusion.

For
\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}
, we denote
cc(\pi)
the number of connected components of
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
so that
\mathbb{M}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)=(|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}/n)^{cc(\pi)-1}
. Hence, we have

\left|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\pi)\right|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc(\pi)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging this inequality in (
41
) leads us to

|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|\leq 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left|\left\{\pi,\kern 5.0ptcc(\pi)=cc\right\}\right|\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc-1}\kern 5.0pt.

(44)

Let us fix
cc\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
and let us count the number of
\pi
’s with
cc(\pi)=cc
.

Lemma E.4
.

For any
cc\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
,

\left|\left\{\pi\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S},\kern 5.0ptcc(\pi)=cc\right\}\right|\leq(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2})^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}n^{cc}\left(2\rho\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-|\boldsymbol{S}|}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining Lemma
E.4
and Eq. (
44
) leads us to

\displaystyle|C_{\boldsymbol{S}}|\leq

\displaystyle 2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{8|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2})^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}n^{cc}\left(2\rho\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}}{n}\right)^{cc-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2n\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{10}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|}\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\sum_{cc=1}^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2(cc-1)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4|\boldsymbol{S}|\left(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{10}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{4}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(\frac{2\rho}{n}\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-1}\left(\frac{1}{2\rho}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|-1}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
E.2
.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
E.4

Let us fix
cc\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
and let us count the number of
\pi=\left(\pi_{S}\right)_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}
’s with
cc(\pi)=cc
. To do so, we fix a permutation
\psi\in\mathcal{S}([|\boldsymbol{S}|])
of
|\boldsymbol{S}|
and we fix
\mathcal{G}
a subgraph of the set of edges
\left\{(\psi(1),\psi(2)),(\psi(2),\psi(3)),\ldots,(\psi(|\boldsymbol{S}|-1),\psi(|\boldsymbol{S}|))\right\}
having exactly
cc
connected components. There are less than
|\boldsymbol{S}|!2^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\leq(2|\boldsymbol{S}|)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}
possible choices for
\mathcal{G}
and
\psi
. We first seek to upper-bound the number of
\pi
’s such that the induced weighted graph
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
with vertex set
[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
has the same connected components as
\mathcal{G}
. We recall that
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
has an edge of weight
1
between
t,t^{\prime}\in[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
if and only if there exists
i\in S_{t}
and
i^{\prime}\in S_{t^{\prime}}
with
\pi(i)=\pi(i^{\prime})
. In the following, we write
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
for the graph
\mathcal{V}\langle 1\rangle
in order to emphasize the dependency with respect to
\pi
. Let us upper-bound the number of
\pi\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
and
\mathcal{G}
have the same connected components. Without loss of generality, we suppose in the following that
\psi
is the identity; in particular, the connected components of
\mathcal{G}
are intervals.

Let us fix
R\subseteq[|\boldsymbol{S}|]
a connected component of
\mathcal{G}
and we upper-bound the number of choices for
\pi^{(R)}=\left(\pi_{S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}\in\prod_{t\in R}\mathcal{A}_{S_{t}}
connecting
R
, i.e such that the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\pi^{(R)}}
with vertex set
R
is connected. By symmetry, we can suppose that
R=[|R|]
. Let us choose a permutation
\phi
of
[|R|]
and let us count the number of
\pi^{(R)}
satisfying; for all
t\in[2,|R|]
,
\mathcal{G}_{\pi^{(R)}}
has an edge between
\phi(t)
and
\left\{\phi(t^{\prime}),t^{\prime}\leq t-1\right\}
. Without loss of generality, we now suppose that the chosen and fixed permutation
\phi
is the identity. Let us choose for
t\in[2,|R|]
, an element
i^{t}_{1}\in supp(S_{t})
and let us count the number of
\pi^{(R)}=\left(\pi_{S_{t}}\right)_{t\in R}
such that
\pi_{S_{t}}(i^{t})
is in
\left(\cup_{t^{\prime}\leq t-1}\cup_{i\in S_{t^{\prime}}}\pi_{S_{t^{\prime}}}(i)\right)
. There are at most
\prod_{t\in[|R|]}|supp(S_{t})|
possibilities for choosing the
i^{t}_{1}
’s. For
t\leq[|R|]
, let us order
S_{t}=\left\{s_{1}^{t},\ldots,s^{t}_{|S_{t}|}\right\}
, with
i^{t}_{1}\in\left\{i_{s_{1}^{t}},j_{s_{1}^{t}}\right\}
, in such a way that, for all
j\in[2,|S_{t}|]
,
\left\{i_{s_{j}^{t}},j_{s_{j}^{t}}\right\}
intersects
\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\leq j-1}\left\{i_{s_{j^{\prime}}^{t}},j_{s_{j^{\prime}}^{t}}\right\}
. Then:

•

For
S_{1}
, we choose a value in
[n]
for
\pi_{S_{1}}(i_{s_{1}^{1}})
. That makes
n
possibilities. Then, choosing the other labels for the elements
supp({S_{1}})
in the order of appearance in the couples indexed by
\left\{s_{1}^{1},\ldots,s^{1}_{|S_{1}|}\right\}
, we have at each time only
2\rho
possibilities (each label must be at distance at most
\rho
from the already chosen label of the point which is in the same
\left\{i_{s_{j}^{1}},j_{s_{j}^{1}}\right\}
for some
j\leq|S_{1}|
). That makes at most
n(2\rho)^{|supp(S_{1})|-1}
possibilities.

•

For the other
S_{t}
’s, the same thing occurs except that the label of the first element
i^{t}_{1}
must be in the labels already chosen previously. That makes at most
|I_{R}|\left(2\rho\right)^{|supp\left(S_{t}\right)|-1}
possibilities, where we recall that
I_{R}=\cup_{t\leq|R|}supp(S_{t})
.

In total, counting the number of possibilities for choosing the
i^{t}_{1}
’s and for choosing the permutation
\phi
, we have
|R|^{|R|}n\left(2\rho\right)^{|I_{R}|-|R|}|I_{R}|^{|R|-1}\prod_{t\in R}|supp(S_{t})|
possibilities. Doing so for all the connected components of
\mathcal{G}
, the number of
\pi
’s such that
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
has the same connected components as
\mathcal{G}
is at most

n^{cc}\left(2\rho\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-|\boldsymbol{S}|}\left(|\boldsymbol{S}||supp(S^{(0)})|^{2}\right)^{|\boldsymbol{S}|}\kern 5.0pt.

Multiplying for the number of choices for the graph
G
and for the permutation
\psi
, we deduce that the number of
\pi\in\prod_{S\in\boldsymbol{S}}\mathcal{A}_{S}
with
cc(\pi)=cc
is at most

(2|\boldsymbol{S}|^{2}|supp(S^{(0)})|^{2})^{\boldsymbol{S}}n^{cc}\left(2\rho\right)^{|supp(S^{(0)})|-|\boldsymbol{S}|}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.

Appendix F
Proof of Lemma
3.1

This section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
3.1
. We first treat the first point of the lemma (case
r\geq 1
), postponing to Section
F.9
the proof for the particular case
r=0
. Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
have mixed moments given by (
8
): for all
\Delta\subseteq R

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{t}\right]=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x_{0}}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x_{0}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y_{0})\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y_{0}\right).\

The random variables
\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[\ell]}
have their moments that are close to be factorized. If instead of (
8
), we had considered random variables
\left(\tilde{Z}_{t}\right)_{t\in[\ell]}
, such that, for any
\Delta\subseteq R
, we had

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}\tilde{Z}_{t}\right]=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\left[\frac{1}{1-x_{0}}\right]^{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y_{0})^{|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|}\kern 5.0pt,\

then, we would have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}\tilde{Z}_{t}\right]=\prod_{t\in\Delta}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\tilde{Z}_{t}\right]
. Since the mixed moments of
\tilde{Z}_{t}
are the same as if they were independent, it then follows from Lemmas
B.2
and
B.1
that
\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(\tilde{Z}_{t}\right)_{t\in[\ell]}\right)=0
. The purpose of Lemma
3.1
is to quantify to what extent the fact that the moments of the
Z_{t}
’s in (
8
) are close to be factorized implies that their joint cumulant is small.

F.1
Small-cumulant and quasi-factorization

A key result of Féray
[
Fer18
]
is Proposition 5.8 (in
[
Fer18
]
), that we recall here informally. Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
be random variables, with a distribution depending on some parameter
n
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be a weighted graph with vertex set
[\ell]
. If for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least 2 we have the quasi-factorisation

\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\delta}Z_{t}\right]^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}=1+O_{n}(\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta])),

(45)

then, we have the small cumulant property for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least 2

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\in\Delta}\right)=\prod_{t\in\Delta}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[Z_{t}\right]\times O_{n}(\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta])),

(46)

where
O_{n}(\cdot)
refers to the parameter
n
, and
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta])
is the weight of the graph
\mathcal{L}
restricted to the node set
\Delta
, see Section
B.3
for the definition of
\mathbb{M}
.

To prove Lemma
3.1
, we provide a non-asymptotic and quantitative version of this result.
Instead of considering moments
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\delta}Z_{t}\right]
as real numbers, our approach is to consider them as bivariates series in
(x_{0},y_{0})
. In this direction,
for any subset
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, we introduce the multivariate series

\displaystyle u_{\Delta}(x,y):=

\displaystyle\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right),

(47)

with the convention that
u_{\emptyset}=1
. We have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{t}\right]=u_{\Delta}(x_{0},y_{0})
, and

u_{\Delta}(x,y)
is well-defined when
x<\frac{1}{L}
, where
L=|I_{[\ell]}|
.

Having in mind the Möbius formula (Lemma
B.1
) for cumulants, we define, for
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, the bivariate series

\kappa_{\Delta}(x,y):=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{P}[\Delta]}m(G)\prod_{\delta\in G}u_{\delta}(x,y)\kern 5.0pt,

(48)

so that

\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[\ell]}\right)=\kappa_{[\ell]}(x_{0},y_{0})\kern 5.0pt.

(49)

To derive a non asymptotic upper bound on
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x_{0},y_{0})
, we will first control the so-called
order
(defined in Section
F.2
) of the bivariate series
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)
, by building on ideas from
[
Fer18
]
. Then, this will allow us to work around the non-asymptotic bound by carefully bounding the terms. The counterpart of the
quasi-factorisation property (
45
) in our framework, will be some lower bound on the order of the following bivariate rational function
P_{\Delta}[u]
.

Definition 6
.

Consider any family
(u_{\Delta}(x,y))
with
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of bivariate power series with an open domain of convergence.
For
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
non empty, we define the multivariate rational function

P_{\Delta}[u](x,y):=\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}\left(u_{\delta}(x,y)\right)^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}\kern 5.0pt.

(50)

A key property of the rational function
P_{\Delta}[v](x,y)
is that when the power series
(v_{\Delta}(x,y))_{\Delta\subset[\ell]}
can be factorized, as if they were mixed moments of variables with some independence, then
P_{\Delta}[v](x,y)=1
.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
and
v=(v_{\Delta}(x,y))_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}
be a family of power series indexed by subsets of
[\ell]
. For some
(x,y)
in the domain, we suppose that for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
,
v_{\Delta}(x,y)\neq 0
. Let
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, and let us suppose that there exist
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
forming a partition of
\Delta
such that, for all
\delta\subseteq\Delta
, one has
v_{\delta}(x,y)=v_{\delta\cap I_{1}}(x,y)v_{\delta\cap I_{2}}(x,y)
. Then,
P_{\Delta}[v](x,y)=1
.

In the two following subsection, we provide a counterpart of the small cumulant property (
46
), by proving that
the order of
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)
is at least that of
P_{[\ell]}[u](x,y)-1
. Then, we will lower bound the latter for
u
defined in (
47
).

F.2
Order of a power series

For our purpose, we introduce a specific notion of
order
of a power series, which slightly differs from that in the literature.
Let us first introduce a partial order relation on
\mathbb{N}^{2}
.

Definition 7
.

Let
s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}\geq 0
and
s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1}\geq 0
. We write that
(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})\succcurlyeq(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})
if and only if
s_{1}\geq s_{0}
and
s_{1}+s^{\prime}_{1}\geq s_{0}+s^{\prime}_{0}
. The binary relation
\succcurlyeq
is an order relation.

Lemma F.2
.

Any subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{2}
admits a unique infimum for the order relation
\succcurlyeq
. This infimum is not necessarily in
S
.

We are now in position to define the
order
of a multivariate series.

Definition 8
.

Let
f(x,y)=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\geq 0}f_{s,s^{\prime}}x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}
a multivariate series with positive radius. Let
\mathcal{D}(f)=\left\{(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\kern 5.0ptf_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0\right\}
. We define the
order
of
f
as the infimum of
\mathcal{D}(f)
with respect to the partial order
\succcurlyeq
. It is referred as
\mathrm{ord}(f):=\inf\mathcal{D}(f)
.

Our objective in this subsection is to control
\mathrm{ord}\left(\kappa_{\Delta}\right)
for the power series
\kappa_{\Delta}(x,y)
defined by (
48
).
For that purpose, we introduce a notion of
polynomial graph
, which are weighed graphs whose edges have weights
x
,
y
or
1
. Consider such a polynomial graph
\mathcal{L}
with edges
w_{e}\in\left\{x,y,1\right\}
. Consider, if it exists (i.e if
\mathcal{L}
is connected), a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{L}
. We define the weight of
\mathcal{T}
as the monomial in
x
and
y
defined by
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}}w_{e}
, and we write
deg(\mathcal{T})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
the
degree
of this monomial, i.e. if
\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}}w_{e}=x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}
, then
deg(\mathcal{T}):=(s,s^{\prime})
.

Definition 9
.

For
\mathcal{L}
a polynomial graph, we define the
order
of
\mathcal{L}
, and we write
\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})
the infimum (w.r.t.
\succcurlyeq
) of
deg(\mathcal{T})
over all spanning trees
\mathcal{T}
, with the convention that
\inf\emptyset=(+\infty,+\infty)
.

It turns out that the order of
\mathcal{L}
is achieved by a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
, its there exists any.

Lemma F.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a connected polynomial graph. There exists a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})=deg(\mathcal{T})
.

In the following lemma, we adapt a result from
[
Fer18
]
to the setup of multivariate series, which enables us to lower-bound the
order
of a polynomial graph
\mathcal{L}
by the
order
of well-chosen sub-graphs. Given two partition
\boldsymbol{V}
and
\boldsymbol{W}
of a set
V
, we write
\mathbf{W}\vee\mathbf{V}
as the finest partition which is coarser than
\boldsymbol{W}
and coarser than
\boldsymbol{W}
. In other words, this partition is obtained by merging the groups of
\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
whenever they intersect.

Given
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
a family of subsets of
[\ell]
, we write, for
i\in[m]
,
\Pi\left(\Delta_{i}\right)
the partition of
[\ell]
made of
\Delta_{i}
and singletons. The partition
\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})
is defined as previously as the finest partition with is coarser than all
\Pi(\Delta_{i})
.

Lemma F.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(V,E)
by a polynomial graph. Let
\mathbf{V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{|CC|})
be the partition of the vertices induced by the connected components of
\mathcal{L}
while only keeping the edges with weights one. Let
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
be another partition of
V
that satisfies
\mathbf{V}\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\{V\}
. Then, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

We gather in next lemma some properties of the
order
of multivariate power series that we will use throughout the proofs.

Lemma F.5
.

Let
f,g
be multivariate series with positive radius. We have;

1.

\mathrm{ord}(fg)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)+\mathrm{ord}(g)
;

2.

\mathrm{ord}(f+g)\succcurlyeq\inf\left(\mathrm{ord}(f),\mathrm{ord}(g)\right)
, where the infimum is taken for the order
\succcurlyeq
;

3.

If
f(0,0)=0
, then
\frac{1}{1+f}-1
is a multivariate series with positive radius and
\mathrm{ord}(\frac{1}{1+f}-1)=\mathrm{ord}(f)
.

F.3
Control of the order of
\kappa_{{}_{[\ell]}}

We are now in position to introduce the polynomial graph
\mathcal{L}^{*}
that will be useful to lower-bound the
order
of
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)
.
We recall that
I_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell},A_{1},\ldots,A_{q},B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
are subsets of
[n]
involved in the definition of the
mixed moments (
47
) of
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
.

Definition 10
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{*}
be the polynomial graph with vertex set
[\ell]
whose edges are defined as follows. For
(t,t^{\prime})\in[\ell]
:

•

there is an edge with weight
y
between
t
and
t^{\prime}
if there exists
i\in[r]
such that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersect
B_{i}
.

•

there is an edge with weight
x
between
t
and
t^{\prime}
, if there does not exist
i\in[r]
such that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersect
B_{i}
and if there exists
j\in[q]
such that that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersect
A_{j}
.

•

Otherwise, there is no edge between
t
and
t^{\prime}
.

We shall lower-bound the
order
of
\kappa_{\ell}(x,y)
in term of the
order
of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
The following lemma, which is the counterpart in our setting of Proposition 5.8 of
[
Fer18
]
, ensures that, if one is able to lower-bound the
order
of
P_{\Delta}[u]
, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
, with respect to the
order
of some well chosen polynomial graph, then one is able to lower-bound the
order
of
\kappa_{\Delta}
for all
\Delta\subseteq R
. The proof is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma F.6
.

Let
\left(u_{\Delta}(x,y)\right)_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}
be a sequence of multivariate series indexed by subsets of
[\ell]
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a polynomial graph on
[\ell]
whose edges are equal either to
x
,
y
,
0
or
1
. Let us suppose that, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
, one has
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[u]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta]\right)
. Then, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, one also has

\mathrm{ord}(\kappa_{\Delta})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta]\right)+\sum_{i\in\Delta}\mathrm{ord}(u_{\{i\}})\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, to lower bound the order of
\kappa_{\Delta}
, we mostly have to lower bound the order of
P_{\Delta}[u]-1
in terms of that of
\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]
, and then in turn to control the order of
\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]
.
The following lemma, proved in Section
F.7
, lower-bounds the
order
of
P_{\Delta}[u]-1
, for all
\Delta
of size at least
2
.

Lemma F.7
.

For all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
, one has

\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[u]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Combining Lemma
F.6
and Lemma
F.7
, we get that, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
,

\mathrm{ord}\left(\kappa_{\Delta}\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)+\sum_{i\in\Delta}\mathrm{ord}\left(u_{\{i\}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

When
\Delta
is a singleton that is
\Delta=\left\{i\right\}
, we directly have
\kappa_{\Delta}=u_{\{i\}}
and so the above inequality still holds.
Since
\mathrm{ord}\left(u_{\{i\}}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have for all non-empty
\Delta\subseteq R
,

\mathrm{ord}\left(\kappa_{\Delta}\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)\kern 5.0pt\kern 5.0pt.

(51)

It remains to lower-bound
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}\right)=\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[[\ell]]\right)
. We recall that the graph
\mathcal{B}
defined in the statement of Lemma
3.1
, is a graph on the vertex set
[\ell]
with an edge between
t
,
t^{\prime}
in
[\ell]
if and only if there exists
i\in[r]
such that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersects
B_{i}
and that
|cc(\mathcal{B})|
stands for the number of connected components of this graph.

Lemma F.8
.

Writing
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}\right)=(d_{[\ell]},d^{\prime}_{[\ell]})
, we have

d_{[\ell]}\geq|cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)|-1\quad\quad\text{ and }d_{[\ell]}+d^{\prime}_{[\ell]}\geq\ell-1\kern 5.0pt.

In particular,
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)|-1,\ell-|cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)|\right)
.

Combining (
51
) with Lemma
F.8
leads to o lower-bound on the order of
\kappa_{[\ell]}

\mathrm{ord}\left(\kappa_{[\ell]}\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)|-1,\ell-|cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)|\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(52)

F.4
Non-asymptotic control of the coefficients

In order to deduce from (
52
) a non-asymptotic upper-bound of
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x_{0},y_{0})
, it is sufficient to upper-bound the absolute value of the coefficients of the decomposition of
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)
as a multivariate series. We recall that, for
[\ell]\subseteq R
, we have

\displaystyle u_{\Delta}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In order to upper-bound the coefficients of the series
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)
, we shall first do so for the
u_{\Delta}(x,y)
’s. In the following, given
f
a multivariate series and
d,d^{\prime}\geq 0
, we write
[d,d^{\prime}]_{f}
the coefficient of
x^{d}y^{d^{\prime}}
in the decomposition of
f
. For any
d
,
d^{\prime}\geq 0
, and
j=1,\ldots,q
, we define

\displaystyle g_{1,j}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}\ ;

\displaystyle g_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j=1}^{q}g_{1,j}(x,y)\ ;

\displaystyle g_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right)\ ,

so that
[d,d^{\prime}]_{u_{\Delta}}=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}[d,0]_{g_{1}}[0,d^{\prime}]_{g_{2}}
. One the one hand, for any
j=1,\ldots,q
and any
d\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle[d,0]_{g_{1},j}=
