\displaystyle\underset{d_{1}+\ldots+d_{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1}=d}{\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1}\geq 0}}\prod_{a=1}^{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1}a^{d_{a}}\leq\left(1+\ldots+(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)\right)^{d}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d}\kern 5.0pt.

We deduce that

\displaystyle[d,0]_{g_{1}}=

\displaystyle\underset{d_{1}+\ldots+d_{q}=d}{\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{q}\geq 0}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}[d_{j},0]_{g_{1},j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underset{d_{1}+\ldots+d_{q}=d}{\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{q}\geq 0}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\left(\frac{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d}\leq\left(\frac{|I_{\Delta}|^{2}}{2}\right)^{d}\ ,

where the last equality stems from the disjunction of the
A_{i}
.
Then, turning our attention to
g_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|[0,d^{\prime}]_{g_{2}}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\binom{\sum_{i=1,\ldots,r}|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1}{d^{\prime}}|I_{\Delta}|^{d^{\prime}}\leq|I_{\Delta}|^{2d^{\prime}}\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude that

|[d,d^{\prime}]_{u_{\Delta}}|\leq\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}|I_{\Delta}|^{2d+2d^{\prime}}\kern 5.0pt.

(53)

Then, we consider any partition
\boldsymbol{\Delta}=(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|})
of
[\ell]
. Define
h_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}=\prod_{a=1}^{{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}}u_{\Delta_{a}}
.
Recall that the
I_{t}
’s defined for (
8
) have disjoint support so that the
I_{\Delta_{a}}
’s are disjoint and
\sum_{a=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}|I_{\Delta_{a}}|=L
. Hence, we deduce from (
53
) that

\displaystyle\left|[d,d^{\prime}]_{h_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\underset{d_{1}+\ldots+d_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}=d}{\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}\geq 0}}\quad\underset{d^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+d^{\prime}_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}=d^{\prime}}{\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}\geq 0}}\prod_{a=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}[d_{a},d^{\prime}_{a}]_{u_{\Delta_{a}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{L}\underset{d_{1}+\ldots+d_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}=d}{\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}\geq 0}}\quad\underset{d^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+d^{\prime}_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}=d^{\prime}}{\sum_{d^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,d^{\prime}_{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}\geq 0}}\prod_{a=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}|I_{\Delta_{a}}|^{2d_{a}+2d^{\prime}_{a}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{L}\left(\sum_{a=1}^{|\boldsymbol{\Delta}|}|I_{\Delta_{a}}|\right)^{2d+2d^{\prime}}=\eta^{L}L^{2d+2d^{\prime}}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, combining this with a triangular inequality and the Möbius formula (Lemma
B.1
), we obtain the following bound for the coefficients of
\kappa_{[\ell]}
.

\left|[d,d^{\prime}]_{\kappa_{[\ell]}}\right|\leq\eta^{L}L^{2d+2d^{\prime}}\ell^{2\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

(54)

F.5
Conclusion

Combining Equation (
54
) together with (
52
) leads us to

\displaystyle\left|\kappa_{[\ell]}\left(x_{0},y_{0}\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\underset{d+d^{\prime}\geq\ell-1}{\sum_{d\geq cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}}L^{2d+2d^{\prime}}x_{0}^{d}y_{0}^{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\sum_{d\geq cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}\sum_{d^{\prime}\geq\ell-1-d}\left(L^{2}x_{0}\right)^{d}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\sum_{d\geq cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}\left(L^{2}x_{0}\right)^{d}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{\ell-1-d}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}\sum_{d\geq cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}\left(x_{0}/y_{0}\right)^{d}

\displaystyle\leq 4\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{y_{0}}\right)^{cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where the third and fifth inequalities are satisfied whenever
L^{2}y_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}y_{0}
. We have shown

\left|\mathop{cumul}\left(\left(Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[\ell]}\right)\right|\leq 4\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}y_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{y_{0}}\right)^{cc\left(\mathcal{B}\right)-1}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

F.6
Proof of Lemma
F.6

The proof of Lemma
F.6
build on the ideas of the proof of Proposition 5.8 of
[
Fer18
]
, which are adapted to the framework of multivariate series.

Let us first reduce the problem to the case where
u_{\{i\}}=1
. Let us define the family of power series

w_{\Delta}(x,y)=\frac{u_{\Delta}(x,y)}{\prod_{i\in\Delta}u_{\{i\}}(x,y)}.

We have
P_{\Delta}[w]=P_{\Delta}[u]
, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
. Defining
\kappa_{\Delta}[w]
as the counterpart of
\kappa_{\Delta}
when the function
u
is replaced by
w
, we get

\kappa_{\Delta}(x,y)=\left(\prod_{i\in\Delta}u_{\{i\}}(x,y)\right)\kappa_{\Delta}[w](x,y)\kern 5.0pt.

So, if we prove that
\mathrm{ord}(\kappa_{\Delta}[w])\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta])
(see Definition
9
for the order of a power serie), then by Lemma
F.5

\mathrm{ord}(\kappa_{\Delta})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta])+\sum_{i\in\Delta}\mathrm{ord}(u_{\left\{i\right\}}).

Hence, in the following, we assume that
u_{\{i\}}=1
for all
i\in[\ell]
. By assumption, for all
\Delta
of size at least
2
, one has
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}(u)-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta]\right)
and we shall lower-bound
\mathrm{ord}\left(\kappa_{\Delta}\right)
for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
. Without loss of generality, it is sufficient to do so for
\Delta=[\ell]
.

For
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, define
R_{\Delta}(x,y)=P_{\Delta}(u)(x,y)-1
. By assumption, we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(R_{\Delta}\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta]\right)
. Let us fix a partition
\boldsymbol{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{s})
of
[\ell]
, and let us consider
W\in\boldsymbol{W}
. Since
u_{\emptyset}=1
,
we have

\displaystyle\prod_{\Delta\subseteq W}P_{\Delta}[u](x,y)=

\displaystyle\prod_{\Delta\subseteq W}\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}\left(u_{\delta}(x,y)\right)^{\left(-1\right)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\delta\subseteq W}\left(u_{\delta}(x,y)\right)^{\sum_{\delta\subseteq\Delta\subseteq W}(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{W}(x,y)\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality comes from the fact that, whenever
\delta\subsetneq W
,
\sum_{\delta\subseteq\Delta\subseteq W}(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}=\left(1-1\right)^{|W|-|\delta|}=0
. In turn, expanding
P_{\Delta}(x,y)=1+R_{\Delta}(x,y)
and using the fact that for all
\left\{i\right\}
,
P_{\left\{i\right\}}[u](x,y)=1
,
we get,

u_{W}(x,y)=\underset{|\Delta|\geq 2}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq W}}\left(1+R_{\Delta}(x,y)\right)=\sum_{\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}}R_{\Delta_{1}}(x,y)\ldots R_{\Delta_{m}}(x,y)\kern 5.0pt,

where the sum runs over all finite sets of distinct subsets of
W
of size at least
2
. Therefore,

\prod_{i=1}^{s}u_{W_{i}}(x,y)=\sum_{\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}}R_{\Delta_{1}}(x,y)\ldots R_{\Delta_{m}}(x,y)\kern 5.0pt,

(55)

where the sum is taken over all finite sets of distinct subsets (but not necessarily disjoint) of
[\ell]
of size at least
2
, such that each
\Delta_{i}
is contained in a group of the partition
\boldsymbol{W}
. Let us fix such a set
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
and, for
i\in[m]
, let us write
\Pi\left(\Delta_{i}\right)
the partition of
[\ell]
containing
\Delta_{i}
and singletons. We recall that
\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)
is the partition of
[\ell]
defined by; for
t,t^{\prime}\in[\ell]
,
t
and
t^{\prime}
are in the same group of
\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)
if and only if there exist
t_{1},\ldots,t_{h}
with
t_{1}=t
,
t_{h}=t^{\prime}
and, for all
h^{\prime}\leq h-1
, there exists
i\in[m]
such that
t_{h}
and
t_{h+1}
are in the same group of the partition
\Pi\left(\Delta_{i}\right)
. Since each
\Delta_{i}
is contained in a group of
\boldsymbol{W}
, we have that each group of
\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)
must be contained in a group of
\boldsymbol{W}
. Consequently,
\boldsymbol{W}\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\boldsymbol{W}
.

In the following, given
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
a set of distinct subsets of
[\ell]
of size at least
2
, we denote
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}([\ell])
the set of all partitions
\boldsymbol{W}
of
[\ell]
satisfying that, for all
i\in[m]
,
\Delta_{i}
is contained in a group of
\boldsymbol{W}
. For all
\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}([\ell])
, we have
\boldsymbol{W}\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\boldsymbol{W}
.
Using the Möbius formula stated in Lemma
B.1
, together with (
55
), and after reversing the summation order, we get

\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)=\underset{|\Delta_{i}|\geq 2}{\underset{\Delta_{i}\subseteq[\ell]}{\sum_{\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}}}}R_{\Delta_{1}}(x,y)\ldots R_{\Delta_{m}}(x,y)\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}([\ell])}m(\boldsymbol{W})\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(56)

where
m(\boldsymbol{W})=(-1)^{|\boldsymbol{W}|-1}(|\boldsymbol{W}|-1)!
is the Möbius function.
Next lemma prunes some of the terms of (
56
).

Lemma F.9
.

Let
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
a family of distinct subsets of
[\ell]
. If
\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})\neq\left\{[\ell]\right\}
, we have

\sum_{\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}([\ell])}m(\boldsymbol{W})=0\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of Lemma
F.9
.

Let us consider
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}=\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})
, and let us write
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}=W^{(0)}_{1},\ldots,W^{(0)}_{|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|}
, with
|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|\geq 2
. By definition of
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}([\ell])=\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}}([\ell])
and so it is sufficient to prove that
\sum_{\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}^{(0)}}([\ell])}m(\boldsymbol{W})=0
, when
\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}
is a partition of
[\ell]
different from the trivial partition
\left\{[\ell]\right\}
. We can construct a bijection
\phi
from all partitions
\boldsymbol{G}
of
[|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|]
to the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}^{(0)}}([\ell])
defined by
\phi(\boldsymbol{G})=\left\{\cup_{i\in G}W^{(0)}_{i},\kern 5.0ptG\in\boldsymbol{G}\right\}
. This bijection preserves the Möbius function in the sense that
m(\phi(\boldsymbol{G}))=m(\boldsymbol{G})
and so

\sum_{\boldsymbol{W}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{W}^{(0)}}([\ell])}m(\boldsymbol{W})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\mathcal{P}([|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|])}m(\boldsymbol{G})=\mathop{cumul}\left(1,\ldots,1\right)=0\kern 5.0pt,

where the last cumulant is the cumulant of
|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|
copies of the deterministic variable
1
, which is equal to
0
as soon as
|\boldsymbol{W}^{(0)}|\geq 2
(see Lemma
B.2
). This concludes the proof of Lemma
F.9
.
∎

Combining Lemma
F.9
and Equality (
56
), we get

\kappa_{[\ell]}(x,y)=\underset{|\Delta_{i}|\geq 2}{\underset{\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})=\left\{[\ell]\right\}}{\sum_{\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}}}}R_{\Delta_{1}}(x,y)\ldots R_{\Delta_{m}}(x,y)\kern 5.0pt.

(57)

Let us now consider
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
a set of subsets of
[\ell]
such that
\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\left\{[\ell]\right\}
. We consider
\boldsymbol{V}
the partition of
[\ell]
induced by the connected components of
\mathcal{L}\langle 1\rangle
, which is the graph keeping only the edges of weight
1
of
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\left\{[\ell]\right\}
, one necessarily has
\boldsymbol{V}\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\vee\ldots\vee\Pi\left(\Delta_{m}\right)=\left\{[\ell]\right\}
. Hence, using Lemma
F.4
, we get

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}])\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})\kern 5.0pt.

We recall our assumption that
\mathrm{ord}(R_{\Delta_{i}})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}])
. Lemma
F.5
ensures that,

\mathrm{ord}\left(R_{\Delta_{1}}\ldots R_{\Delta_{m}}\right)\succcurlyeq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}])\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, all the non-zero terms in the sum (
56
) have their
order
higher than
\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})
with respect to the ordering
\succcurlyeq
. Invoquing a last time Lemma
F.5
, we get the claimed inequality

\mathrm{ord}(\kappa_{[\ell]})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L})\kern 5.0pt.

F.7
Proof of Lemma
F.7

The power series
u_{\Delta}
involves multiple terms that are alike factorial terms. For proving Lemma
F.8
, we shall apply several times the following result which which is variation of Proposition 5.10 in
[
Fer18
]
. Its proof is postponed to the end of the subsection.

Lemma F.10
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be non-negative integers, and, for
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, let us define

\displaystyle v_{\Delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\left(1-x\right)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta}a_{i}-1\right)x\right)\kern 5.0pt;

\displaystyle w_{\Delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left(1-x\right)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta}a_{i}-1\right)x\right)}\kern 5.0pt\ ,

with the convention, if
\sum_{i\in\Delta}a_{i}=0
or
\sum_{i\in\Delta}a_{i}=1
,
v_{\Delta}(x,y)=w_{\Delta}(x,y)=1
. Recall the operator
P_{\Delta}
defined in (
50
).
Then, for all
\Delta
of size at least
2
, we have

\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(|\Delta|-1,0)\kern 5.0pt\ ;\quad\quad\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(|\Delta|-1,0)\kern 5.0pt.

We shall also use next lemma, whose proof is also postponed to the end of this subsection, and which enables us to decompose
u
as a product of power series.

Lemma F.11
.

Let
\left(v_{\Delta}\right)_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}
and
\left(w_{\Delta}\right)_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}
to families of power series in
x
and
y
indexed by subsets of
[\ell]
. Suppose that there exists
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
. Then, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
,
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[vw]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
.

Now, we have all the ingredients to prove Lemma
F.7
. Let
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
of size at least
2
. We seek to lower bound
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[u]-1\right)
, where

P_{\Delta}[u](x,y)=\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}\left(u_{\delta}(x,y)\right)^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}\kern 5.0pt,

where we recall that, for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
,

\displaystyle u_{\Delta}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-y)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Let us define, for
j=1,\ldots,q
,

w^{(j)}_{\Delta}(x,y):=\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}\kern 5.0pt,

and, for
i\in[r]
,

v^{(i)}_{\Delta}(x,y):=(1-y)\times\ldots\times\left(1-\left(|I_{\Delta}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right)\kern 5.0pt,

and
z_{\Delta}(x,y):=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}
.

For all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, we have
u_{\Delta}(x,y)=z_{\Delta}(x,y)\prod_{j=1}^{q}w^{(j)}_{\Delta}(x,y)\prod_{i=1}^{r}v^{(i)}_{\Delta}(x,y)
. In the light of Lemma
F.11
, in order to prove that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[u]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
, it is sufficient to prove that we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
, for all
j=1,\ldots,q
, and
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v^{(i)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
, for all
i\in[r]
, and
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[z]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
.

The term z

. Let us first deal with the constant term
P_{\Delta}[z]
. It is clear that for all
\delta\subseteq\Delta
, we have
z_{\delta}=\prod_{t^{\prime}\in\delta}z_{\left\{t^{\prime}\right\}}
. Therefore, using Lemma
F.1
, we deduce that as soon as
\Delta
is of size at least
2
,
P_{\Delta}[z]=1
and thus
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[z]-1\right)=+\infty
. Hence, it is clear that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[z]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)
.

The term
w^{(j)}
.

Let us now deal with the term
P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]
. If there exists
t\in\Delta
such that
I_{t}\cap A_{j}=\emptyset
, we can easily check using Lemma
F.1
that
P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]=1
and so
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
. Let us now suppose that for all
t\in\Delta
, we have
I_{t}\cap A_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Note that this implies that
\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]
is a complete graph, and is in particular connected (we recall that
\mathcal{L}^{*}
is defined in Definition
10
, page
10
). Since any spanning tree of
\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]
has exactly
|\Delta|-1
edges, we derive that
\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])\preccurlyeq\left(|\Delta|-1,0\right)
.

For all
\delta\subseteq\Delta
, we remark that

w^{(j)}_{\delta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{(1-x)\ldots(1-(\sum_{t\in\delta}|I_{\left\{t\right\}}\cap A_{j}|-1)x)}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma
F.10
then ensures that, for all
\Delta
of size at least
2
,
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|\Delta|-1,0\right)
. We have proved that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w^{(j)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
.

The term
v^{(i)}
.

Finally, we shall prove that, for all
i\in[r]
,
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v^{(i)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)
. If there exists
t\in\Delta
such that
I_{t}\cap B_{i}=\emptyset
, then, using Lemma
F.1
, we deduce
P_{\Delta}[v^{(i)}]=1
and so
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v^{(i)}]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])
. Let us now suppose that all
t\in\Delta
, we have
B_{i}\cap I_{t}\neq\emptyset
. In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]
is a complete graph with edges with weight
y
. Hence,
\mathrm{ord}(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta])=\left(0,|\Delta|-1\right)
. For all
\delta\subseteq\Delta
,
v^{(i)}_{\delta}=\left(1-y\right)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\delta}|I_{t^{\prime}}\cap B_{i}|-1\right)y\right)
. So , using Lemma
F.10
, we deduce that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(0,|\Delta|-1)=\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}[\Delta]\right)
.

Combining those three points together concludes the proof of Lemma
F.7
.

Proof of Lemma
F.10
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
be a set of non-negative integers. We remark that
v_{\Delta}=1/w_{\Delta}
for any subset
\Delta
, which in turn implies
P_{\Delta}[v]-1=\frac{1}{P_{\Delta}[w]}-1=\frac{1}{1+(P_{\Delta}[w]-1)}-1
. Since
\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1(0,0)\right)=\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)(0,0)=0
, we get, using Lemma
F.5
, that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)
and, similarly,
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)
. We deduce
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)=\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)
. Hence, for
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
, having
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|\Delta|-1,0\right)
is equivalent to having
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|\Delta|-1,0\right)
. Hence, we only need to prove the result for
P_{\Delta}[v]
.

We argue by induction on
\ell
and then
a_{\ell}
that this holds for all
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
. For
\ell=1
, the result is trivial. Consider
\ell>1
, and assume that for all
\Delta\subsetneq[\ell]
, we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq\left(|\Delta|-1,0\right)
. Let us prove that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
.

In the following, for
k\geq 0
, we define
v_{\Delta}[k]
as

v_{\Delta}[k](x,y):=\left(1-x\right)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\mathbf{1}\left\{\ell\in\Delta\right\}-1\right)x\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, we have
v_{\Delta}=v_{\Delta}[a_{\ell}]
. Hence, we only need to prove that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}(v[k])-1\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
for all non-negative integers
k
and any
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell-1})
.

Let us first consider the case
k=0
. Then, for any
\Delta\subseteq[\ell-1]
,

v_{\Delta}[k](x,y)=v_{\Delta\cup\left\{\ell\right\}}[k](x,y)\kern 5.0pt.

By definition (
50
) of
P_{\Delta}
, this implies that for all
x,y
,
P_{[\ell]}(v[a_{\ell}])(x,y)=1
. Hence, we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}(v[a_{\ell}])-1\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
.

Now assume for some
k>0
, we have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}(v[k])-1\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
and let us prove that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}(v[k+1])-1\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
. We observe that, for
\ell\in\Delta
,

\displaystyle v_{\Delta}[k+1]=

\displaystyle(1-x)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{l\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-x)\ldots\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k-1\right)x\right)\times\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{\Delta}[k]\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)x\right)\kern 5.0pt.

On the other hand, if
\ell\notin\Delta
, we have
v_{\Delta}[k+1]=v_{\Delta}[k]
.
Thus, we deduce that

P_{[\ell]}[v[k+1]]=\left(\underset{\ell\in\Delta}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}}\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)x\right)^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}\right)P_{[\ell]}[v[k]]\kern 5.0pt.

Let us define the multivariate series

\displaystyle Q(x):=

\displaystyle\underset{\ell\in\Delta}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}}\left(1-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)x\right)^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}=\underset{\ell\in\Delta}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}}x^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}\left(\frac{1}{x}-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)\right)^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\ell\in\Delta}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}}\left(\frac{1}{x}-\left(\sum_{i\in\Delta\setminus\left\{\ell\right\}}a_{i}+k\right)\right)^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}\ ,

where we used that, for
\ell\geq 2
,
\underset{\ell\in\Delta}{\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell]}}x^{(-1)^{\ell-|\Delta|}}=1
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, define
F(t):=\prod_{\Delta\subseteq[\ell-1]}\left(t-\sum_{i\in\Delta}a_{i}\right)^{(-1)^{|\Delta|+1}}
. Then, we have

Q(x)=\left(F\left(\frac{1}{x}-k\right)\right)^{(-1)^{\ell}}\kern 5.0pt.

From Lemma A.1 of
[
Fer18
]
, we know that
F(t)-1
is a rational function in
t
of degree at most
-\ell+1
. Hence,
Q(x)-1
is a multivariate series in
x
of
order
at least
\ell-1
. Combining this with the induction hypothesis, we deduce that
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{[\ell]}[v[k+1]]\right)\succcurlyeq(\ell-1,0)
, which concludes the induction and the proof of Lemma
F.10
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
F.11
.

Let
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
. We have

P_{\Delta}[vw]-1=P_{\Delta}[v]-1+P_{\Delta}[w]-1+\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\kern 5.0pt.

By hypothesis of the lemma, we both have
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{ord}\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
. This also implies by Lemma
F.5
that
\mathrm{ord}\left(\left(P_{\Delta}[v]-1\right)\left(P_{\Delta}[w]-1\right)\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
and so, still using Lemma
F.5
,
\left(P_{\Delta}[vw]-1\right)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
F.11
.

∎

F.8
Proof of the remaining technical lemmas

Proof of Lemma
F.1
.

For
\delta\subset\Delta
, we write
\delta_{1}=\delta\cap I_{1}
and
\delta_{2}=\delta\cap I_{2}
. By definition, one has

\displaystyle P_{\Delta}[v]=

\displaystyle\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}v_{\delta}^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}=\prod_{\delta\subseteq\Delta}v_{\delta_{1}}^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}v_{\delta_{2}}^{(-1)^{|\Delta|-|\delta|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}\prod_{\delta_{2}\subseteq I_{2}}v_{\delta_{1}}^{(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}}v_{\delta_{2}}^{(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}\left(v_{\delta_{1}}^{(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}}\right)^{\sum_{\delta_{2}\subseteq I_{2}}(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}}\prod_{\delta_{2}\subseteq I_{2}}\left(v_{\delta_{2}}^{(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}}\right)^{\sum_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}}\kern 5.0pt.

We have
\sum_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}=\left(-1\right)^{-|I_{1}|}\sum_{t=1}^{|I_{1}|}\binom{|I_{1}|}{t}(-1)^{t}=0^{|I_{1}|}=0
since
I_{1}\neq\emptyset
. As a consequence,

\prod_{\delta_{2}\subseteq I_{2}}\left(v_{\delta_{2}}^{(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}}\right)^{\sum_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}}=1\kern 5.0pt,

and, similarly,

\prod_{\delta_{1}\subseteq I_{1}}\left(v_{\delta_{1}}^{(-1)^{|I_{1}|-|\delta_{1}|}}\right)^{\sum_{\delta_{2}\subseteq I_{2}}(-1)^{|I_{2}|-|\delta_{2}|}}=1\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude that
P_{\Delta}[v]=1
.

∎

Proof of lemma
F.2
.

Consider any
S\subset\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Let

s_{0}=\min\left\{s\in\mathbb{N},\kern 5.0pt\exists s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\kern 5.0pts.t\kern 5.0pt(s,s^{\prime})\in S\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

and let
s_{0}^{\prime}
the maximal element in
\mathbb{N}
such that for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in S
,
s_{0}+s_{0}^{\prime}\leq s+s^{\prime}
, i.e.

s_{0}+s^{\prime}_{0}=s_{0}+\min_{(s,s^{\prime})\in S}(s+s^{\prime}).

It is clear that
(s,s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})
for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in S
. It remains to prove that for all
(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})
satisfying
(s,s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})
for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in S
, we have
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})\succcurlyeq(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})
. First, for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in S
,
s_{1}\leq s
, which implies that
s_{0}\geq s_{1}
. Then, for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in S
, we have
s+s^{\prime}\geq s_{1}+s^{\prime}_{1}
. Again, this readily implies that
s_{0}+s^{\prime}_{0}\geq s_{1}+s^{\prime}_{1}
. We conclude that
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})\succcurlyeq(s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1})
. Hence,
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})
is the infimum of
S
, which is unique by definition
∎

Proof of Lemma
F.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(V,E)
be a polynomial graph. We write
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{|CC|})
for the partition of the vertices corresponding to the connected components of a subgraph of
\mathcal{L}
, where we only keep the edges with weight
1
. Let
\mathcal{T}
denote a spanning tree of
\mathcal{L}
and let
(s,s^{\prime})
be its degree. By definition,
\mathcal{T}
must span all the
V_{t}
’s. Hence, it has at least
|CC|-1
edges of weight
x
or
y
. We deduce that
s+s^{\prime}\geq|CC|-1
.

Let us construct a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
, with degree
(s,s^{\prime})
, such that
s+s^{\prime}=|CC|-1
. To do so, we consider
E_{1}
the set of edges of
\mathcal{L}
whose extremities are between two different connected components
V_{t}
and
V_{t^{\prime}}
. We denote
\mathcal{G}_{CC}
the induced polynomial multi-graph on
[|CC|]
. We extract from
\mathcal{G}_{CC}
a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}^{CC}
, and for all edges of
\mathcal{T}_{CC}
between some
t
and
t^{\prime}
, we keep an edge of
E_{1}
connecting
V_{t}
and
V_{t^{\prime}}
. We denote by
E_{1}^{\prime}
, which is of size
|CC|-1
, the corresponding subset of
E_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the polynomial graph obtained by the union of
E_{1}^{\prime}
with all the edges of weight
1
. It is clear that
\mathcal{G}
spans the vertex set
V
. We extract
\mathcal{T}
a spanning tree of
\mathcal{G}
. By definition, we have
s+s^{\prime}=|CC|-1
.

Let us now take
\mathcal{T}_{0}
a spanning tree of
\mathcal{G}_{CC}
with
|CC|-1
edges of weight
x
or
y
and that minimizes the number of edges of weight
x
. By constructing a spanning graph
\mathcal{T}^{*}
of
\mathcal{G}
from
\mathcal{T}_{0}
as above, we deduce that its degree is the infimum of the degrees of all spanning trees.
∎

Proof of Lemma
F.4
.

We consider
\mathbb{\Delta}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}\right\}
a set of subsets of
[\ell]
such that
\boldsymbol{V}\vee\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})=\{V\}
. If there exists
i\in[m]
such that
\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]
is not connected, then
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]\right)=\left(+\infty,+\infty\right)
and the result is trivial. Let us now suppose that, for all
i\in[m]
,
\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]
is connected. For
i\in[m]
, consider
\mathcal{T}_{i}
a spanning tree of
\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]
with
deg(\mathcal{T}_{i})=\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]\right)
–such a tree exists by Lemma
F.3
. Each
\mathcal{T}_{i}
can then be viewed as a subset of edges of the original graph
\mathcal{L}
. Let
E_{0}
be the union of all the edges of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
’s and of all edges of weight
1
of
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\boldsymbol{V}\vee\Pi(\Delta_{1})\vee\ldots\vee\Pi(\Delta_{m})=\{V\}
, we know that
E_{0}
spans
V
. We extract a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
from
E_{0}
. Considering
E_{0}
as a monomial in
x
and
y
, we deduce that

deg(\mathcal{T})\preccurlyeq deg(E_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}deg(\mathcal{T}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\preccurlyeq deg(\mathcal{T})
, we conclude that

\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\preccurlyeq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}[\Delta_{i}]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Proof of Lemma
F.5
.

Let us take
f(x,y)=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\geq 0}f_{s,s^{\prime}}x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}
and
g=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\geq 0}g_{s,s^{\prime}}x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}
.

1.

We have
(fg)(x,y)=\sum_{s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime}\geq 0}\left(\sum_{s\leq s_{0}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\leq s^{\prime}_{0}}f_{s,s^{\prime}}g_{s_{0}-s,s^{\prime}_{0}-s^{\prime}}\right)x^{s_{0}}y^{s^{\prime}_{0}}
. We write
(fg)_{s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}}=\sum_{s\leq s_{0}}\sum_{s^{\prime}\leq s^{\prime}_{0}}f_{s,s^{\prime}}g_{s_{0}-s,s^{\prime}_{0}-s^{\prime}}
. For having
(fg)_{s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}}\neq 0
, there must exists
s\leq s_{0}
and
s^{\prime}\leq s^{\prime}_{0}
such that
f_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0
and
g_{s_{0}-s,s^{\prime}_{0}-s^{\prime}}\neq 0
. In that case, by definition, we have
(s,s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)
and
(s_{0}-s,s^{\prime}_{0}-s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(g)
so that
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)+\mathrm{ord}(g)
. This being true for all
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})
with
(fg)_{s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}}\neq 0
, we deduce taking the infimum that
\mathrm{ord}(fg)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)+\mathrm{ord}(g)
.

2.

We have
(f+g)(x,y)=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\geq 0}\left(f_{s,s^{\prime}}+g_{s,s^{\prime}}\right)x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}
. Let us write
(f+g)_{s,s^{\prime}}=f_{s,s^{\prime}}+g_{s,s^{\prime}}
. For having
(f+g)_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0
, one needs that either
f_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0
or
g_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0
and so
(s,s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq\inf\left(\mathrm{ord}(f),\mathrm{ord}(g)\right)
. We conclude
\mathrm{ord}(f+g)\succcurlyeq\inf\left(\mathrm{ord}(f),\mathrm{ord}(g)\right)
:

3.

Let us suppose that
f(0,0)=0
. Then, on an open ball around
(0,0)
,
|f(x,y)|<1
, and so

\frac{1}{1+f(x,y)}-1=\sum_{d\geq 1}(-f(x,y))^{d}:=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\geq 0}\left(\frac{1}{1+f}-1\right)_{s,s^{\prime}}x^{s}y^{s^{\prime}}\kern 5.0pt.

Using point 1., we know that for all
d\geq 1
,
\mathrm{ord}((-f)^{d})\succcurlyeq d\cdot\mathrm{ord}(f)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)
. For
s,s^{\prime}
such that
\left(\frac{1}{1+f}-1\right)_{s,s^{\prime}}\neq 0
, there exists
d
such that, wherenever
d^{\prime}\geq d+1
,
d^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{ord}(f)\succcurlyeq(s,s^{\prime})
. And so, using the second point of this proof generalized to finite sums of multivariate power series, we get that
(s,s^{\prime})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(\sum_{d^{\prime}\leq d}f^{d})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)
. We conclude
\mathrm{ord}(\frac{1}{1+f}-1)\succcurlyeq\mathrm{ord}(f)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
F.8
.

Let us write
\mathrm{ord}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}\right)=\left(d_{[\ell]},d^{\prime}_{[\ell]}\right)
. Suppose
\left(d_{[\ell]},d^{\prime}_{[\ell]}\right)
is finite. From Lemma
F.3
, we know that there exists a spanning tree
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
of degree
\left(d_{[\ell]},d^{\prime}_{[\ell]}\right)
.

A spanning tree, if there exists one, has necessarily at least
|\ell|-1
edges. All the edges in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
are of weight
x
,
y
. Hence,
d_{[\ell]}+d^{\prime}_{[\ell]}\geq\ell-1
.

We recall that
\mathcal{B}
is the graph on
[\ell]
with an edge between
t
,
t^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
i\in[r]
such that both
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
intersects
B_{i}
, and that
cc(\mathcal{B})
stands for the number of connected components of this graph. We remark that
(t,t^{\prime})
is an edge of
\mathcal{B}
if and only if it is an edge of weight
y
of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
. Thus,
\mathcal{T}
has at least
cc(\mathcal{B})-1
edges of weight
x
and so
d_{[\ell]}\geq cc(\mathcal{B})-1
.

∎

F.9
Case
r=0
.

This section is devoted to proving the second point of Lemma
3.1
. Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}
have mixed moments given by

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{t\in\Delta}Z_{t}\right]=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x_{0}}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x_{0}}\ ,\quad\text{for $\Delta\subseteq[R]$.}

This is a particular case of the cumulant dealt in the first point. We could use the result of the first point, but loose a factor
2
compared to the result that we want to prove. However, let us explain in this section what simplifications we can make in the specific case
r=0
. We Define, for
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
,
u_{\Delta}(x)=\eta^{|I_{\Delta}|}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{1-x}\times\ldots\times\frac{1}{1-(|I_{\Delta}\cap A_{j}|-1)x}
which is a power series in
x
. We define the associated power series, for
\Delta\subseteq[\ell]
,
\kappa_{\Delta}(x)=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{P}\left(\Delta\right)}m(G)\prod_{\delta\in G}u_{\delta}(x)
. We remark that
\mathop{cumul}\left(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}\right)=\kappa_{[\ell]}(x_{0})
. The
order
of this power series is of the form
(d_{\Delta},0)
and, using the results of Section
F.3
, we get that
d_{[\ell]}\geq\ell-1
.
When
x
is small enough, we can expand the power series
\kappa_{[\ell]}(x)=\sum_{d\geq\ell-1}\kappa_{d}x^{d}
. We plug the results of Section
F.4
and we get that, for all
d\geq\ell-1
,
|\kappa_{d}|\leq\eta^{L}L^{2d}\ell^{2\ell}
, where we recall
L=|I_{[\ell]}|
. When
2Lx^{2}\leq 1
, we end up with,

\displaystyle|\kappa_{[\ell]}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d\geq\ell-1}\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}L^{2d}x^{d}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}x\right)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt,

and we conclude with
|\kappa_{[\ell]}(x_{0})|=|\mathop{cumul}\left(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\ell}\right)|\leq 2\ell^{2\ell}\eta^{L}\left(L^{2}x_{0}\right)^{\ell-1}
when
2Lx_{0}^{2}\leq 1
.

Appendix G
Other losses for seriation

In this section, we introduce the general Tœplitz-Robinson seriation model. Given a vector
\theta=(\theta_{0},\dots,\theta_{n-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
T(\theta)
for the Tœplitz matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with entries
T(\theta)_{i,j}=\theta_{i-j}
, where we use the convention
\theta_{k}:=\theta_{-k}
for
k\leq 0
.
The matrix
T(\theta)
is said to be Tœplitz Robinson if the entries of
\theta
are non-increasing.

Given a permutation
\pi^{*}
in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we write
P(\pi^{*})
for the corresponding permutation matrix. In this seriation model, we observe a
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
Y=X+E
where the matrix
E
is made of independent standard normal entries and

X=P(\pi^{*})T(\theta)P(\pi^{*})

(58)

with
T(\theta)
is a possibly unknown Tœplitz-Robinson matrix, and
P(\pi^{*})
is an unknown permutation matrix to be estimated. The permutation
P(\pi^{*})
is not identifiable:
P(\pi^{*})
and the corresponding reverse permutation
P(\pi^{*})_{-}
are such that
P(\pi^{*})T(\theta)P(\pi^{*})^{T}=P(\pi)_{-}^{*}T(\theta)P(\pi^{*})_{-}^{T}
. Hence, the main goal in seriation is to recover a permutation
\pi
such that
X=P(\pi)T(\theta)P(\pi)^{T}
.

Given a positive integer
\rho
and
\lambda>0
, we defined the vector
\theta_{\lambda,\rho}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
(\theta_{\lambda,\rho})_{i}=\lambda\mathbf{1}_{i\leq\rho}
. Note that the model (
15
) introduced in Section
4
corresponds to
X=P(\pi^{*})T(\theta_{\lambda,k})P(\pi^{*})

One way of quantifying the error of a given permutation matrix
\Pi
is the
\ell_{2}
loss which quantifies to what extent

X
is close in Frobenius distance to a Robinson matrix ordered by
\pi
.

\ell_{2}(\pi):=\inf_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}}\|X-P(\pi)RP(\pi)^{T}\|_{F}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is the collection of Robinson matrices, that is the collection of symmetric matrices
R
such, for any
i<j
, we have
R_{i,j}\leq R_{i+1,j}
and
R_{i,j}\geq R_{i,j+1}
. Equivalently,
R
is a Robinson matrix if its entries are non-increasing when one moves away from the diagonal.

It is established in
[
BCV24
]
that the minimax rate in
\ell^{2}_{2}
for the seriation problem is of the order of
n
. In contrast, the best known polynomial-time procedures
[
BCV24
]
only achieves a rate of the order of
n^{3/2}
. To provide evidence of the optimality of the
n^{3/2}
for polynomial-time procedures,
[
BCV24
]
also provide a nearly matching LD polynomial lower bound but their model was quite different as
\pi^{*}
was not necessarily a permutation. Here, we strengthen this evidence by deriving a computational
n^{3/2}
lower bound in the model (
58
).

As for clustering, the problem of estimating a permutation
\hat{\pi}
is of combinatorial nature and in order to state a LD lower bounds, we reduce it to the problem of estimating the matrix
X=P(\pi^{*})T(\lambda,\rho)P(\pi^{*})^{T}
.

The following lemma is a slight adaptation of Lemma 8 in
[
BCV24
]
–see Section
H.1
for a proof.

Lemma G.1

(Reduction to matrix estimation)
.

Consider any
\lambda>0
, positive integer
\rho
and any estimator
\widehat{\pi}
.
Define the matrix
\hat{X}
by
\hat{X}_{i,j}=\lambda/2
if
|\hat{\pi}^{-1}(i)-\hat{\pi}^{-1}(j)|\leq\rho
and
\hat{X}_{i,j}=0
otherwise. Then, we have

\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})\geq\frac{1}{4}\left[\left[\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]-\lambda^{2}\rho n\right]

(59)

As a consequence of this lemma, if all polynomial-time estimators
\hat{X}
of
X
satisfy
\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}]\geq\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{2}\rho n
, then no polynomial-time estimators of
\hat{\pi}
satisfy

\mathbb{E}[\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})]\geq c\lambda^{2}\rho n
for some
c>0
. Hence, to establish the hardness of achieving
\mathbb{E}[\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})]\preccurlyeq_{\log(n)}n^{3/2}
, it suffices to prove that, for
\rho=\lceil\sqrt{n}\rceil
and
\lambda
such that
1/\lambda
is a polylogarithmic term, one has
\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}]\geq\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{2}\rho n
. The latter precisely corresponding to LD lower bound in Theorem
4.1
.

In particular, taking
D\asymp\log(n)
,
\rho\asymp\sqrt{n}
,
\lambda
as the inverse of a polylogarithmic term, we deduce from that theorem that

MMSE_{\leq\log(n)}\geq\lambda^{2}\frac{3\rho}{2}n\succcurlyeq_{\log(n)}n^{3/2}\ .

Together with Lemma
G.1
, this suggests the hardness of achieving seriation with a
\ell_{2}^{2}
rate smaller than
n^{3/2}
.

Appendix H
Remaining proofs

H.1
Seriation

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

The first result straightforwardly derives from the proof of Theorem 4 in
[
BCV24
]
. Translating the third line of that proof with our notation, we deduce that there exists an event
\mathcal{A}
of probability higher than
1-4/n
such that

\|X_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}n+\lambda n^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}

Since
\|X_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}n^{2}
, we deduce that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|X_{\hat{\pi}}-X\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq 5\lambda^{2}n+\lambda n^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(n)}\ .

Let us turn to the second result. For a fixed
(i,j)
, it follows from a standard control of the Gaussian tail distribution that
(\hat{X}_{th})_{ij}=X_{ij}
with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{E}[((\hat{X}_{th})_{ij}-X_{ij})^{2}]=\lambda^{2}/n^{2}
. The result follows by linearity.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

\|Y-\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|Y-X\|^{2}_{F}
implies that
\|X-\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}\|_{F}^{2}+2\langle E,X-\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}\rangle\leq 0
. Let us apply an union bound of the random variable
\langle E,\hat{X}_{\pi}-X\rangle
over all
n!
matrices of the form
X_{\pi}
.
Hence, with probability higher than
1-e^{-x}
, we have
-\langle E,X-\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}\rangle\leq\sqrt{2[n\log(n)+x]}\|X-\hat{X}_{\hat{pi}}\|_{F}
. On this event, we have
\|X-\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}}\|^{2}_{F}\leq 8[n\log(n)+x]
. The result follows by integrating with respect to
x
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
G.1
.

The proof closely follows that of Lemma 8 in
[
BCV24
]
. Still, we provide here a complete proof as
the notation and the conventions slightly differ from
[
BCV24
]
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
\pi^{*}
is the identity permutation.
If
|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|\leq 2\rho
, we have
|\hat{X}_{ij}-X_{ij}|=\lambda/2
. Hence, it follows that

\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}\rho n+\lambda^{2}\left|\{(i,j):X_{ij}=\lambda\text{ and }|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho\}\right|

(60)

We bound the RHS of (
59
) using the loss
\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})
. For that purpose, let us introduce
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}
the collection of
n\times n
matrices whose rows are unimodal and achieve their maximum on the diagonal. Obviously
\mathcal{R}_{n}\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}
and so
\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})\geq\inf_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}}\|X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}\|_{F}^{2}
. Besides, we introduce
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}(\lambda)
as the subset of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}
that only take its values in
\{0,\lambda\}
outside the diagonal. For
R\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}
, let us introduce
R_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(\lambda)
defined by
\left[R_{0}\right]_{ij}=\lambda\mathbf{1}\left\{R_{ij}\leq\lambda/2\right\}
. Since
X
takes its values on
\left\{0,\lambda\right\}
, we have that
\|X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}\|_{F}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\|X-P(\hat{\pi})R_{0}P(\hat{\pi})^{T}\|_{F}^{2}
. We deduce

\ell^{2}_{2}(\hat{\pi})\geq\inf_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}}\|X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}\|_{F}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\inf_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}(\lambda)}\|X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}\|_{F}^{2}.

(61)

Let
R
denote any matrix in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{n}(\lambda)
that achieves the above infimum. Fix any
i\in[n]
. We claim that

\displaystyle\frac{\|[X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}]_{i}\|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\left|\{j:|i-j|\leq\rho\text{ and }|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho\}\right|,

(62)

where
[X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}]_{i}
stands for the
i
-th row ot the matrix. Before showing this claim, let us conclude the proof. Since

\left|\{j:|i-j|\leq\rho\text{ and }|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho\}\right|=\left|\{j:X_{i,j}=\lambda\text{ and }|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho\}\right|\kern 5.0pt,

it follows from
(
60
) and (
62
) that

\|\hat{X}-X\|_{F}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}\rho n+4\ell^{2}_{2}(P(\hat{\pi}))\ ,

which is precisely the desired bound.

Let us now show (
62
). Without loss of generality, we can assume that the
j
’s such that
|i-j|\leq\rho
and
|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho
are such that
\hat{\pi}(j)>\hat{\pi}(i)+2\rho
. We consider two cases: (i)

R_{\hat{\pi}(i),\hat{\pi}(i)+2\rho+1}=0
. For all
j
such that
|i-j|\leq\rho
and
|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|>2\rho
, we have
[X-P(\hat{\pi})RP(\hat{\pi})^{T}]_{ij}=\lambda
. Then, this straightforwardly implies (
61
). Now, suppose (ii) that
R_{\hat{\pi}(i),\hat{\pi}(i)+2\rho+1}=\lambda
. There exists at least
\left|\{j:|i-j|\leq\rho\text{ and }|\hat{\pi}(i)-\hat{\pi}(j)|\geq 2\rho\}\right|
indices
j
such that
\hat{\pi}(j)\in(\hat{\pi}(i),\hat{\pi}(i)+2\rho]
and
|i-j|>\rho
. Since, for a such a
j
, we have
X_{i,j}=0
and
R_{\hat{\pi}(i),\hat{\pi}(j)}=\lambda
, we again derive (
62
).
∎

H.2
Proof of Proposition
5.1

This proof is adapted from the proof of Proposition A.3 of
[
EGV25
]
. Let us consider a regime where
n,K
go to infinity (and possibly
p
) and where
K=o(n)
. Given a partition
G=G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}
such that
|G_{k}|=n/K
for all
k\in[K]
, we define the partnership matrix
\Gamma^{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by
\Gamma^{G}_{i,j}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathbf{1}\left\{i,j\in G_{k}\right\}
. We write
\Gamma^{*}
for
G^{*}
. Then,

n(n-1)MMSE_{poly}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]-corr_{poly}^{2}=n^{2}/K-corr_{poly}^{2},

where we define

corr_{poly}^{2}:=\sup_{\hat{\Gamma}\ poly-time,\ \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\Gamma^{*},\hat{\Gamma}\rangle_{F}\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
MMSE_{poly}=1/K(1+o(1))
, it follows that
corr_{poly}^{2}=o(n^{2}/K)
. For
\hat{G}
a polynomial time estimator of
G^{*}
with prescribed group size
n/K
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\Gamma^{\hat{G}},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\right]\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]}\ corr_{poly}=\sqrt{n^{2}\over K}\ corr_{poly}

since
\|\Gamma^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}=n^{2}/K
almost surely. We deduce

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{\hat{G}}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=

\displaystyle 2n^{2}/K-2\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\Gamma^{\hat{G}},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2n^{2}/K-\sqrt{n^{2}/K}\ corr_{poly}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2n^{2}/K(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude the proof of the proposition with the following lemma.

Lemma H.1
.

For any
G
with prescribed group size
n/K
, we have

\left[1-err(G,G^{*})\right]^{2}\leq 1-\frac{K\|\Gamma^{G}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}}{2n^{2}}
.

Proof of Lemma
H.1
.

With no loss of generality, we assume that the permutation
\psi
in the definition of
err(G,G^{*})
is the identity. Thus,

err(G,G^{*})=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\Delta G^{*}_{k}|=1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|\ ,

which implies that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|=n[1-err(G,G^{*})]
. We have, for
G,G^{*}
with group size
n/K
, that

\|\Gamma^{G}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\Gamma^{G}\|^{2}_{F}+\|\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}-2\langle\Gamma^{G},\Gamma^{*}\rangle_{F}=2n^{2}/K-2\langle\Gamma^{G},\Gamma^{*}\rangle_{F}\ .

Furthermore,

\langle\Gamma^{G},\Gamma^{*}\rangle_{F}=\sum_{ij\in[n]}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in[K]}\mathbf{1}\left\{i,j\in G_{k}\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{i,j\in G_{k}^{*}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

For
k\in[K]
, let us denote
N_{k}=\left|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}\right|
. Then,

\langle\Gamma^{G},\Gamma^{*}\rangle_{F}\geq\sum_{k\in[K]}N_{k}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{K}\left[\sum_{k\in[K]}N_{k}\right]^{2}\geq n^{2}\left[1-err(G,G^{*})\right]^{2}/K\kern 5.0pt,

where we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the second inequality. We conclude that

\|\Gamma^{G}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2{n^{2}\over K}\left(1-\left[1-err(G,G^{*})\right]^{2}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

∎

H.3
Proof of Proposition
2.1

This proof is also adapted from the proof of Proposition A.3 of
[
EGV25
]
and is similar to the proof of Proposition
5.1
. Let us consider a regime where
M,K
go to infinity (and possibly
p
). Given a
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi=\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{M}
, we define the partnership matrix
\Gamma^{\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K\times M)^{2}}
by
\Gamma^{\pi}_{(k,m),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})}=\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi_{m}(k)=\pi_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\right\}
. We write
\Gamma^{*}
for
\pi^{*}
. Then,

(M(M-1))K^{2}MMSE_{poly}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]-corr_{poly}^{2}=KM^{2}-corr_{poly}^{2},

where we define

corr_{poly}^{2}:=\sup_{\hat{\Gamma}\ poly-time,\ \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{\Gamma}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\Gamma^{*},\hat{\Gamma}\rangle_{F}\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
MMSE_{poly}=1/K(1+o(1))
, it follows that
corr_{poly}^{2}=o(M^{2}K)
. For
\hat{\pi}\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}
a polynomial time estimator of
\pi^{*}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\Gamma^{\hat{\pi}},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\right]\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{\hat{\pi}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]}\ corr_{poly}=\sqrt{KM^{2}}\ corr_{poly}

since
\|\Gamma^{\hat{\pi}}\|^{2}_{F}=KM^{2}
almost surely. We deduce

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\Gamma^{\hat{\pi}}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=

\displaystyle 2KM^{2}-2\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\Gamma^{\hat{\pi}},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2KM^{2}-\sqrt{KM^{2}}\ corr_{poly}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2KM^{2}(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude the proof of the proposition with the following lemma.

Lemma H.2
.

For any
\pi\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}
, we have

\left[1-err(\pi,\pi^{*})\right]^{2}\leq 1-\frac{\|\Gamma^{\pi}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}}{2KM^{2}}
.

Proof of Lemma
H.2
.

With no loss of generality, we can suppose that the permutation
\psi
minimizing the definition of the error
err(\pi,\pi^{*})
is the identity and thus

err(\pi,\pi^{*})=\frac{1}{KM}\sum_{k,m}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi_{m}(k)\neq\pi^{*}_{m}(k)\right\}=1-\frac{1}{KM}\sum_{k,m}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi_{m}(k)=\pi^{*}_{m}(k)\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

And, we also have

\|\Gamma^{\pi}-\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\Gamma^{\pi}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}-2\langle\Gamma^{\pi},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

We can directly compute
\|\Gamma^{\pi}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\Gamma^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=KM^{2}
. For the crossed-product term, we expand

\langle\Gamma^{\pi},\Gamma^{*}\rangle=\sum_{(k,m),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi_{m}(k)=\pi_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi^{*}_{m}(k)=\pi^{*}_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

For
k\in[K]
, let us denote
N_{k}=\left|\left\{(k^{\prime},m)\in[K]\times[M],\pi_{m}(k^{\prime})=k\\
\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{*}_{m}(k^{\prime})=k\right\}\right|
. We get

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma^{\pi},\Gamma^{*}\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}N_{k}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{K}\left[\sum_{k\in[K]}N_{k}\right]^{2}\geq KM^{2}\left[1-err(\pi,\pi^{*})\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the second inequality. We conclude that

\|\Gamma^{\pi}-\Gamma^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\leq 2KM^{2}-2KM^{2}\left[1-err(\pi,\pi^{*})\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

H.4
Proof of Corollary
2.3

We write
G^{*}
the partition of
[K]\times[M]
defined by
G_{k}^{*}=\left\{(k^{\prime},m)\in[K]\times[M],\kern 5.0pt\pi^{*}_{m}(k^{\prime})=k\right\}
. We remark that
G^{*}
is a balanced partition of
[K]\times[M]
into
K
groups and that
Y^{(m)}_{k^{\prime}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{k},\sigma^{2}I_{p}\right)
.

1.

Using Proposition 4 of
[
EGV24
]
, we know that a single linkage hierarchical procedure is able to recover exactly
G^{*}
with high probability as soon as
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(KM)+\sqrt{p\log(KM)}
;

2.

Using Theorem 1 of
[
GV19
]
, we know that a SDP relaxation procedure is able to recover exactly
G^{*}
in high dimension
p\geq MK
as soon as
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}
;

3.

Using Proposition 3.2 of
[
EGV25
]
, we deduce that, with a method based on spectral projections together with the low-dimensional clustering procedure from
[
LL22
]
, we are able to recover exactly
G^{*}
with high probability when
M\geq K^{c}
, for some numerical constant
c
, and
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}1+\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}
;

4.

Still using Proposition 3.2 of
[
EGV25
]
, we know that, with a method based on spectral projections together with a single linkage hierarchical procedure, we are able to recover exactly
G^{*}
with high probability when
M\gtrsim K
,
MK\geq p\geq M
and
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}1+\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}
.

All the methods that are used for these four points are computable in polynomial time. Hence, combining those results, we deduce that, except when both
M\in[K,K^{c}]
and
p\in[\log(KM),\frac{p}{KM}]
, there exists an efficient algorithm
\hat{G}
which recovers exactly with high probability
G^{*}
as soon as
\Delta^{2}\geq_{\log}1+\min\left(\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\frac{pK}{M}}\right)
. We define the estimator
\hat{\pi}
by
\hat{\pi}_{m}(k^{\prime})=k
if
(k^{\prime},m)\in\hat{G}_{k}
. On the event where
\hat{G}=G^{*}
,
\hat{\pi}
is a
M
-tuple of permutations on
[K]
which satisfies
err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})=err(\hat{G},G^{*})=0
. This concludes the proof of the corollary.

H.5
Sketch of proof of Proposition
A.1

This proof is directly adapted from the proof of Theorem 2 of
[
EGV24
]
for exact
K
-means and we mainly explain the key steps. We view
Y
as a matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{KM\times p}
. Given
\pi\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}
, let
B(\pi)
the normalized partnership matrix
B(\pi)\in\mathbb{R}^{KM\times KM}
defined
\left(B(\pi)\right)_{(k,m),(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{M}\mathbf{1}\left\{\pi_{m}(k)=\pi_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\right\}
. As noticed by
[
PW07
]
, minimizing Criterion (
21
) is equivalent to maximizing the quantity
\langle YY^{T},B(\pi)\rangle
. Let
\mathcal{B}:=\{B(\pi),\pi\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}\}
and let

\hat{B}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{B\in\mathcal{B}}\langle YY^{T},B\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

\mathcal{B}
is a subset of the set
\mathcal{C}=\{B\in S_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}:\ B_{ij}\geq 0,\mathrm{Tr}(B)=K,B1=1,B^{2}=B\}
which is the set of normalized partnership matrices associated to partitions
G
of
[KM]
into
K
groups. In particular, all the inequalities from
[
EGV24
]
which are valid on
\mathcal{C}
are in fact valid on
\mathcal{B}
. In the following, we write
B^{*}
for
B(\pi^{*})
. Let us consider;

•

A\in\mathbb{R}^{KM\times K}
the membership matrix defined by
A_{(k^{\prime},m),k}=\mathbf{1}_{\pi^{*}_{m}(k^{\prime})=k}
,

•

E\in\mathbb{R}^{KM\times p}=Y-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y]
the noise matrix,

•

\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times p}
whose
k
-th row is
\mu_{k}
.

By definition of
\hat{B}
, we have
\langle YY^{T},\hat{B}-B^{*}\rangle\geq 0
. Decomposing
Y
with the relation
Y=A\mu+E
, we get

\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-\hat{B}\rangle\leq\langle A\mu E^{T}+E(A\mu)^{T},\hat{B}-B^{*}\rangle+\langle EE^{T}-pI_{MK},\hat{B}-B^{*}\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

Given a matrix
A
, we write
\|A\|_{1}
for the sum of the absolute values of its entries.
For any
M
-tuple of permutation, the error
err(\pi,\pi^{*})
is linked to the norm
\|B^{*}-B^{*}B(\pi)\|_{1}

through the inequality
err(\pi,\pi^{*})\lesssim\frac{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B(\pi)\|_{1}}{KM}
(see Lemma 9 of
[
EGV24
]
). Building on this bound;

1.

One can lower-bound the signal term
\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle
with respect to
\frac{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}}{KM}
. Using Lemma 4 from
[
GV19
]
which is valid on a set containing
\mathcal{C}
and thus
\mathcal{B}
, we get that, for all
\pi\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{K}\right)^{M}
,
\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-B(\pi)\rangle\gtrsim\Delta^{2}\|B^{*}-B^{*}B(\pi)\|_{1}\gtrsim KM\Delta^{2}err(\pi,\pi^{*})
.

2.

Directly plugging Lemma 11 of
[
EGV24
]
, we deduce that with high probability, simultaneously on all
B\in\mathcal{B}
,

\displaystyle|\langle EE^{T}-pI_{KM},B^{*}-B\rangle|\lesssim

\displaystyle\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}\left(\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(K)}\right)

\displaystyle+\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}})}+\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}})\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, the first term of this some is smaller than one half of the signal term when
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(K)}
.

3.

Finally, using Lemma 12 of
[
EGV24
]
, we get that, with high probability, simultaneously on all
B\in\mathcal{B}
,

\langle A\mu E^{T}+E(A\mu)^{T},B-B^{*}\rangle\lesssim\sqrt{\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle}\sqrt{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}})}\kern 5.0pt.

In the following, let us denote
\hat{\delta}=\|B^{*}-B^{*}\hat{B}\|_{1}
. Combining those three points together, we get that, whenever
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(K)}
, with high probability,

\displaystyle\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-\hat{B}\rangle\lesssim

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\hat{\delta}})}+\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\hat{\delta}})\right)+\sqrt{\langle A\mu(A\mu)^{T},B^{*}-\hat{B}\rangle}\sqrt{\hat{\delta}\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\hat{\delta}})}\kern 5.0pt,

which, in turn, implies,

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}\hat{\delta}\lesssim

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}\left(\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\hat{\delta}})+\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(\frac{MK^{4}}{\hat{\delta}})}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

and so

\hat{\delta}\lesssim MK^{4}\exp\left(-c\left(\Delta^{2}\wedge\frac{M\Delta^{4}}{p}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with
c
some numerical constant. Finally, we end up with

err(\hat{\pi},\pi^{*})\lesssim\frac{\hat{\delta}}{KM}\lesssim K^{3}\exp\left(-c\left(\Delta^{2}\wedge\frac{M\Delta^{4}}{p}\right)\right)\leq\exp\left(-c^{\prime}\left(\Delta^{2}\wedge\frac{\Delta^{4}M}{p}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality holds for some numerical constant
c^{\prime}
if
\Delta^{2}\gtrsim\log(K)+\sqrt{\frac{p\log(K)}{M}}
. This concludes the (sketch of) proof.

H.6
Proof of Theorem
A.2
; Perfect Recovery

This section is dedicated to proving the first point of Theorem
A.2
. Without loss of generality, we suppose throughout this proof that
\sigma=1
. We suppose that
K\geq K_{0}
with
K_{0}
a constant that we will choose large enough.

To prove the first point of Theorem
A.2
, we will distinguish two cases. First, we will prove that there exist positive numerical constants
c_{1}
and
C
such that, when
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{1}\log(KM)
, we have

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\mu\in\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0)>C\kern 5.0pt.

Second, we will show that there exists a numerical constant
c_{2}
such that this still holds when
c_{1}\log(KM)\leq\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}.

H.6.1
Case
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{1}\log(KM)
.

Let us suppose that
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{1}\log(KM)
for
c_{1}
a positive numerical constant that we will choose small enough later. Let
e
be a unit vector of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and define
\mu_{k}=\sqrt{2}k\bar{\Delta}e
for
k\in[1,K]
. To prove our statement, we will use Fano’s Lemma that we recall here (see e.g p 57 of
[
Gir21
]
).

Lemma H.3

(Fano’s Lemma)
.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{s})_{s\in[\ell]}
be a set of probability distributions on some set
\mathcal{Y}
. For any probability distribution
\mathbb{Q}
such that for all
s\in[\ell]
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{s}<<\mathbb{Q}
,

\min_{\hat{J}:\mathcal{Y}\to[\ell]}\frac{1}{\ell}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{s}\left(\hat{J}(Y)\neq s\right)\geq 1-\frac{1+\frac{1}{\ell}\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{s},\mathbb{Q})}{\log(\ell)}\kern 5.0pt,

where we recall that
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{Q})=\int\log\left(\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}}{d\mathbb{Q}}\right)d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}
stands for the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

Let us consider the distribution
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id}
as reference distribution for applying Lemma
H.3
. Our goal will be to find different
M
-tuples of permutations
\pi^{(1)},...,\pi^{(\ell)}
, with
\ell
as large as possible, such that
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s)}},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id})
remains small for all
s\in[\ell]
. To do so, for
s\leq\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor
and
m\in[M]
, we define
\pi^{(s,m)}
the
M
-tuple of permutations defined as follows. If
m^{\prime}\neq m
, then
\pi^{(s,m)}_{m^{\prime}}=id
, and
\pi^{(s,m)}_{m}=(2s-1,2s)
where
(2s-1,2s)
stands for the transposition of
2s-1
and
2s
.

For
s\leq\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor
and
m\in[M]
, let us compute
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id})
. Given
m^{\prime}\in[M]
and
k\in[K]
, we denote
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)};k,m^{\prime}}
the marginal distribution of the vector
Y_{k}^{(m^{\prime})}
under
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}
. Using the independence of the
Y_{k}^{(m^{\prime})}
’s for
k\in[K]
and
m^{\prime}\in[M]
, we get

\displaystyle KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{i=K}\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{M}KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)};k,m^{\prime}},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id;k,m^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle KL\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)};2s-1,m},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id;2s-1,m}\right)+KL\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)};2s,m},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,id;2s,m}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle KL\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2s-1},I_{p}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2s},I_{p})\right)+KL\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2s},I_{p}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2s-1},I_{p})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq 2c_{1}\log(KM)\kern 5.0pt.

For any estimator
\hat{\pi}
, we associate
\hat{j}
the estimator that gives
(s,m)
if
\hat{\pi}=\pi^{(s,m)}
and
(1,1)
otherwise. Fano’s Lemma (Lemma
H.3
) implies that, for all estimators
\hat{\pi}
, the corresponding estimator
\hat{j}
satisfies

\frac{1}{M\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{(s,m)}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}(\hat{j}\neq(s,m))\geq 1-\frac{1+2c_{1}\log(KM)}{\log(M\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor)}\kern 5.0pt.

If
c_{1}
is small enough and
K_{0}
large enough, there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all integers
K\geq K_{0}
and
M\geq 2
, we have
1-\frac{1+2c_{1}\log(KM)}{\log(M\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor)}\geq C.

Since, for any estimator
\hat{\pi}
and its corresponding estimator
\hat{j}
, for any
s\leq\lfloor K/2\rfloor
and
m\leq M
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}(\hat{\pi}=\pi^{(s,m)})\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}(\hat{j}=(s,m))
, we get

\frac{1}{M\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{(s,m)}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}(\hat{\pi}\neq\pi^{(s,m)})\geq C\kern 5.0pt.

This, with the fact that, for all estimator
\hat{\pi}
,

\sup_{\mu\in\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(\hat{\pi}\neq\pi)\geq\frac{1}{M\lfloor\frac{K}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{(s,m)}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi^{(s,m)}}(\hat{\pi}\neq\pi^{(s,m)})

concludes the proof of our statement in the regime where
\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{1}\log(KM)
.

H.6.2
Case
c_{1}\log(KM)\leq\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}
.

We suppose that

c_{1}\log(KM)\leq\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}\kern 5.0pt,

(63)

with
c_{1}
defined just above and
c_{2}
that we will choose small enough. Given
\rho
a probability distribution on
(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{K}
and a
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
, we define the probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}
by

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}(A)=\int\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(A)d\rho(\mu)\kern 5.0pt.

In this regime, we shall use the following lemma.

Lemma H.4
.

We suppose that there exists a probability distribution
\rho
on
(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{K}
and
a>0
such that

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}(err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0)-\rho(\mathbb{R}^{p}\setminus\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}})>a\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we have

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\mu\in\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0)>a\kern 5.0pt.

We refer to Section
H.6.4
for a proof of this lemma. We will consider the following distribution on
(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{K}
. Define
\varepsilon=2\sqrt{\frac{1}{p}}\bar{\Delta}
and define
\rho
the uniform distribution on the hypercube
\mathcal{E}=\{-\varepsilon,\varepsilon\}^{p\times K}
. We will use Fano’s Lemma to lower bound
\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}(err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0)
. To do so, we need to find many
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(\ell)}\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
, with
\ell
large, such that
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi^{(s)}},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})
remains small for all
s\in[\ell]
.

Given
m\in[M]
,
k<k^{\prime}\in[K]
, we define
\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}
the
M
-tuple of permutations that satisfies; for all
m^{\prime}\neq m
,
\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}_{m^{\prime}}=id
, and
\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}_{m}
is the transposition
(k,k^{\prime})
. We denote
V
the set of all these
M
-tuple of permutation;
V=\{\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})},\kern 5.0ptm\in[M-1],\kern 5.0ptk<k^{\prime}\in[K]\}
. For a
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi\in V
, let us compute the quantity
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})=\int\log(\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}})d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}
. We refer to Section
H.6.3
for a proof of the next lemma.

Lemma H.5
.

We suppose that the numerical constant
c_{2}
is small enough with respect to
c_{1}
, where both
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
arise in (
63
). Then, there exists a numerical constant
c>0
such that, for all
m,k,k^{\prime}
with
m\in[M]
,
k<k^{\prime}\in[K]
, we have

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})=\int\log(\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}})d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}}\leq cc_{2}^{2}\log(KM)\kern 5.0pt.

This inequality combined with Lemma
H.3
applied to the set
V
of all the
M
-tuple of permutations
\pi^{(m,k,k^{\prime})}
leads to

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{{}^{K\times M\times p}}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{\pi\in V}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}[\hat{\pi}\neq\pi]\geq 1-\frac{1+cc_{2}^{2}\log(Km)}{\log(|V|)}\kern 5.0pt.

For all estimator
\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}
, let us define
\psi_{\hat{\pi}}\in\mathcal{S}_{K}
the permutation on
[K]
such that
\psi_{\hat{\pi}}\circ\hat{\pi}_{M}=id
. We remark that, for any
\pi\in V
, having
err(\hat{\pi},\pi)=0
is equivalent to having
\pi=\psi_{\hat{\pi}}
. Thus,

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{\pi\in V}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}[err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0]\geq 1-\frac{1+cc_{2}^{2}\log(Km)}{\log(|V|)}\kern 5.0pt,

and so

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,\rho}[err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0]\geq 1-\frac{1+cc_{2}^{2}\log(Km)}{\log(|V|)}\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, the fact that
\log(|V|)=\log(K(K-1)M/2)
implies that, if we choose
c_{2}
small enough and if
K_{0}
is large enough, we will dispose of a numerical constant
b>0
satisfying

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\frac{1}{|V|}\sum_{\pi\in V}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}[err(\hat{\pi},\pi)\neq 0]\geq b\kern 5.0pt.

In order to apply Lemma
G.1
, it remains to control
\rho(\left(\mathbb{R}^{p}\right)^{K}\setminus\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}})
. From Lemma 14 of
[
CD16
]
, we get that
\rho(\left(\mathbb{R}^{p}\right)^{K}\setminus\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}})\leq\frac{K(K-1)}{2}e^{\frac{-p}{8}}
. Moreover,
c_{1}\log(KM)\leq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}
implies
p\geq\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{c_{2}^{2}}\log(KM)
. Thus,
\rho(\mathbb{R}^{p}\setminus\Theta_{\bar{\Delta}})\leq\frac{b}{2}
, provided
c_{2}
is small enough compared to
c_{1}
. Hence, Lemma
H.4
gives

\inf_{\hat{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{K\times M\times p}\to(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\sup_{\pi\in(\mathcal{S}_{K})^{M}}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}[\hat{\pi}\neq\pi]\geq\frac{b}{2}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of the first point of Theorem
A.2
(perfect recovery).

H.6.3
Proof of Lemma
H.5

By symmetry, it is sufficient to bound the Kullback-Leibler discrepansy for
\pi=\pi^{(1,1,2)}=\left((1,2),id,\ldots,id\right)
. In the following, we denote
\rho^{\prime}
the probability distribution on
(\mathbb{R})^{p\times K}
that satisfies; if
(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K})\sim\rho^{\prime}
, all the
\mu_{i}
’s are independent,
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
and all the other
\mu_{i}
’s are drawn uniformly on the set
\{\varepsilon,-\varepsilon\}^{p}
.

First, we will compute the quantity

\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}=\frac{\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}}}{\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}}}=\frac{\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}}}{\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},id}}}\kern 5.0pt,

(64)

where the second equality comes from the fact that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},id}=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}
. Given a probability law
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}
on some Euclidean space, we denote
d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}
the density of this law with respect to the Lebesgue measure on this space (when
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure). For the numerator in (
64
), we have,

\displaystyle\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}}(Y)=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(Y)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left[d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\mu,\pi}(Y)\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{k\in[1,K]}\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k)}-\mu_{k}\|^{2}\right)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{k\in[1,K]}\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k)}-\mu_{k}\|^{2}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{d\in[1,p]}\prod_{k\in[K]}\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|X^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d}\|^{2}\right)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{d\in[1,p]}\prod_{k\in[K]}\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d}\|^{2}\right)\right]}\kern 5.0pt.

Using the independence of the
\mu_{k,d}
’s both for the law
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
together with the fact that, when
k>3
,
\mu_{k,d}
has the same law under
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}}(Y)=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}\prod_{k\in[K]}\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d})^{2}\right)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d})^{2}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}\prod_{k\in\{1,2\}}\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d})^{2}\right)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d})^{2}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}\prod_{k\in\{1,2\}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left((Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}-\mu_{k,d})^{2}-(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{k}^{-1}(k),d})^{2}\right)\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}\prod_{k\in\{1,2\}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\prod_{m\in[M]}\exp{\left(Y^{(m)}_{\pi_{m}^{-1}(k),d}\mu_{k,d}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}e^{\frac{-M\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}\cosh\left[\varepsilon\left(Y^{(1)}_{2,d}+\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}\right)\right]\cdot\prod_{d\in[p]}e^{\frac{-M\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}\cosh\left[\varepsilon\left(Y^{(1)}_{1,d}+\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where the third equality comes from the fact that, for all
d\in[1,p]
and when
k\leq 2
,
\mu_{k,d}=0
almost surely under the law
\rho^{\prime}
. Similarly, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho^{\prime},id}}(Y)=

\displaystyle\prod_{d\in[p]}e^{\frac{-M\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}\cosh{\left(\sum_{m\in[M]}\varepsilon Y^{(m)}_{1,d}\right)}\cdot\prod_{d\in[p]}e^{\frac{-M\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}\cosh{\left(\sum_{m\in[M]}\varepsilon Y^{(m)}_{2,d}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining these two equalities in (
64
) leads to

\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}=\frac{\prod_{d\in[p]}\cosh{\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}+\varepsilon Y^{(1)}_{2,d}\right)}\cosh{\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}+\varepsilon Y^{(1)}_{1,d}\right)}}{\prod_{d\in[p]}\cosh{\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}\right)}\cosh{\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}\right)}}\kern 5.0pt.

We denote
\phi
the standard Gaussian density
\phi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{\frac{-x^{2}}{2}}
. Under the law
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}
, conditionally on
\mu\sim\rho
, we have that:

•

\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(M)}_{1,d}\sim\mathcal{N}((M-1)\mu_{1,d}+\mu_{2,d},M)
,

•

\sum_{M\in[M]}Y^{(M)}_{2,d}\sim\mathcal{N}((M-1)\mu_{2,d}+\mu_{1,d},M)
,

•

\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}+Y^{(1)}_{2,d}\sim\mathcal{N}(M\mu_{1,d},M)
,

•

\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}+Y^{(1)}_{1,d}\sim\mathcal{N}(M\mu_{2,d},M)
.

This leads to

\displaystyle KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})=

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\left(\frac{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi}}{d\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d\in[p]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}+\varepsilon Y^{(1)}_{1,d}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{d\in[p]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}+\varepsilon Y^{(1)}_{2,d}\right)\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{d\in[p]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,d}\right)\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{d\in[p]}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{2,d}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2p\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[2,M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,1}+Y^{(1)}_{2,1}\right)\right]-2p\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho,\pi}\left[\log\cosh\left(\varepsilon\sum_{m\in[M]}Y^{(m)}_{1,1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2p\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(\varepsilon(M\mu_{1,1}+\sqrt{M}x))\phi(x)dx\right]

\displaystyle-2p\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(\varepsilon((M-1)\mu_{1,1}+\mu_{2,1}+\sqrt{M}x))\phi(x)dx\right]\kern 5.0pt.

First, let us upper-bound the term

A:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(\varepsilon(M\mu_{1,1}+\sqrt{M}x))\phi(x)dx\right]\kern 5.0pt.

We denote
u=\varepsilon\left((M-1)\mu_{1,1}+\sqrt{M}x\right)
and
h=\varepsilon\mu_{1,1}
. Then,
A=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(u+h)\phi(x)dx\right]\kern 5.0pt.
We will use the Taylor expansion of the function
\log\cosh
around
u
. We compute the following derivatives:

•

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\log\cosh^{\prime}(x)=\tanh(x)
,

•

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\log\cosh^{\prime\prime}(x)=1-\tanh^{2}(x)
which is bounded by
2
in absolute value.

Hence, Taylor-Lagrange inequality implies

|\log\cosh(x+y)-\log\cosh(x)-\tanh(x)y|\leq y^{2},\kern 5.0pt\forall(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

(65)

Plugging this inequality leads to

A=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(u+h)\phi(x)dx\right]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\log\cosh(u)\right]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\tanh{(u)}h\phi(x)dx\right]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}(h^{2})\kern 5.0pt.

(66)

First, since
h^{2}=\varepsilon^{4}
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}(h^{2})=\varepsilon^{4}
. Now, we need to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\tanh{(u)}h\phi(x)dx\right]
. For any
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\tanh^{\prime}(y)=1-tanh^{2}(y)
and
\tanh^{\prime\prime}(y)=-2\tanh(y)(1-\tanh(y)^{2})
, which is bounded by
4
in absolute value. Hence, Taylor-Lagrange inequality taken at
0
leads to

|\tanh(y)-y|\leq 2y^{2}\kern 5.0pt,\forall y\in\mathbb{R}\kern 5.0pt.

This leads us to
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\tanh{(u)}h\phi(x)dx\right]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int uh\phi(x)dx\right]+2\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int u^{2}|h|\phi(x)dx\right]
. On the one hand,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int uh\phi(x)dx\right]=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int((M-1)\mu_{1,1}+\sqrt{M}x)\mu_{1,1}\phi(x)dx\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[(M-1)\mu_{1,1}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(M-1)\varepsilon^{4}\kern 5.0pt.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}[\int u^{2}|h|\phi(x)dx]=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}[\int((M-1)\mu_{1,1}+\sqrt{M}x)^{2}\phi(x)dx]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}\left((M-1)^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+M\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging all these inequalities in (
66
) leads us to

A\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\log\cosh(u)\right]+(M-1)\varepsilon^{4}+2\varepsilon^{4}\left((M-1)^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+M\right)+\varepsilon^{4}\kern 5.0pt.

(67)

Now, let us lower-bound the term

B:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(\varepsilon(m\mu_{1,1}+\mu_{2,1}+\sqrt{m+1}x))\phi(x)dx\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(u+h^{\prime})\phi(x)dx\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where we define
h^{\prime}=\varepsilon\mu_{2,1}
which is independent of
u
. Using inequality (
65
) together with the independence of
u
and
h^{\prime}
leads to

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(u+h^{\prime})\phi(x)dx\right]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\log\cosh{u}\right]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\tanh{(u)}\phi(x)dx\right]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[h^{\prime}\right]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[h^{\prime 2}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[h^{\prime}\right]=0
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[h^{\prime 2}\right]=\varepsilon^{4}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\int\log\cosh(u+h^{\prime})\phi(x)dx\right]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho}\left[\log\cosh{u}\right]-\varepsilon^{4}\kern 5.0pt.

(68)

Hence, plugging inequalities (
67
) and (
68
) in the expression of
KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})
leads us to

\displaystyle KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})=

\displaystyle 2p(A-B)\leq 2\left(2\varepsilon^{4}+(M-1)\varepsilon^{4}+2\varepsilon^{4}((M-1)^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+M)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(pM\varepsilon^{4}\left(1+M\varepsilon^{2}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

for some numerical constant
c
. Since
\varepsilon^{2}=\frac{4}{p}\bar{\Delta}^{2}
, for some numerical constant
c
that may differ, we have

KL(\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,\pi},\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\rho,id})\leq c\left(\bar{\Delta}^{4}\frac{M}{p}(1+\frac{M}{p}\bar{\Delta}^{2})\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The hypothesis
c_{1}\log(KM)\leq\bar{\Delta}^{2}\leq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{M}\log(KM)}
gives us

\bar{\Delta}^{4}\frac{M}{p}\leq c_{2}^{2}\log(Km)\kern 5.0pt,

and
